The method converts detector confidence and vision-language embeddings into probabilities of correctness, propagating these through the containment hierarchy to estimate room-level belief. It uses four signals at the logit scale, combines them in closed form without additional training, and handles correlated failures across objects sharing detectors and vocabulary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current 3DSGs record every entry as equally certain, making it impossible for robots to distinguish reliable from unreliable entries. Existing semantic uncertainty estimators require repeated sampling, training, or held-out labels—none of which are available to deployed systems at query time.

Method: The framework exploits detector confidence and embeddings already stored in the 3DSG. Four signals, each paired with the object-level error it indicates, are converted to probabilities at the logit scale learned by the vision-language model and combined in closed form. Objects sharing a detector and vocabulary fail together, so the framework aggregates them in the fully correlated limit rather than treating repeated errors as independent evidence.

Result: Evaluated on HM3DSem against a state-of-the-art 3DSG system, the framework improves object retrieval and lowers the error of room-level assertions of the graph it reads.

Conclusion: The proposed uncertainty estimation framework successfully enablesrobots to distinguish reliable from unreliable entries in 3DSGs without requiring additional training, sampling, or labeled data at query time. It effectively propagates uncertainty from object to room level while accounting for correlated failures.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary 3D Scene Graphs (3DSGs) ground each object node in a vision-language embedding, yet they record every entry as equally certain, so a robot querying the map cannot tell which of its entries are unreliable. Estimators of semantic uncertainty could supply that distinction, but they require repeated sampling of a model, training, or held-out labels, none of which are available to a deployed system at query time. We present a framework that exploits the detector confidence and the embeddings a 3DSG already stores, converts them into a probability that an entry is correct, and propagates that probability through the containment hierarchy into a belief that a room contains a queried class. Four signals, each paired with the object-level error it indicates, are converted to probabilities at the logit scale learned by the vision-language model and combined in closed form with no additional perception or training. Objects sharing a detector and a vocabulary fail together, so the framework aggregates them in the fully correlated limit, where an aggregation under independence would treat one repeated error as repeated evidence. Evaluated on HM3DSem against a state-of-the-art 3DSG system, the framework improves object retrieval and lowers the error of the room-level assertions of the graph it reads.

</details>


### [27] [MarsRecon: Self-Supervised and Multimodal Surface Representations for Mars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22379)
*Akshay Naik,Marius F. R. Juston,Jay Mahajan*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MarsRecon is a geospatially aware pipeline for learning visual and multimodal representations from HiRISE orbital imagery of Olympus Mons, using a masked autoencoder pretrained on unlabeled data and then aligned with text, coordinates, and local-global image context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution orbital imagery of Mars provides rich surface data, but sparse geological labels limit supervised representation learning, necessitating self-supervised approaches for Mars-specific pretraining.

Method: The pipeline calibrates NASA PDS products, extracts georeferenced patches, trains a masked autoencoder on unlabeled imagery, and aligns the frozen visual encoder with observation text, coordinates, and local-global image context.

Result: Stage A model achieved reconstruction loss reduction from 0.1751 to 0.1342 with increased resolution and invalid token filtering. The strongest local-primary model achieved image-to-text recall@10 of 0.3787, text-to-image recall@10 of 0.9161, and local-to-global recall@10 of 0.4350 on the held-out test split.

Conclusion: The pipeline establishes a working Mars-specific pretraining and retrieval framework, but further crop-overlap controls and downstream geological evaluations are needed to assess broader utility of embeddings.

Abstract: High-resolution orbital imagery offers a rich record of the Martian surface, but sparse geological labels limit supervised representation learning. We present MarsRecon, a geospatially aware pipeline for learning visual and multimodal representations from HiRISE observations of Olympus Mons. The pipeline calibrates NASA Planetary Data System products, extracts valid georeferenced patches, and trains a masked autoencoder on unlabeled imagery. Increasing input resolution and filtering invalid tokens reduced held-out reconstruction loss from 0.1751 to 0.1342 in the principal Stage A model series. We then freeze the visual encoder and align its features with observation text, coordinates, and local--global image context. The strongest current local-primary model achieves image-to-text recall@10 of 0.3787, text-to-image recall@10 of 0.9161, and local-to-global recall@10 of 0.4350 on the held-out test split. These results establish a working Mars-specific pretraining and retrieval pipeline; further crop-overlap controls and downstream geological evaluations are needed to assess the broader utility of its embeddings.

</details>


### [28] [Spatiotemporal Flux Probing for Single-Photon Videography](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22479)
*Jerry Yan,Matteo Forlivesi,Bowen Tan,Andrew Xie,Siddharth Somasundaram,Sotiris Nousias*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses the challenge of recovering high-speed videos from dynamic scenes under extreme photon sparsity (low-light conditions). The key insight is that motion and illumination information is encoded in correlations across the full space-time pattern of photon arrivals, rather than just local spatiotemporal windows. The authors develop a spatiotemporal flux probing theory and an algorithm that estimates Fourier coefficients of the underlying intensity directly from the photon stream.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods rely on local spatiotemporal window aggregation to improve signal-to-noise ratio, which discards global structure and fails in low-light regimes where photon detections are sparse in both space and time. The authors aim to overcome this fundamental limitation.

Method: The authors develop a spatiotemporal flux probing theory and an algorithm that estimates Fourier coefficients of the underlying intensity directly from the photon stream, leveraging correlations over the full space-time pattern of photon arrivals rather than local groupings.

Result: The approach (1) recovers fast motion and temporal illumination dynamics with substantially fewer photons than prior methods, (2) enables velocity-selective videography that automatically refocuses video onto specific detected motions, and (3) generalizes across sensing modalities including single-photon, event, and spike cameras.

Conclusion: The proposed spatiotemporal flux probing theory successfully recovers high-speed video from extreme photon sparsity by exploiting global correlations in photon arrival patterns, achieving superior performance with fewer photons and enabling novel applications like velocity-selective imaging across multiple camera modalities.

Abstract: We address the problem of recovering high-speed videos from dynamic scenes under extreme photon sparsity. Existing methods rely on aggregating photon detections in local spatiotemporal windows to improve signal-to-noise ratio; however, this local grouping discards global structure and fails in low-light regimes where photon detections are sparse in space and time. In this work, we show that the information needed to recover both motion and illumination is encoded in correlations over the full space-time pattern of photon arrivals. Building on this insight, we develop a spatiotemporal flux probing theory and an algorithm that estimates the Fourier coefficients of the underlying intensity directly from the photon stream. We demonstrate that our approach (1) recovers fast motion and temporal illumination dynamics with substantially fewer photons than prior methods, (2) enables velocity-selective videography that automatically refocuses video onto specific detected motions, and (3) generalizes across sensing modalities including single-photon, event, and spike cameras.

</details>


### [29] [Event-Frame Fusion for Inter-Frame Segmentation via Event-Guided Motion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22500)
*Dalia Hareb,Jean Martinet,Benoit Miramond,Elisabetta Chicca*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a hybrid vision architecture for autonomous navigation that combines conventional frame-based and event-based cameras using two Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs). A compact SNN (42k parameters) handles motion estimation, while a lightweight event-driven SNN (0.84M parameters) performs frame-based semantic segmentation with inter-frame interpolation. The system achieves segmentation rates up to 500 Hz with energy consumption below 1.87 mJ per inference, and can compensate for information loss in challenging conditions like motion blur or overexposure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing frame-based semantic segmentation for autonomous navigation suffers from motion blur, glare, latency, and low temporal resolution (20-30 FPS), leading to information loss between frames. While event cameras offer high temporal resolution, high dynamic range, and sparse outputs, event-based algorithms still lag frame-based ones in accuracy. This work aims to combine the strengths of both camera types to overcome their individual limitations.

Method: The proposed hybrid vision architecture integrates two complementary SNN components: (1) a compact SNN with 42k parameters for motion estimation, and (2) a lightweight event-driven SNN with 0.84M parameters for frame-based semantic segmentation. The system interpolates motion between frames to refine segmentation results, compensating for information loss in frames affected by blur or overexposure.

Result: The framework achieves segmentation rates of up to 500 Hz with energy consumption below 1.87 mJ per inference, while maintaining real-time GPU execution at frequencies up to 200 Hz. The approach enables more robust perception in challenging conditions by compensating for information loss in frames affected by blur or overexposure.

Conclusion: The hybrid SNN architecture successfully combines frame-based and event-based cameras for semantic segmentation, achieving high temporal resolution (500 Hz) with low energy consumption (<1.87 mJ per inference). This approach provides a promising solution for robust autonomous navigation in challenging environments where traditional frame-based cameras fail.

Abstract: Autonomous navigation requires precise and efficient semantic segmentation, yet existing frame-based approaches remain limited by motion blur, glare, latency, and the low temporal resolution (20-30 FPS) of conventional cameras, which leads to information loss between frames. Event cameras have emerged as an alternative sensing modality, capturing intensity changes asynchronously with high temporal resolution, high dynamic range, and sparse outputs. However, event-based algorithms still fall short of frame-based ones in accuracy, as most segmentation methods are designed for dense frame data. To overcome these limitations, we propose a hybrid vision architecture that combines conventional frame-based and event-based cameras. The system integrates two complementary components: (1) a compact Spiking Neural Network (SNN) with 42k parameters for motion estimation, and (2) a lightweight event-driven SNN with 0.84M parameters for frame-based semantic segmentation, which interpolates motion between frames to refine segmentation results. By predicting inter-frame segmentations, the framework achieves segmentation rates of up to 500 Hz with an energy consumption below 1.87 mJ per inference, while maintaining real-time GPU execution at frequencies up to 200 Hz. Additionally, our approach compensates for information loss in frames affected by blur or overexposure, enabling more robust perception in challenging conditions.

</details>


### [30] [Rethinking Vision Architectures with Gated Linear Attention and KAN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22506)
*Ali Mehizel,Oussama Khaldi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LKAT (Linear Kolmogorov-Arnold Transformer) is an isotropic ViT encoder that combines chunkwise Gated Linear Attention (GLA) with a two-layer KAN feed-forward network, featuring an I/O-aware fused RBF-KAN kernel. It outperforms ViT, ViT-5, and MLP-Mixer on ImageNet-100, with consistent scaling across Tiny/Small/Base variants on CIFAR-10/100.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision Transformers allocate most parameters to MLPs for channel mixing while using quadratic multi-head self-attention (MHSA) for token interactions. Linear attention reduces complexity to O(N) but remains coupled with fixed-activation MLP. Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) use learnable univariate maps but prior vision KANs keep MHSA or omit attention entirely. This work aims to combine the benefits of linear attention and KAN-based feed-forward networks.

Method: LKAT couples chunkwise Gated Linear Attention (GLA) with a two-layer KAN feed-forward network, providing an I/O-aware fused RBF-KAN kernel for radial-basis grid maps. Under a shared DeiT-style recipe, LKAT was compared with ViT, ViT-5, and MLP-Mixer on ImageNet-100, and Tiny/Small/Base variants were tested on CIFAR-10/100 and ImageNet-100 pretraining transferred to CIFAR fine-tuning.

Result: LKAT-B exceeds ViT-B/16, ViT-5-B, and Mixer-B/16 on ImageNet-100. Tiny/Small/Base LKAT variants scale consistently on CIFAR-10/100, and ImageNet-100 pretraining transfers to CIFAR fine-tuning.

Conclusion: Gated linear attention and KAN-based radial-basis functions serve as complementary inductive biases for mid-scale visual representation learning, offering a promising alternative architecture for vision transformers.

Abstract: Vision Transformers allocate most parameters to multi-layer perceptrons (MLPs) for channel mixing, while token interactions usually rely on quadratic multi-head self-attention (MHSA). Linear attention reduces sequence complexity to O(N), but remains coupled with the same fixed-activation MLP as softmax Transformers. Kolmogorov-Arnold Networks (KANs) instead place learnable univariate maps on edges, yet prior vision KANs keep MHSA or omit attention entirely. We introduce LKAT (Linear Kolmogorov-Arnold Transformer), an isotropic ViT encoder that couples chunkwise Gated Linear Attention (GLA) with a two-layer KAN feed-forward, and we provide an I/O-aware fused RBF-KAN kernel for the radial-basis grid maps. Under a shared DeiT-style recipe we compare LKAT with ViT, ViT-5, and MLP-Mixer. LKAT-B exceeds ViT-B/16, ViT-5-B, and Mixer-B/16 on ImageNet-100. Tiny/Small/Base LKAT variants scale consistently on CIFAR-10/100, and ImageNet-100 pretraining transfers to CIFAR fine-tuning. The results support gated linear attention and KAN-based radial-basis functions as complementary inductive biases for mid-scale visual representation learning.

</details>


### [31] [AdaMerge: Tuning-Free Patch Compression for Multi-Vector Visual Document Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22562)
*Jianxin You,Kun Ni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes AdaMerge, a plug-and-play compression method for multi-vector visual document retrieval models that automatically detects each document's optimal merge boundary via gap analysis on the merge-cosine trajectory, avoiding per-dataset hyperparameter tuning while outperforming or matching state-of-the-art methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-vector visual document retrieval (VDR) models like ColPali and ColNomic achieve strong accuracy but at substantial storage and latency cost due to representing each document with hundreds to thousands of patch-level embeddings. Existing compression methods (pruning or merging) either remove unimportant patches or merge similar ones into clusters. The state-of-the-art method Prune-then-Merge (PtM) requires per-dataset tuning of cluster budget m via grid search, which is inconvenient.

Method: The authors observed that the merge-cosine sequence from hierarchical clustering exhibits a sharp "cliff" separating mergeable redundancy from salient signal, and this cliff location is concentrated in a narrow band across 14 datasets. Based on this, they propose AdaMerge with two components: (i) detects each document's cliff via gap analysis on the merge-cosine trajectory, and (ii) builds attention-weighted cluster centroids to preserve salient signal.

Result: On the long-document benchmark ViDoRe-V2 (4 datasets, two backbones), AdaMerge significantly outperforms tuned PtM across the operating range (p < 10^-4). On the short-document benchmark ViDoRe-V1 (10 datasets, two backbones), where all merging methods are already near-lossless, AdaMerge matches tuned PtM without any per-dataset tuning.

Conclusion: AdaMerge adds only about 10 ms per document and exposes a single global hyperparameter shared across all datasets and backbones, making it a practical and effective solution for compressing VDR models across diverse document retrieval tasks.

Abstract: Multi-vector visual document retrieval (VDR) models such as ColPali and ColNomic achieve strong accuracy by representing each document with hundreds to thousands of patch-level embeddings, at substantial storage and latency cost. Existing compression methods either prune unimportant patches or merge similar ones into clusters; the recent state-of-the-art merging method Prune-then-Merge (PtM) consistently outperforms pruning-only baselines at high compression, but requires a per-dataset cluster budget m to be tuned by grid search. We observe that the merge-cosine sequence produced by hierarchical clustering exhibits a sharp cliff separating mergeable redundancy from salient signal, and that the location of this cliff is concentrated in a narrow band across more than 11,000 documents from 14 datasets. This suggests the merge boundary can be detected per document rather than tuned per dataset. Building on this observation, we propose AdaMerge, a plug-and-play compression method that (i) detects each document's own cliff via gap analysis on the merge-cosine trajectory, and (ii) builds attention-weighted cluster centroids to preserve salient signal. On the long-document benchmark ViDoRe-V2 (4 datasets, two backbones), AdaMerge significantly outperforms tuned PtM across the operating range (p < 10^-4); on the short-document benchmark ViDoRe-V1 (10 datasets, two backbones), where all merging methods are already near-lossless, AdaMerge matches tuned PtM without any per-dataset tuning. AdaMerge adds only about 10 ms per document and exposes a single global hyperparameter shared across all datasets and backbones.

</details>


### [32] [Beyond the Leaderboard: Counterfactual Diagnosis of End-to-End and VLA Driving Policies Under Domain Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22582)
*Ruolin Yang,Zilin Huang,Buoyue Wang,Zhengyang Wan,Yuhao Luo,Zihao Sheng,Sikai Chen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper proposes a "counterfactual check-up" method to evaluate end-to-end and vision-language-action (VLA) driving policies by editing real frames (pedestrian removal or night-style re-lighting) and reading trajectory changes as diagnostics rather than scores. Current leaderboard rankings don't predict policy behavior at new sites. The method reveals five diagnostic axes: planned driving distance, safety response to pedestrians, danger scaling, plan stability under irrelevant changes, and sensitivity to lighting changes. Results show only 1.9% genuine avoidance in near-pedestrian scenarios, with minimal median clearance (≤0.03m) and planned distance (≤0.08m) changes. Some metrics transfer across left/right-hand drive sites while others don't.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation methods (leaderboard rank, nuScenes open-loop error, NAVSIM leaderboard) report outcomes but not the underlying behavior, so they fail to predict how a policy will perform at a new site. The authors observed that rank on existing benchmarks does not carry over to scenes with pedestrians at new sites.

Method: The authors propose a counterfactual check-up: using a few hundred real frames, each edited two ways (pedestrian removed or re-lit by night-style perturbation), verifying edits with an independent detector, and reading the change in planned trajectory as a diagnosis. From these edits, two causal axes are derived, and five diagnostic exams are built to separate what scores merge.

Result: On 246 NAVSIM near-pedestrian scenes, only 1.9% of responses show genuine avoidance when the pedestrian lies on the planned path. Under the open-loop protocol, median clearance change is at most 0.03m and median planned distance change is at most 0.08m for every policy. In a pre-registered left- to right-hand drive test, exposure/specificity orderings, lighting verdict, and collision outcome transfer, while point values and hazard-sensitivity verdict do not.

Conclusion: The counterfactual check-up profiles reveal which policy is safe because it plans short, which covers human-like distance without yielding, and which is unsteady under irrelevant changes. Most verdicts settle within a few dozen frames, but hazard sensitivity requires hundreds. This method provides a more useful selection report than scores for choosing policies for new sites.

Abstract: End-to-end and vision-language-action (VLA) driving policies are compared by leaderboard rank, but a rank reports an outcome, not the behaviour behind it, so it predicts poorly how a policy will behave at a new site. On six released policies, rank on nuScenes open-loop error or on NAVSIM's leaderboard does not carry over to scenes with a pedestrian near the ego corridor at a new site. We propose a counterfactual check-up: a few hundred real frames, each edited two ways (pedestrian removed, or re-lit by a night-style perturbation), every edit verified by an independent detector, and the change in the planned trajectory read as a diagnosis rather than a score. From these edits two causal axes are read, and five exams built on them separate what a score merges: how far the policy plans to drive, whether seeing the pedestrian buys safety, whether that response scales with danger, whether the plan moves when nothing requires it, and how much an irrelevant lighting change moves it. On 246 NAVSIM near-pedestrian scenes, in the cells where the pedestrian lies on the planned path only 1.9% of responses are genuine avoidance, and under our open-loop protocol the median clearance change is at most 0.03 m and the median change in planned distance at most 0.08 m for every policy. In a pre-registered test from left- to right-hand drive, the exposure and specificity orderings, the lighting verdict and the collision outcome transfer, while point values and the hazard-sensitivity verdict do not. Read as a selection report, the profiles say which policy is safe because it plans short, which covers a human-like distance without yielding, and which is unsteady under a change that requires no reaction, and they price each verdict: most settle within a few dozen frames, hazard sensitivity needs hundreds. Code and edited frames will be released.

</details>


### [33] [Seeing is not Enough: Vision-Language Models Perceive Evidence but Fail to Act](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22588)
*Yuyang Dai,Bofei Huang,Hongbo Zhang,Haoran Xie*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical gap in vision-language models (VLMs): they often correctly identify visual evidence but fail to use it appropriately when making decisions. The authors distinguish between perceptual failure (evidence not recognized) and process failure (evidence recognized but not properly constrained). They introduce VPAC-Bench, a benchmark with 9 real-image process families annotated with activity stages and transitions, and propose State-Relevance-Target (SRT) interventions that require models to connect visual evidence to process states before answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language models perform well on benchmarks but often make decisions that contradict visual evidence they have correctly identified. This reveals a fundamental limitation in how VLMs leverage recognized evidence for decision-making. The motivation is to understand and address this disconnect between visual recognition and decision-making.

Method: The authors introduce VPAC-Bench, a benchmark spanning nine real-image process families (assembly, physical state transitions, navigation, traffic, object-use affordance), with each image annotated by current activity stage and nearby stage transition. They propose State-Relevance-Target (SRT), a family of structured process-prior interventions requiring models to connect visible evidence to relevant process states before answering. The approach includes boundary-aligned SRT that aligns with scene-specific decision boundaries.

Result: Process failure is widespread: models correctly enumerating visual candidates still over-commit to single answers in >95% of ambiguous cases. SRT intervention reduces this to <13% without degrading performance on unambiguous cases. However, transfer is model-dependent, and generic SRT does not consistently outperform strong chain-of-thought baselines. When the relevant stage transition is known, boundary-aligned SRT substantially outperforms generic process prompting and all tested chain-of-thought baselines across assembly, physical state transition, navigation/traffic, and object-use affordance tasks.

Conclusion: Process priors are most useful when aligned with the scene's specific decision boundary. This motivates boundary-aware prior selection for process-grounded visual reasoning. The key insight is that understanding where decision boundaries occur in process space enables more effective intervention strategies.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) perform strongly on visual question answering benchmarks, yet often make decisions that contradict visual evidence they have already identified correctly. We distinguish perceptual failure, where relevant evidence is not recognized, from process failure, where recognized evidence fails to constrain the final decision. We introduce VPAC-Bench, a benchmark spanning nine real-image process families, with each image annotated by its current activity stage and nearby stage transition. We also propose State-Relevance-Target (SRT), a family of structured process-prior interventions that requires models to connect visible evidence to the relevant process state before answering. Across multiple VLMs, process failure is widespread: models that correctly enumerate visual candidates still over-commit to a single answer in more than 95% of ambiguous cases. An explicit process-structured intervention reduces this rate to below 13% without degrading performance on unambiguous cases. However, the transfer of process priors is model-dependent, and generic SRT does not consistently outperform strong chain-of-thought baselines. When the relevant stage transition is known, boundary-aligned SRT substantially outperforms generic process prompting and all tested chain-of-thought baselines across assembly, physical state transition, navigation and traffic, and object-use affordance tasks. These results show that process priors are most useful when aligned with the scene's specific decision boundary, motivating boundary-aware prior selection for process-grounded visual reasoning.

</details>


### [34] [X-Beat: An Explainable Framework for ECG Image Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22631)
*Mohammad Sadman Tahsin,Haitham Y. Adarbah,Afzel Noore*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: X-Beat is an explainable and reliability-aware benchmark framework for ECG image classification, evaluating four cardiac classes (Abnormal Heartbeat, History of Myocardial Infarction, Myocardial Infarction, Normal Heartbeat) using transfer learning with multiple CNN backbones (EfficientNet-B0, ResNet-50, DenseNet-121, MobileNetV3-Large), combined with Grad-CAM explanations and systematic reliability evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the clinical deployment barrier of deep learning models for ECG interpretation due to limited transparency and lack of reliability validation, enabling trustworthy AI systems in healthcare.

Method: Transfer learning with ImageNet-pretrained CNN backbones under unified training protocol; Grad-CAM-based visual explanations; explanation stability analysis; regional sensitivity analysis; confidence-based reliability assessment.

Result: ResNet-50 achieves best performance with 91.94% accuracy, macro F1-score of 0.9098, and AUC up to 0.995. Model focuses on waveform-relevant regions; most incorrect predictions occur with lower confidence.

Conclusion: X-Beat provides a structured and reproducible benchmark for evaluating both predictive performance and explanation reliability in ECG image classification, contributing to trustworthy and interpretable AI for clinical decision support.

Abstract: Accurate automated interpretation of electrocardio- grams (ECGs) is essential for early detection of cardiac condi- tions such as myocardial infarction and rhythm abnormalities. However, many high-performing deep learning models remain difficult to deploy in clinical settings due to limited transparency and lack of reliability validation. In this work, we present X- Beat, an explainable and reliability-aware benchmark framework for ECG image classification designed to support trustworthy AI systems in healthcare. The proposed framework combines transfer learning with post-hoc explainability and systematic reliability evaluation across four cardiac classes: Abnormal Heartbeat, History of Myocardial Infarction, Myocardial In- farction, and Normal Heartbeat. Multiple ImageNet-pretrained CNN backbones, including EfficientNet-B0, ResNet-50, DenseNet- 121, and MobileNetV3-Large, are evaluated under a unified training protocol. Beyond standard performance metrics, we incorporate Grad-CAM-based visual explanations together with additional analyses, including explanation stability, regional sen- sitivity, and confidence-based reliability assessment, to examine whether model predictions are supported by clinically meaningful evidence. Experimental results show that ResNet-50 achieves the best performance, reaching 91.94% accuracy and a macro F1- score of 0.9098, with strong class separability (AUC up to 0.995). Explanation analyses indicate that the model primarily focuses on waveform-relevant regions, while reliability evaluation suggests that most incorrect predictions occur with lower confidence. Overall, this work provides a structured and reproducible bench- mark for evaluating both predictive performance and explanation reliability in ECG image classification, contributing toward the development of trustworthy and interpretable AI components for clinical decision support systems.

</details>


### [35] [ConsistWorld: Evidence Routing for Consistent Multi-Agent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22641)
*Qianxun Xu,Xianfang Zeng,Xinyao Liao,Wei Cheng,Gang Yu,Chi Zhang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ConsistWorld is a multi-agent world model that generates consistent camera-controlled video streams from a single shared image for multiple independently controlled observers. It uses pose-conditioned memory retrieval to recover historical evidence beyond the context window and visibility-gated peer sharing to coordinate information across agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending autoregressive video world models from single observer to multiple agents faces challenges in maintaining consistency across independently controlled views and temporal gaps under causal streaming. Existing approaches lack mechanisms for cross-agent and cross-time coherence.

Method: The method formulates consistency as routing evidence from committed multi-agent history and concurrently generated peer views. Two key mechanisms: (1) Pose Conditioned Memory Retrieval selects relevant historical observations from all agents using camera geometry; (2) Visibility-Gated Peer Sharing regulates current peer information based on estimated historical coverage and current-view overlap. Both maintain bounded active context for fixed agent count and retrieval budget.

Result: Experiments on evidence sharing cases and generalization tests show ConsistWorld achieves strong cross-time and cross-agent consistency while preserving competitive generation quality.

Conclusion: ConsistWorld successfully enables multi-agent video world models with consistent generation across independently controlled camera views and temporal gaps, with applications in coordinated exploration and multi-view video generation.

Abstract: Autoregressive video world models enable temporally coherent generation for a single observer. Extending them to multiple agents requires consistency across independently controlled views and temporal gaps under causal streaming. We present ConsistWorld, a multi-agent world model that generates camera-controlled video streams of a static scene from one shared image. We formulate consistency as routing evidence from committed multi-agent history and concurrently generated peer views to the tokens being generated. Pose Conditioned Memory Retrieval selects relevant historical observations from all agents, recovering evidence beyond the recent context window. Visibility-Gated Peer Sharing regulates current peer information according to estimated historical coverage and current-view overlap. Together, they determine which historical observations enter the context and where concurrent peer information contributes, supporting long-term recall and coordinated exploration. Both mechanisms use camera geometry and maintain a bounded active context for a fixed agent count and retrieval budget. Experiments on evidence sharing cases and video length and agent number generalizations show that ConsistWorld achieves a strong cross-time and cross-agent consistency while preserving competitive generation quality.

</details>


### [36] [Math2Visual-X: A Modular Framework for Pedagogically Aligned Lower-Primary Math Visuals Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22647)
*H. D. E. Maduranga,S. K. Munasinghe,K. P. T. I. Weerasekara,Surangika Ranathunga,Nisansa de Silva*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper presents Math2Visual-X, a symbolic visual generation framework for creating classroom-usable visuals for lower-primary Math Word Problems. The framework combines an LLM-based routing layer with three worksheet-oriented generation modules and two fallback mechanisms for SVG asset acquisition, outperforming Stable Diffusion XL, Nano Banana, and GPT Image in human evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual representations can help lower-primary learners understand Math Word Problems, but generating appropriate classroom visuals remains challenging. Existing symbolic systems offer controllability but have limited coverage, while end-to-end text-to-image systems often fail to satisfy exact mathematical constraints required for educational content.

Method: The proposed framework includes: (1) an LLM-based routing layer to direct problems to appropriate generators, (2) three worksheet-oriented generation modules for broader problem coverage and scalable asset generation, and (3) two fallback mechanisms for open-world SVG asset acquisition. The system was evaluated through human comparison against Stable Diffusion XL, Nano Banana, and GPT Image.

Result: Math2Visual-X achieved the strongest overall performance in human evaluation, demonstrating superior capability in generating pedagogically appropriate and mathematically accurate visuals for lower-primary math word problems.

Conclusion: The symbolic visual generation framework offers a scalable and pedagogically grounded approach for automatic MWP visual generation, successfully addressing the limitations of both symbolic systems (limited coverage) and text-to-image systems (mathematical constraint satisfaction).

Abstract: Visual representations can help lower-primary learners understand Math Word Problems, but generating classroom-usable visuals remains difficult. Existing symbolic systems are controllable but limited in coverage, while end-to-end text-to-image systems often fail to satisfy exact mathematical constraints. This paper presents a symbolic visual generation framework for lower-primary MWP generation with broader problem coverage and more scalable asset generation. The framework includes an LLM-based routing layer, three worksheet-oriented generation modules, and two fallback mechanisms for open-world SVG asset acquisition. A human evaluation comparing Math2Visual-X with Stable Diffusion XL, Nano Banana, and GPT Image showed that the proposed method achieved the strongest overall performance. The results indicate that the framework offers a scalable and pedagogically grounded approach for automatic MWP visual generation.

</details>


### [37] [PanoSeg3R: Feed-Forward 3D Semantic Segmentation for Panoramic Images with an Automatic Data Curation Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22687)
*Heechan Yoon,Dongki Jung,Phuc Nguyen,Ming Lin,Dinesh Manocha*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PanoSeg3R is a feed-forward framework for 3D panoramic semantic segmentation that jointly predicts 3D geometry and multi-view semantic segmentation in a single forward pass, using a pretrained reconstruction backbone with a query-based mask decoder and an automatic panorama data curation pipeline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for 3D semantic segmentation are designed for perspective inputs, not panoramic images. This limits their applicability to panoramic 3D reconstruction tasks. The authors aimed to address this gap by developing a framework specifically designed for panoramic semantic segmentation.

Method: PanoSeg3R extends feed-forward 3D reconstruction with a query-based mask decoder built upon a pretrained reconstruction backbone that supports panoramic images. The method also introduces an automatic panorama data curation pipeline that leverages off-the-shelf foundation models to generate pseudo semantic annotations, expanding training data and improving zero-shot generalization.

Result: PanoSeg3R achieves state-of-the-art performance on panoramic 3D semantic segmentation, improving 3D mIoU by up to 16.02 on ScanNet++. The curated training data further improves zero-shot performance by up to 4.26 mIoU on Stanford2D3D and 43.28 mIoU on ToF-360.

Conclusion: PanoSeg3R successfully enables 3D panoramic semantic segmentation in a single forward pass, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods. The automatic data curation pipeline effectively expands training data and enhances zero-shot generalization capabilities.

Abstract: We present PanoSeg3R, a feed-forward framework for 3D panoramic semantic segmentation. Unlike existing methods designed for perspective inputs, PanoSeg3R jointly predicts 3D geometry and multi-view semantic segmentation in one single forward pass. Built upon a pretrained reconstruction backbone that supports panoramic images, our approach extends feed-forward 3D reconstruction with a query-based mask decoder. Furthermore, we introduce an automatic panorama data curation pipeline that leverages the complementary strengths of off-the-shelf foundation models to generate reliable pseudo semantic annotations, substantially expanding the training data and improving zero-shot generalization. PanoSeg3R achieves state-of-the-art performance on panoramic 3D semantic segmentation, improving 3D mIoU by up to 16.02 on ScanNet++, while the curated training data further improves zero-shot performance by up to 4.26 and 43.28 mIoU on Stanford2D3D and ToF-360, respectively. Website: https://harryyoon777.github.io/PanoSeg3R/

</details>


### [38] [DFD-Lab: A Modular Audio-Visual Deepfake Detection Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23830)
*Jan Rybarczyk,Mateusz Roszkowski,Jacek Komorowski*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DFD-Lab is a modular pipeline for comparing audio-visual deepfake detectors, integrating three detector implementations and supporting configurable experimental workflows. The study demonstrates that JPEG50 training augmentation improves AUROC (0.691, 0.605, 0.570) but decreases accuracy, highlighting challenges in cross-dataset detection and the importance of separating training interventions, evaluation corruptions, and metric-dependent outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Comparing audio-visual deepfake detectors requires coordinating multiple complex factors (dataset adaptation, temporal representation, model interfaces, experimental conditions), but existing approaches lack standardized modular frameworks for fair comparison.

Method: DFD-Lab pipeline integrating three implementations: Xception-based maximum-logit fusion, ResNet with temporal LSTM fusion, and AVFF reimplementation. Experiments cover external testing, degradation-based training augmentation (JPEG50), and evaluation-time corruption on FakeAVCeleb and Deepfake-Eval-2024 datasets.

Result: Baseline AUROC values of 0.504, 0.538, 0.458 on Deepfake-Eval-2024 (trained on FakeAVCeleb). JPEG50 training augmentation raises AUROC to 0.691, 0.605, 0.570 respectively, but decreases accuracy across all three models.

Conclusion: Cross-dataset deepfake detection remains challenging. Training interventions, evaluation corruptions, and metric-dependent outcomes should remain distinct within a common pipeline. Ranking (AUROC) and classification metrics provide complementary information.

Abstract: Comparing audio-visual deepfake detectors requires coordinating dataset adaptation, temporal input representation, model interfaces and experimental conditions. We present DFD-Lab, a modular pipeline that separates these responsibilities while supporting shared training and evaluation workflows. We integrate three implementations: Xception-based maximum-logit fusion, ResNet with temporal LSTM fusion, and our AVFF reimplementation. Experiments cover external testing, degradation-based training augmentation and evaluation-time corruption. On a filtered subset of Deepfake-Eval-2024, models trained on FakeAVCeleb attain baseline AUROC values of 0.504, 0.538 and 0.458. JPEG50 training augmentation raises these to 0.691, 0.605 and 0.570, respectively, while all three accuracies decrease. These results illustrate why training interventions, evaluation corruptions and metric-dependent outcomes should remain distinct within a common pipeline. The contribution is the integration of audio-visual processing, interchangeable detectors and configurable experimental workflows, supported by empirical case studies. The findings highlight the challenge of cross-dataset detection and the complementary information provided by ranking and classification metrics.

</details>


### [39] [Vision2CAD: A Visual Agent Harness for Explicit Geometry Referencing and Localization in Parametric CAD Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22688)
*Xi Cheng,Chenxi Zhai,Hang Cheng,Mingyu Fan,Pingfa Feng,Long Zeng*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Vision2CAD is a visual agent system that combines vision-language models with deterministic CAD kernel operations to generate parametric CAD models. It addresses key challenges in selecting geometric references, interpreting sketch-plane coordinates, and establishing sketch constraints to external geometry through three main mechanisms: ID-based interface, local-coordinate bridge, and projected-edge localization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing methods for generating parametric CAD models face challenges in three areas: 1) selecting geometric references accurately, 2) interpreting sketch-plane local coordinates from view coordinates, and 3) establishing sketch constraints to projected external geometry. These limitations hinder the ability to create accurate geometry with stable feature dependencies.

Method: Vision2CAD combines vision-language model (VLM) reasoning with deterministic CAD kernel operations through three key mechanisms: (1) ID-based interface for explicit geometry selection, (2) local-coordinate bridge to convert view coordinates into sketch coordinates, and (3) projected-edge localization to support external sketch constraints. The system also introduces the Geometry Explicit Reference Dataset (GERD) which aligns commands, geometry states and IDs at every modeling step.

Result: On GERD-EVL and a DeepCAD test subset, Vision2CAD improves mIoU by 11.1% and 5.6% and reduces Chamfer distance by 17.3% and 41.8%, respectively. Parameter-editing experiments and ablation studies further proved the preservation of parametric dependencies.

Conclusion: Vision2CAD successfully establishes feature dependencies within supported modeling operations and constraint types. The combination of VLM reasoning with deterministic CAD operations enables accurate geometry generation while preserving parametric relationships, as validated by quantitative improvements in mIoU and Chamfer distance metrics.

Abstract: Generating parametric CAD models requires accurate geometry and stable feature dependencies. Existing methods face challenges in selecting geometric references, interpreting sketch-plane local coordinates, and establishing sketch constraints to projected external geometry. We present Vision2CAD, a visual agent harness that combines vision-language model (VLM) reasoning with deterministic CAD kernel operations. An ID-based interface supports explicit geometry selection, a local-coordinate bridge converts view coordinates into sketch coordinates, and projected-edge localization supports external sketch constraints. These mechanisms establish feature dependencies within the supported modeling operations and constraint types. We also introduce the Geometry Explicit Reference Dataset (GERD), which aligned commands, geometry states and IDs at every modeling step. On GERD-EVL and a DeepCAD test subset, Vision2CAD improves mIoU by 11.1\% and 5.6\% and reduces Chamfer distance by 17.3\% and 41.8\%, respectively. Parameter-editing experiments and ablation studies further proved the preservation of parametric dependencies.

</details>


### [40] [DOA-SORT: Directional Occlusion-Aware Multi-Object Tracking with Distributional Observations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22706)
*Hao Wang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DOA-SORT is a training-free online multi-object tracker that explicitly models directional occlusion biases (left, right, top, bottom) to improve identity association. It uses a mixture model with clean and directional occlusion components, adapting observation noise based on predicted occlusion and detection confidence. Achieves significant improvements in HOTA (+3.34), AssA (+4.47), and IDF1 (+3.09) on DanceTrack over OA-SORT.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing motion-dominant trackers treat occlusion as a scalar penalty, which fails to capture directional observation bias. Left, right, top, and bottom occlusions distort detection location and shape differently, causing identity errors in multi-object tracking.

Method: DOA-SORT models directional occlusion biases through: (1) inferring soft front-back ordering from box overlap and relative bottom positions; (2) estimating directional occlusion coverage and depth; (3) constructing a mixture model with one clean and four directional occlusion components; (4) using five-dimensional observations (box center, area, confidence, aspect ratio); (5) adapting observation noise to predicted occlusion and detection confidence. Applied to high-confidence association, low-confidence association, and track recovery with ambiguity penalties and local order-consistency swaps.

Result: On DanceTrack validation split, DOA-SORT improves HOTA from 63.00 to 66.34 (+3.34), AssA from 45.10 to 49.57 (+4.47), and IDF1 from 62.19 to 65.28 (+3.09) over OA-SORT with the same detector. Gains are concentrated in association quality while detection accuracy remains stable. Cross-dataset evaluation on MOT17 and MOT20 train splits shows generalization.

Conclusion: DOA-SORT significantly improves MOT performance by explicitly modeling directional occlusion biases. The method achieves substantial gains in association quality metrics (HOTA, AssA, IDF1) on DanceTrack while maintaining stable detection accuracy. The approach is online, training-free, and shows cross-dataset generalization on MOT17 and MOT20.

Abstract: Identity association in multi-object tracking (MOT) is vulnerable to partial occlusion, truncated detections, and fluctuating confidence scores. Existing motion-dominant trackers commonly represent occlusion as a scalar penalty. This treatment misses the directional observation bias caused by occlusion: left, right, top, and bottom occlusions distort the location and shape of a detection in different ways. We propose \ours{} (Directional Occlusion-Aware SORT), an online and training-free tracker that models these biases explicitly. First, it infers a soft front--back ordering from box overlap and relative bottom positions, and estimates directional occlusion coverage and depth. It then constructs a mixture of one clean and four directional occlusion observation components. The model uses a five-dimensional observation comprising box center, area, confidence, and aspect ratio, and adapts observation noise to predicted occlusion and detection confidence. The directional mixture likelihood is used in high-confidence association, low-confidence association, and track recovery; ambiguity penalties and local order-consistency swaps further reduce identity errors among nearby objects. On the DanceTrack validation split, \ours{} improves HOTA from 63.00 to 66.34, AssA from 45.10 to 49.57, and IDF1 from 62.19 to 65.28 over OA-SORT with the same detector and evaluation protocol. The gains are concentrated in association quality while detection accuracy remains stable. Additional local evaluations on MOT17 and MOT20 train splits characterize cross-dataset behavior under the same no-ReID tracking protocol.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [41] [Common Cause, Not Cross-Attention: Blocking Visual Shortcuts in Audio-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22361)
*Jian Xu,Delu Zeng,John Paisley,Qibin Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper investigates a failure mode in joint audio-video generators where models learn to predict sound from visual appearance (a shortcut) rather than from the causal event, because training data exhibits spurious correlations between appearance and sound. Even common remedies like shared latent spaces fail. The solution requires counterfactual invariance through intervention on nuisance variables.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Joint audio-video generators are trained on data where visual appearance and sound are strongly correlated, often spuriously. This creates a failure mode where models learn visual shortcuts instead of causal relationships. The paper aims to understand and fix this fundamental problem.

Method: The authors build an Audio-Video (AV) structural causal model (SCM) where audio is independent of video's nuisance appearance by construction. They prove that counterfactual invariance is necessary and sufficient to identify the causal predictor under stated SCM and intervention assumptions. They test this across: (1) feature-vector SCM, (2) procedural pixel video, (3) real images with spectrogram audio and pretrained backbone, (4) moving real digits, and (5) conditional generator. They also perform an input-intervention test on a real pretrained video-to-audio generator.

Result: Models using cross-attention or shared latent to read video directly learn visual shortcuts: they predict sound from appearance rather than causal events and fail when appearance-event correlation is broken. Common remedies (bottleneck, unsupervised shared/private factorization, faithful shared-prior model) all fail—they grab the appearance proxy. Only intervention on the nuisance fixes the shortcut. On a real pretrained generator, recolouring or graying a video substantially changes the generated sound, demonstrating lack of invariance.

Conclusion: Counterfactual invariance is necessary and sufficient for identifying the causal predictor when blocking shortcuts. The popular approach of routing modalities through a shared common-cause latent does not fix the problem. Blocking the shortcut requires intervention on the nuisance variable. Even pretrained models exhibit this failure, synthesizing wrong event sounds when appearance is manipulated.

Abstract: Joint audio--video generators are trained on data in which what an event looks like and what it sounds like are strongly, often spuriously, correlated: a particular material, texture, or object appearance co-occurs with a particular sound. This paper is a controlled causal study of the resulting failure mode. Building an AV structural causal model in which the audio is, by construction, independent of the video's nuisance appearance, we show that models which let audio read video directly-through cross-attention or a shared latent-learn a visual shortcut: they predict sound from appearance rather than from the causal event, and collapse when the appearance-event correlation is broken at test time, literally synthesizing the wrong event's sound. Crucially, the popular remedy of routing both modalities through a shared common-cause latent does not fix this: a bottleneck, an unsupervised shared/private factorization, and a faithful shared-prior model all grab the appearance proxy and fail like the direct model. Blocking the shortcut instead requires an intervention on the nuisance. Under the stated SCM and intervention assumptions we prove that counterfactual invariance is necessary and sufficient to identify the causal predictor, and we verify the mechanism across a feature-vector SCM, procedural pixel video, real images with spectrogram audio and a pretrained backbone, moving real digits, and a conditional generator. On a \emph{real, pretrained} video-to-audio generator, an input-intervention test shows the model is far from invariant to sound-irrelevant edits (recolouring or graying a video substantially changes the sound it generates).

</details>


### [42] [PRQuant: Permutation Residual Quantization for Low-Overhead Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22106)
*Peiran Wang,Anqi Wang,Jiaying Zhao,Huiwen Yang,Zhenyu Ming,Rongqian Wang,Yiwu Yao,Kun Tian,Xin Yao,Gong Zhang,Fan Yang,Zhongyi Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PRQuant is a training-free quantization framework that combines channel permutation with static residual compensation to address outliers in low-bit quantization of linear layers, achieving hardware-friendly contiguous layouts that eliminate dynamic gathering overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Low-bit quantization accuracy is dominated by a small number of outliers. Existing methods (smoothing, rotation, residual-based) mitigate this but introduce new accuracy bottlenecks to weights and heavy execution overheads due to online operations.

Method: PRQuant uses AWQ-style scaling, then identifies input channels most contributing to weight quantization error, permutes them into contiguous tail blocks, and constructs residual weight sub-tensors offline. This enables activation side to use tail blocks seamlessly without expensive online gathering, turning scattered residual compensation into regular tail-augmented GEMM.

Result: PRQuant effectively reduces down-projection reconstruction error. Ablation shows smoothing and residual compensation are primary numerical drivers; permutation provides marginal numerical benefit but enables hardware-friendly contiguous layout eliminating dynamic gathering overhead. Outperforms default MXFP4 and PTQ baselines, improving by 1.24 on Qwen3-4B and 0.55 on Qwen3-30B-A3B.

Conclusion: PRQuant successfully addresses outlier-dominated quantization error with low overhead. The combination of channel reorganization and static residual compensation achieves better accuracy while maintaining hardware efficiency through contiguous memory layout, making it superior to existing PTQ approaches for LLMs.

Abstract: Accuracy of Low-bit quantization of linear layers is often dominated by a small number of outliers. Although existing methods, such as smoothing, rotation, or residual-based approaches, may mitigate this problem, they often introduce new accuracy bottlenecks to weights. Besides, most of these techniques are implemented as online approaches, which can result in heavy execution overheads. To address the afore-mentioned issues, We propose PRQuant (Permutation Residual Quantization), a training-free and low-overhead framework that combines channel reorganization with static weight-side residual compensation. After AWQ-style scaling, PRQuant identifies the input channels that contribute most to weight quantization error, permutes them into contiguous tail blocks, and constructs their residual weight sub-tensors offline. During inference, this contiguous structure enables the activation side to use tail blocks seamlessly without the expensive online gathering operation, and turns scattered residual compensation into a regular tail-augmented GEMM, substantially reducing latency. Experiments demonstrate that PRQuant effectively reduces down-projection reconstruction error. Ablation studies confirm that smoothing and residual compensation are the primary drivers of numerical improvement, while permutation provides a consistent marginal numerical benefit and, more importantly, enables a hardware-friendly contiguous layout that eliminates dynamic gathering overhead. Overall, PRQuant outperforms default MXFP4 and the evaluated PTQ baselines in average accuracy across five downstream benchmarks, improving over MXFP4 by 1.24 and 0.55 on Qwen3-4B-Instruct-2507 and Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507, respectively.

</details>


### [43] [Generalized Multimodal Foundation Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22107)
*Huizi Cui,Zongbo Han,Chenggong Ding,Naichuan Xiao,Jialong Yang,Jingdong Chen,Guangyu Wang,Qinghua Hu,Changqing Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes a generalized multimodal foundation model that can handle arbitrary modality combinations and prediction tasks by training on synthetic multimodal datasets with diverse causal structures and using in-context learning at inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal fusion models are limited to predefined modalities (e.g., vision, text, audio) and single tasks, making it difficult to quickly adapt to new downstream applications. The authors seek to investigate whether a general multimodal fusion model can be applied to arbitrary modality combinations and arbitrary prediction tasks.

Method: The authors propose a simple and effective learning paradigm based on training over the generation of large-scale synthetic multimodal datasets with diverse causal structures that formally characterize the generative processes of multimodal data in the real world. They build a generalized multimodal foundation model that encodes transferable multimodal correlations during training and activates appropriate associations through in-context examples during inference.

Result: Extensive experiments on 18 real-world datasets spanning 12 modalities and 11 prediction tasks demonstrate that the model achieves competitive performance with specialized models without task-specific adaptation.

Conclusion: The proposed generalized multimodal foundation model can serve as a unified foundation model for generalized multimodal learning, achieving competitive performance across diverse modalities and tasks without task-specific adaptation.

Abstract: Making prediction with multimodal data is widely used in diverse scenarios. Existing multimodal fusion models, once deployed, can only handle predefined modalities (e.g., vision, text and audio) and single tasks, making it difficult to quickly adapt to new downstream applications. Therefore, a natural yet rather aggressive question arises, whether there exists a general multimodal fusion model that can be applied to arbitrary modality combinations and arbitrary prediction tasks. We argue that a unified multimodal fusion model should not depend on specific modalities and instead encode transferable patterns of multimodal correlation. To this end, we propose a simple and effective learning paradigm based on training over the generation of large-scale synthetic multimodal datasets with diverse causal structures that formally characterize the generative processes of multimodal data in real world. Building on this framework, we propose the generalized multimodal foundation model, a unified foundation model for generalized multimodal learning. By constructing large-scale synthetic multimodal datasets with diverse correlation patterns, our model encodes transferable multimodal correlations during training and activates appropriate associations through in-context examples during inference. Extensive experiments on 18 real-world datasets spanning 12 modalities and 11 prediction tasks demonstrate that our model achieves competitive performance with specialized models without task-specific adaptation.

</details>


### [44] [Correcting Learning-based Perception for Safety](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22108)
*Yan Miao,Hussein Darir,Sayan Mitra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-step strategy is proposed to correct ML-based state estimation in autonomous systems: (1) offline characterization of perception uncertainties using preimages of perception contracts, and (2) runtime risk heuristic for selecting states from uncertain estimates to drive control decisions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ML perception boundaries are poorly characterized, leading to incorrect perception that causes unsafe or overly conservative downstream control actions in autonomous systems.

Method: The method uses a two-step approach: (1) offline computation to characterize uncertainties from ML module's state estimation using preimages of perception contracts, and (2) at runtime, a risk heuristic to choose particular states from uncertain estimates to drive control decisions. Evaluation was done through simulation on vision-based adaptive cruise controllers (ACC) in different weather conditions and road scenarios.

Result: Out of 45 ACC scenarios where the original Yolo and LaneNet-based perception control led to safety violations, 73% (33 scenarios) were corrected by the runtime perception correction. The remaining 27% couldn't be recovered due to non-conformant preimage construction. The method is not overly conservative with only a 2.8% average increase in completion time.

Conclusion: The runtime perception correction strategy effectively preserves safety in the majority of scenarios (73%) while maintaining reasonable performance (only 2.8% increase in completion time). However, 27% of scenarios cannot be recovered when the preimage construction of perception contracts is not fully conformant, indicating limitations in the current approach.

Abstract: Learning-enabled perception is important in many autonomous systems. Unlike traditional sensors, the boundary where ML perception does or does not work is poorly characterized. Incorrect perception can lead to unsafe or overtly conservative downstream control actions. In this paper, we propose a two-step strategy for correcting ML-based state estimation. First, an offline computation is used to characterize the uncertainties resulting from the ML module's state estimation, using preimages of perception contracts. Second, at runtime, a risk heuristic is used to choose particular states from the uncertain estimates to drive the control decisions. We perform extensive simulation-based evaluation of this runtime perception correction strategy on different vision-based adaptive cruise controllers (ACC modules), in different weather conditions, and road scenarios. Out of 45 ACC scenarios where the original perception-based control system using Yolo and LaneNet led to safety violations, in 73% of the scenarios, our runtime perception correction preserved safety; our method wouldn't be able to recover 27% of the scenarios where the construction of the preimages of perception contracts is not fully conformant. Further, our runtime perception correction strategy is not overly conservative---on the average only a 2.8% increase in completion time is experienced in the corrected scenarios, with mild interventions.

</details>


### [45] [A Shared Learning Rate Is Not a Neutral Control in Selective On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22109)
*Chencheng Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper reveals that comparing selective vs dense knowledge distillation methods under a single shared learning rate is fundamentally flawed, as learning rate choice critically affects which method performs better. This "selector-rate entanglement" means the literature's conclusions about which method is superior may be artifacts of the learning rate chosen rather than true method differences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The literature compares selective distillation selectors under a single shared learning rate, treating this as a neutral choice. The authors discovered this assumption is false - learning rate dramatically affects selective methods but not dense supervision, making comparisons unreliable.

Method: Empirical study across an 8x learning rate grid on GSM8K (Qwen2.5-1.5B student, 7B teacher) using LoRA, with dense supervision vs multiple selective methods (random 5%, total-variation, teachability selectors). Also includes a preregistered frozen-scoring ablation (12 seeds per cell) and验证 on MATH-500.

Result: Dense supervision is statistically flat across learning rates (1.8 pp swing, p=0.26) while selective methods are highly sensitive: 5.4-17.7 pp swings. The dense-vs-selective verdict ranges from 10.1 pp at lr=1e-4 to 5.1 pp at lr=5e-5 (2.0x difference). AdamW update magnitudes track learning rate within 2.2% despite 15.5x gradient-norm differences. Frozen-scoring ablation shows live scoring adds 3.79±1.69 pp rate sensitivity (p=0.035) while frozen arm remains entangled (p=0.015). Under full fine-tuning, dense swings 19.8 pp, selective swings 49.5 pp, verdict ranges from +3.6 pp (non-significant) to +34 pp (p=0.005). On MATH-500, rate dependence doesn't reproduce under LoRA but ~10 pp selective cost does.

Conclusion: The standard practice of reporting a single shared learning rate for selector comparisons is invalid. The authors prescribe reporting the full arm × rate matrix as a precondition for valid selector comparisons. The learning rate is not a neutral parameter but a critical factor that determines which distillation method wins.

Abstract: Selective on-policy distillation trains a student only at the token positions a selector scores highest, and the literature compares selectors under a single shared learning rate--a control chosen to be neutral. We show it is not. Under LoRA on GSM8K (Qwen2.5-1.5B student, 7B teacher), across an 8x learning-rate grid, dense supervision is statistically flat (swing 1.8 pp, p=0.26) while every selective arm moves with the rate: 5.4 pp for a random 5% subset, 6.7 pp for a total-variation selector, up to 17.7 pp for a teachability selector. Consequently the dense-versus-selective verdict reads 10.1 pp at lr=1e-4 but 5.1 pp at 5e-5--a 2.0x difference decided by a parameter the protocol treats as scenery--and two of six pairwise significance calls between selectors flip between adjacent rates without any rank inversion. We call this selector-rate entanglement and trace it to selection itself rather than step size: AdamW update magnitudes track the rate to within 2.2% despite 15.5x gradient-norm differences across arms. A preregistered frozen-scoring ablation (selection scored by the initial student; criterion, budget, and on-policy rollouts unchanged; 12 seeds per cell) shows live scoring adds 3.79+/-1.69 pp of rate sensitivity (p=0.035) while the frozen arm remains significantly entangled (p=0.015): the feedback loop aggravates the phenomenon rather than causing it. Under full fine-tuning at the rates this literature actually uses (1e-6 to 1e-5) the pattern grows: dense itself swings 19.8 pp, the selective arm 49.5 pp, and the verdict ranges from a non-significant +3.6 pp at the published operating point to +34 pp (p=0.005) one notch hotter. On MATH-500 the rate dependence does not reproduce under LoRA, scoping that result, while the ~10 pp cost of selective training does. We prescribe reporting the arm x rate matrix, not a shared-rate column, as a precondition for selector comparisons.

</details>


### [46] [Toward Fairness in Machine Learning Models for Predicting Treatment Retention and Premature Discontinuation in Medication for Opioid Use Disorder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22113)
*Tongnian Wang,Carolina Vivas-Valencia,Cici Bauer,Yanmin Gong,Kim-Kwang Raymond Choo,Yuanxiong Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study evaluates algorithmic fairness in machine learning models predicting medication for opioid use disorder (MOUD) retention and premature discontinuation, using TEDS-D data (2015-2019). The research assesses subgroup-level performance gaps across race, ethnicity, age, and sex, and tests bias mitigation techniques. Findings show ML models can exhibit significant subgroup-level performance disparities even with acceptable overall metrics, and bias mitigation only partially reduces these gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Persistent low retention and completion rates in MOUD treatment have driven interest in ML-based prediction models, but the fairness of these models across different patient populations remains unexplored, raising concerns about their use in treatment decision support.

Method: Used cross-sectional Treatment Episode Data Set-Discharges (TEDS-D) containing U.S. treatment episodes (2015-2019). Trained four ML models to predict: 1) premature treatment discontinuation, and 2) retention beyond 180 days in outpatient MOUD. Evaluated overall performance and subgroup-level error rates across race, ethnicity, age, and sex. Applied model explanation analyses and tested bias mitigation techniques.

Result: ML models for MOUD outcome prediction can exhibit subgroup-level performance gaps even when overall predictive performance appears acceptable. Bias mitigation can reduce but not fully eliminate these gaps without trade-offs.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates the importance of fairness-aware evaluation and transparent reporting of subgroup performance for responsible ML use in MOUD treatment settings for risk stratification and care prioritization.

Abstract: Persistent low retention and completion rates in medications for opioid use disorder (MOUD) have driven the use of machine learning (ML) models to predict retention and identify patients at risk of premature discontinuation. However, the fairness of these models across patient populations remains largely unexplored, raising concerns about their application in treatment decision support. This study systematically assesses algorithmic fairness in ML models for predicting MOUD retention and premature discontinuation and investigates the effectiveness of bias mitigation techniques. Using the cross-sectional Treatment Episode Data Set-Discharges (TEDS-D), which includes treatment episodes for individuals in the U.S. discharged between 2015 and 2019, we trained four ML models to predict premature treatment discontinuation and retention beyond 180 days among individuals receiving outpatient MOUD. We evaluated overall performance and subgroup-level error rates across patient subgroups defined by race, ethnicity, age, and sex, complemented by model explanation analyses. We further assessed bias mitigation techniques and their effects on both fairness and predictive performance. Our findings demonstrate that ML models for MOUD outcome prediction can exhibit subgroup-level performance gaps even when overall predictive performance appears acceptable and that bias mitigation can reduce, but not fully eliminate, these gaps without trade-offs. By demonstrating the importance of fairness-aware evaluation and transparent reporting of subgroup performance, this study provides practical insights for the responsible and context-sensitive use of ML models for risk stratification and care prioritization in MOUD treatment settings.

</details>


### [47] [ZoAQ: Adaptive Zeroth-Order Querying via Query-Reuse Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22115)
*Yangyang Feng,Yao Shu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ZoAQ is an adaptive zeroth-order optimization method that reuses past query evaluations to both generate updates and determine query budget allocation, eliminating the need for separate validation queries. It achieves 43-48% query reduction on synthetic objectives and 100% success in black-box attacks with significantly fewer queries than fixed-budget baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The key insight is that fixed-budget ZOO methods waste past query evaluations, while adaptive controllers require additional oracle calls to validate estimator reliability, offsetting their savings. The authors aim to enable adaptive query allocation without extra validation queries by reusing past evaluations for both update computation and budget determination.

Method: The method introduces query reuse, where past evaluations inform both the next gradient estimate and the decision of whether to query further. The analysis characterizes conditions under which the agreement between reused queries identifies updates supporting descent and guides the controller to determine sufficient query budgets.

Result: On synthetic objectives, ZoAQ reduces queries by 43-48% relative to fixed baselines using 1.2M total queries. In black-box attacks, it achieves 100% success with 320 average queries on MNIST and 625 on CIFAR-10. Across four OPT fine-tuning settings, ZoAQ saves 43-46% forward evaluations relative to fixed K=4, with accuracy changes ranging from -0.018 to +0.010.

Conclusion: ZoAQ successfully enables adaptive query allocation in zeroth-order optimization without requiring extra validation queries. The method provides significant query savings (43-48%) across various tasks while maintaining comparable or slightly improved performance, demonstrating the effectiveness of query reuse as a principled approach to budget allocation in ZOO.

Abstract: Zeroth-order optimization (ZOO) estimates updates from function evaluations, making perturbation queries a primary cost. Fixed budgets spend the same number of queries at every step, while adaptive controllers may offset their savings by using additional oracle calls to test estimator reliability. We introduce ZoAQ, an adaptive ZOO method built around query reuse. Rather than discarding past evaluations after each step, ZoAQ makes them useful for both the next update and the decision to query further. This enables adaptive query allocation without extra validation queries. Our analysis characterizes when this agreement identifies an update that supports descent and guides the controller to a sufficient query budget. On synthetic objectives, ZoAQ reduces queries by 43-48% relative to fixed baselines using 1.2M queries. In black-box attacks, it reaches 100% success with 320 and 625 average queries on MNIST and CIFAR-10, respectively. Across four OPT fine-tuning settings, ZoAQ saves 43-46% forward evaluations relative to fixed K=4, with accuracy changes within tasks ranging from -0.018 to +0.010.

</details>


### [48] [LE4Mob: Towards Inductive, Distance-Aware and General-Purpose Location Embedding for Human Mobility Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22117)
*Xinglei Wang,Stephen Law,Zichao Zeng,Junyuan Liu,Guangsheng Dong,Tao Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LE4Mob is a novel location embedding framework for human mobility modelling that addresses limitations of existing embeddings by being inductive, distance-aware, and geography-derived. It uses contrastive pre-training with distance-aware regularization to preserve spatial relationships, enabling representation of unseen locations and transferability across different mobility tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing location embeddings have three key limitations: (1) they depend on mobility observations, (2) they cannot represent unseen locations, and (3) they weakly preserve geographic distance constraints. These limitations restrict their reuse across datasets and mobility tasks.

Method: LE4Mob extends contrastive language-location pre-training and introduces a distance-aware regularisation objective that encourages the embedding space to preserve spatial relationships. The framework is pre-trained from geographic context to encode rich spatial-semantic information, allowing inductive generation of embeddings for unseen locations without requiring downstream task supervision.

Result: Experiments across multiple datasets and study areas show that LE4Mob outperforms strong baselines, with particular advantages in inductive settings and when downstream models rely directly on interactions between location embeddings.

Conclusion: Distance-aware, geography-derived location representations have significant potential as reusable foundations for human mobility modelling, offering transferability across different mobility tasks and the ability to handle unseen locations.

Abstract: Location representations provide mobility models with fundamental information about the spatial position, functional characteristics, and relationships of places. However, existing embeddings are often dependent on mobility observations, unable to represent unseen locations, and weakly constrained to retain geographic distance. This limits their reuse across datasets and mobility tasks. To address these limitations, we propose LE4Mob, an inductive, distance-aware, and geography-derived location embedding framework for mobility modelling. LE4Mob extends contrastive language-location pre-training while introducing a distance-aware regularisation objective that encourages the embedding space to preserve spatial relationships. Pre-trained from geographic context, LE4Mob can encode rich spatial-semantic information and generate embeddings for unseen locations inductively. Its independence from downstream mobility task supervision also makes it transferable across different mobility tasks.
