We evaluate LE4Mob on individual-level next location prediction and population-level commuter flow generation. Experiments across multiple datasets and study areas show that LE4Mob outperforms strong baselines, with particular advantages in inductive settings and when downstream models rely directly on interactions between location embeddings. These findings demonstrate the potential of distance-aware, geography-derived location representations as reusable foundations for human mobility modelling.

</details>


### [49] [Success Leaves Detours: Learning Executable Walkthroughs for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22120)
*Kaijie Chen,Chenyu Fang,Liang Yan,Bo Li,Bo Zhang,Peng Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Trace is a framework that extracts executable, state-conditioned procedures ("Walkthroughs") from sparse-reward trajectories by using credit-guided dependency analysis to identify valuable actions, estimate prerequisites, and remove irrelevant loops/detours, improving test-time agent performance on long-horizon tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test-time self-evolving agents reuse past experience, but sparse-reward trajectories contain failures, loops, and detours, while summaries omit state conditions and action dependencies needed for execution. There is a need to extract compact, state-conditioned, and verifiable procedures from noisy trajectories.

Method: Trace uses three main components: (1) Progress anchor detection from rewards and persistent state changes; (2) Credit propagation to identify valuable transitions; (3) Action prerequisite estimation from cross-episode success/failure evidence. Backward dependency slicing traces required facts to producers, extracting dependency-consistent action chains while removing irrelevant loops and detours.

Result: Trace consistently outperforms eight test-time learning and memory baselines across J-TTL, WebShop, and ScienceWorld with three open-source LLMs. It improves average AUC by 30.0% and Final-3 by 40.5% while using fewer inference tokens.

Conclusion: Long-horizon interaction benefits more from state-conditioned executable procedures (Walkthroughs) than from complete trajectories or abstract summaries. The dependency-grounded approach effectively extracts actionable, verifiable procedures from noisy trajectories.

Abstract: Test-time self-evolving agents improve by reusing past experience, yet sparse-reward trajectories contain failures, loops, and detours, while summaries often omit the state conditions and action dependencies needed for execution. We study executable Walkthrough induction from sparse-reward trajectories: extracting compact, state-conditioned, and verifiable procedures. Our key observation is that delayed credit identifies actions associated with progress but cannot determine whether they produce facts required by later actions. We propose Trace, a credit-guided, dependency-grounded framework that compiles noisy trajectories into executable Walkthrough Memory. It detects progress anchors from rewards and persistent state changes, propagates credit to identify valuable transitions, and estimates action prerequisites from cross-episode success and failure evidence. Backward dependency slicing then traces required facts to their producers, extracting dependency-consistent action chains while removing irrelevant loops and detours. The resulting Walkthroughs encode entry conditions, ordered state--action--effect steps, and completion and failure predicates, supporting reuse, intermediate-state resumption, and programmatic verification. Experiments on J-TTL, WebShop, and ScienceWorld with three open-source LLMs show that Trace consistently outperforms eight test-time learning and memory baselines. Compared with the strongest baseline, it improves average AUC and Final-$3$ by $30.0%$ and $40.5%$, respectively, while using fewer inference tokens. These results show that long-horizon interaction benefits more from state-conditioned executable procedures than from complete trajectories or abstract summaries.

</details>


### [50] [Modelling daily activity patterns from mobile phone location data via deep representation learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22121)
*Xinglei Wang,Junyuan Liu,Guangsheng Dong,Zichao Zeng,Stephen Law,James Haworth,Tao Cheng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper proposes ACE (Activity Chain Encoder), a self-supervised deep learning model that derives activity-purpose patterns from unlabelled mobile phone location data by combining urban embeddings with temporal features and Transformer-based sequence modeling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile phone location data lacks direct activity purpose labels, and the relationship between location characteristics and activity purpose is uncertain, particularly in mixed-use urban environments. Existing methods require labeled data or fail to capture the sequential organization of visits.

Method: ACE combines pre-trained urban embeddings with visit timing and duration, uses a Transformer to model sequential organization of stays, and is trained via masked activity modelling and identity-guided contrastive learning without requiring activity purpose labels. Daily representations are aggregated into user-level profiles, clustered, and interpreted through temporal-functional patterns and Census-derived demographic context.

Result: ACE identifies six differentiated weekday activity-pattern groups characterized by distinct daily rhythms, urban functional contexts, and demographic associations, outperforming three representative comparison methods.

Conclusion: ACE establishes a holistic route for deriving behaviorally meaningful population patterns from unlabelled mobile phone location data, enabling empirically grounded activity-pattern persona development.

Abstract: Passively collected mobile phone location data provide large-scale, longitudinal observations of human mobility but do not directly reveal activity purposes. The functional characteristics of visited locations offer useful contextual information, yet their relationship with activity purpose remains uncertain, particularly in mixed-use urban environments. We conceptualise activity pattern mining as an integrated process of representation, clustering, and interpretation, and propose the Activity Chain Encoder (ACE) for the representation stage. ACE is a self-supervised model that combines pre-trained urban embeddings with visit timing and duration and uses a Transformer to model the sequential organisation of stays. It is trained using masked activity modelling and identity-guided contrastive learning without requiring deterministic activity purpose labels. Learned daily representations are aggregated into user-level profiles, clustered, and interpreted through temporal-functional patterns and Census-derived demographic context. Applied to mobile phone app location data from London and compared with three representative methods, ACE supports the identification of six differentiated weekday activity-pattern groups characterised by distinct daily rhythms, urban functional contexts, and demographic associations. These complementary forms of evidence further support the development of empirically grounded activity-pattern personas, establishing a holistic route for deriving behaviourally meaningful population patterns from unlabelled mobile phone location data. The source code for the entire analytical pipeline developed in this study is publicly available at https://github.com/xlwang233/ACE.

</details>


### [51] [Rank Portability Does Not Imply Feasibility Portability: Target-Specific Evaluation of Joint Hardware Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22122)
*Wesley Shu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper questions the common assumption that architecture rankings transfer across hardware devices in neural architecture search. Through experiments on NAS-Bench-201 and 10,000 GPT architectures across 13 devices, the authors demonstrate that while latency ranking correlations are high (0.951-0.996), the actual feasibility portability (whether a model meets both latency and energy constraints) is poor, with violation risks ranging from 33.3% to 100%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to stress-test the fundamental assumption in cross-device hardware evaluation: that rankings of neural network architectures on a proxy device can reliably predict rankings on target devices. This assumption is critical for neural architecture search (NAS) and hardware co-design, where researchers often use easily accessible hardware (like GPUs) as proxies to select architectures for deployment on different target devices.

Method: The authors conducted two sets of experiments: (1) On NAS-Bench-201 with two public architecture families, examining cross-device rank correlations and feasible-set overlaps; (2) A replication study on 10,000 GPT architectures across 13 HW-GPT-Bench devices. They used AdaProxy as a diagnostic tool to improve latency ranking, and applied finite-sample split-conformal analysis to determine the number of calibration observations needed.

Result: Key results include: (1) Cross-device rank correlations on NAS-Bench-201 are moderate; (2) Target-comparable feasible-set overlap remains incomplete; (3) AdaProxy diagnostic substantially improves latency ranking; (4) A finite one-sided 90% threshold requires at least nine calibration observations; (5) On GPT architectures, relative to RTX3080 proxy, target latency SRCC ranges from 0.951 to 0.996, but proxy-reuse violation risk ranges from 33.3% to 100% under matched joint constraints.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that rank portability, feasibility portability, and target-specific decision support are three distinct evaluation objects. Cross-device evaluations should report which target environments actually support the operating point being claimed. The high correlation in latency rankings does not guarantee that a model selected on a proxy device will meet both latency and energy constraints on the target device.

Abstract: Cross-device hardware evaluation often assumes that if architecture rankings transfer across devices, a proxy device can support target-side model selection. We stress-test this assumption for joint latency-energy feasibility across two public architecture families. On NAS-Bench-201, cross-device rank correlations are moderate, while target-comparable feasible-set overlap remains incomplete. A faithful AdaProxy diagnostic substantially improves latency ranking, showing that the observed boundary failures are not simply due to weak adaptation. Exact finite-sample split-conformal analysis also exposes an evidence bottleneck: a finite one-sided 90% threshold requires at least nine calibration observations. We then replicate the phenomenon on 10,000 GPT architectures across 13 HW-GPT-Bench devices. Relative to an RTX3080 proxy, target latency SRCC ranges from 0.951 to 0.996, yet proxy-reuse violation risk ranges from 33.3% to 100% under matched joint constraints. These results show that rank portability, feasibility portability, and target-specific decision support are distinct evaluation objects. Cross-device evaluations should therefore report which target environments actually support the operating point being claimed.

</details>


### [52] [StationPDE: Station-Oriented Surface PDE Learning for Multi-Station Multivariate Weather Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22123)
*Xiao Wang,Changjian Chen,Rongwen Li,Hongwu Liu,Kun Fang,Zhuo Tang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: StationPDE is a novel multi-station multivariate weather forecasting model that bridges the gap between station-based statistical models and PDE-based physical models. It constructs a continuous surface field from discrete station observations and decomposes physical evolution into surface wind transport and upper-air inference, achieving 9.6% MSE reduction over state-of-the-art baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing station forecasting models lack explicit physical evolution, while PDE-based models require continuous fields and upper-air variables unavailable in surface station data. This work aims to combine the strengths of both approaches by learning physical dynamics from discrete station observations.

Method: StationPDE constructs a terrain-aware continuous surface field from discrete station observations and decomposes physical evolution into two components: (1) Surface wind transport that explicitly evolves observable weather variables, and (2) Upper-air inference using learnable horizontal diffusion to approximate missing upper-air variable influences. Additionally, a parallel data-driven diffusion branch captures complementary motion patterns, and an adaptive router integrates both forecasts for station-level multivariate prediction.

Result: Experiments on Weather2K and MeteoNet datasets show that StationPDE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, achieving approximately 9.6% MSE reduction on average compared with the strongest baseline.

Conclusion: StationPDE successfully bridges the gap between statistical station models and PDE-based physical models, demonstrating that physical dynamics can be learned from discrete surface observations. The approach provides interpretable physical evolution while maintaining accuracy improvements over data-driven baselines.

Abstract: Multi-station multivariate weather forecasting aims to forecast future weather variables at multiple weather stations from historical surface observations. Existing station forecasting models learn statistical dependencies among discrete stations, but lack explicit physical evolution. Meanwhile, PDE-based weather models provide interpretable physical dynamics, yet require continuous fields and upper-air variables unavailable in surface station data. To bridge this gap, we propose StationPDE, a station-oriented surface PDE learning model. StationPDE constructs a terrain-aware continuous surface field from discrete station observations and decomposes its physical evolution into surface wind transport and upper-air inference. Surface wind transport explicitly evolves observable weather variables, while upper-air inference uses learnable horizontal diffusion to approximate the missing influence of unavailable upper-air variables. A parallel data-driven diffusion branch captures complementary motion patterns, and an adaptive router integrates the two forecasts for station-level multivariate forecasting. Experiments on Weather2K and MeteoNet show that StationPDE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, reducing MSE by about $9.6\%$ on average compared with the strongest baseline. Code and implementation details are available at https://github.com/hnu-vis/StationPDE.

</details>


### [53] [SolarFlowRefiner: Refinement-Aware Flow Matching for Surface Solar Radiation Downscaling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22126)
*Udbhav Srivastava,Antonita Racheal,Yiheng Chen,Runlong Yu,Xinyue Ye*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper introduces SolarFlowRefiner, a refinement-aware flow-matching framework for downscaling surface solar radiation (SSR) from coarse ERA5 reanalysis data to high-resolution SolarCube fields. The method addresses the challenge of capturing localized cloud-driven variability that coarse physical reanalysis products cannot resolve.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-resolution surface solar radiation data is crucial for solar forecasting and grid operation, but existing reanalysis products (like ERA5) are too coarse to capture localized cloud-driven variability. A single ERA5 grid cell may contain both sunlit and cloud-shadowed regions, creating spatially sharp and ambiguous missing high-resolution corrections that one-stage predictors tend to oversmooth.

Method: The proposed SolarFlowRefiner uses a two-stage approach: (1) A conditional FlowMatch generator predicts a normalized correction to an upsampled ERA5 baseline; (2) A refiner is trained on prediction-conditioned states between the FlowMatch output and target residual, exposing it to the generator's structured errors. The refinement objective is backpropagated through the FlowMatch sampler, enabling joint optimization of generation and correction.

Result: Experiments on a day-blocked ERA5--SolarCube benchmark show consistent improvements over standalone generation and post-hoc refinement methods.

Conclusion: SolarFlowRefiner provides a general strategy for coupling generative predictors with iterative correctors, demonstrating that refinement-aware training can improve downscaling quality for solar radiation data. The approach addresses the stage-wise mismatch problem in previous two-stage methods.

Abstract: High-resolution surface solar radiation (SSR) is important for solar forecasting and grid operation. However, physically consistent reanalysis products are too coarse to resolve localized cloud-driven variability. In this paper, we study a multisource downscaling task that reconstructs high-resolution SolarCube SSR fields from coarse ERA5 radiative variables and co-registered satellite channels. The task is challenging because a single ERA5 grid cell may contain both sunlit and cloud-shadowed regions. As a result, the missing high-resolution correction can be spatially sharp and inherently ambiguous. One-stage predictors often oversmooth these structures. Post-hoc refinement also introduces a stage-wise mismatch: the generator is optimized independently, even though its output determines the refiner's initial state. We introduce SolarFlowRefiner, a refinement-aware flow-matching framework for SSR downscaling. A conditional FlowMatch generator first predicts a normalized correction to an upsampled ERA5 baseline. The refiner is then trained on prediction-conditioned states between the current FlowMatch output and the target residual. This exposes the refiner to the structured errors produced by the generator. The refinement objective is also backpropagated through the FlowMatch sampler, allowing generation and correction to be jointly optimized for the final reconstruction. Experiments on a day-blocked ERA5--SolarCube benchmark show consistent improvements over standalone generation and post-hoc refinement. More broadly, SolarFlowRefiner provides a general strategy for coupling generative predictors with iterative correctors.

</details>


### [54] [Helix-FNO: Spectral-Domain Operator Learning Coupled with a High-Fidelity Mechanistic Model for Fast Surrogate Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22129)
*Jiabao Zhao,Chuwei Wang,Jinxi Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Helix-FNO is a teacher-student architecture that combines a 32-state mechanistic simulation model with a Fourier neural operator (FNO) student to achieve millisecond inference speeds—three orders of magnitude faster than the mechanistic teacher—while maintaining accuracy for real-time decision support in treatment plant operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic simulation models are the only trustworthy description of treatment process dynamics but are too slow for modern decision engines requiring thousands of forward evaluations at 5-minute cadence. Existing surrogate modeling approaches learn single solutions and generalize poorly to new influent profiles, control settings, or plant layouts.

Method: The method uses a teacher-student architecture where a 32-state mechanistic teacher supplies high-fidelity input-field-to-solution pairs, curated via Latin-hypercube and uncertainty-based active learning to cover boundary and overload regimes. The FNO student learns the solution operator in the spectral domain (rather than any single solution), enabling generalization across entire families of equations. The paper presents the operator formulation, spectral convolution definition, weighted distillation loss, active-learning criterion, and analyzes approximation error of truncated Fourier expansion.

Result: Helix-FNO achieves three orders of magnitude speedup over the mechanistic teacher with millisecond inference latency, enabling massive candidate screening and online decision support. The illustrative study shows it outperforms physics-informed networks and data-driven recurrent surrogates on accuracy, dataset efficiency, and inference latency, placing it on the speed-accuracy Pareto front.

Conclusion: The Fourier neural operator approach successfully bridges the gap between high-fidelity mechanistic models and real-time decision requirements by learning the solution operator itself rather than individual solutions, enabling deployment in high-frequency decision loops while maintaining the physical credibility of the underlying mechanistic model.

Abstract: Mechanistic simulation models of full-scale treatment processes remain the only trustworthy, extrapolative description of the underlying physico-chemical dynamics, yet their runtime is far too slow to support the thousands of forward evaluations that a modern decision engine requires at a 5-minute decision cadence. The standard remedy-surrogate modelling-often produces a network that learns a single solution for a single configuration, so it generalises poorly to new influent profiles, control settings or plant layouts. This paper presents Helix-FNO, a teacher-student architecture that couples a thirty-two-state mechanistic teacher with a Fourier neural operator (FNO) student. The teacher supplies a high-fidelity dataset of input-field-to-solution pairs, curated by Latin-hypercube and uncertainty-based active learning to cover the boundary and overload regimes that matter in practice; the student learns, in the spectral domain, the solution operator itself rather than any single solution, thereby moving from learning one instance to learning an entire family of equations. We give the operator formulation, the spectral convolution definition, the weighted distillation loss and the active-learning criterion, and we analyse the approximation error of a truncated Fourier expansion with respect to the smoothness of the parametric solution manifold. An illustrative study compares Helix-FNO against a physics-informed network and a data-driven recurrent surrogate on accuracy, dataset efficiency and inference latency, and places the methods on a speed-accuracy Pareto front. The resulting operator is three orders of magnitude faster than the mechanistic teacher at millisecond inference, which is precisely the capability required for massive candidate screening and online decision support.

</details>


### [55] [Hierarchical Bayesian optimization of an aircraft-based multi-agent system-of-systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22130)
*Paul Saves,Thierry Lefebvre,Nathalie Bartoli,Jasper Bussemaker,Nikolaos Kalliatakis,Nabih Naeem,Prajwal Prakasha*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A hierarchical Bayesian optimization framework using Gaussian process meta-modeling is proposed for System-of-Systems architecting, handling discrete architectural choices, conditional dependencies, and heterogeneous design variables. Applied to an aircraft-based multi-agent wildfire suppression system, the approach demonstrates improved search efficiency and robustness over conventional surrogate-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex System-of-Systems (SoS) require high-fidelity multiphysics and multidisciplinary simulations, which are computationally expensive and carry evaluation failure risks, making direct optimization challenging. Surrogate-based approaches like Bayesian optimization are needed to manage expensive, black-box simulation tasks.

Method: The framework uses Gaussian process meta-modeling within a hierarchical Bayesian optimization approach to handle discrete architectural choices, conditional dependencies, and heterogeneous design variables inherent to System-of-Systems problems.

Result: The hierarchical formulation improves search efficiency and robustness compared to conventional surrogate-based methods, enabling exploration of large and structurally diverse design spaces with limited simulation budgets.

Conclusion: This work represents one of the first practical demonstrations of hierarchical Bayesian optimization applied to real-world SoS problems, offering transferable insights for aviation, sustainable mobility, and resilience-oriented system design. The hierarchical formulation enables exploration of large, structurally diverse design spaces with limited simulation budgets.

Abstract: Developing innovative system architectures increasingly relies on advanced modeling and optimization techniques to frame the architecting process and define the corresponding computational problems. For complex System-of-Systems (SoS), high-fidelity multiphysics and multidisciplinary simulations are essential for capturing detailed behaviors. However, their computational expense and the risk of evaluation failures make direct optimization challenging. To overcome these limitations, surrogate-based approaches, like Bayesian optimization, have emerged as effective tools for managing expensive, black-box simulation tasks. This work introduces a hierarchical Bayesian optimization framework that leverages Gaussian process meta-modeling to handle discrete architectural choices, conditional dependencies, and heterogeneous design variables inherent to SoS problems. Results show that the hierarchical formulation improves search efficiency and robustness compared to conventional surrogate-based methods, enabling the exploration of large and structurally diverse design spaces with limited simulation budgets.
  We apply the approach to an aircraft-based multi-agent system for wildfire suppression, a use case developed within the EU-funded COLOSSUS project that illustrates how SoS principles can coordinate heterogeneous aerial platforms with complementary roles, supporting both sustainable mobility and emergency response missions. Our framework provides a scalable methodology for SoS architecting and model exploration, offering transferable insights for applications in aviation, sustainable mobility, and resilience-oriented system design. By combining hierarchical representations with surrogate-based optimization, this work is among the first practical demonstrations of hierarchical Bayesian optimization applied to real-world SoS problems, advancing both methodology and practice.

</details>


### [56] [Weak Ties, Strong Signals: Efficient Training Data Detection in Diffusion LLMs via Independent Token Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22145)
*Hongyao Yu,Tianqu Zhuang,Ziyuan Xu,Hao Fang,Jiaxin Hong,Bin Chen,Shu-Tao Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses membership inference attacks on diffusion large language models (dLLMs), proposing Independent Token Sampling (ITS) to overcome limitations of random masking approaches that fail to account for token dependencies. The method uses attention-derived proxies to select independent token sets and incorporates diversity-promoting strategies, achieving significant AUC improvements (0.18 on ArXiv) over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Diffusion LLMs offer an alternative to autoregressive models but risk exposing sensitive training data during denoising. Detecting such memorization is challenging due to lack of efficient probability decomposition in dLLMs. Existing random masking methods introduce structural estimation error from uncontrolled token dependencies, which can obscure subtle memorization signals.

Method: The paper proposes Independent Token Sampling (ITS), a query-efficient framework with two key components: (1) An attention-derived pairwise dependency proxy to approximate CMI-aware selection of weakly-dependent token sets, avoiding costly direct CMI estimation over token combinations. (2) A diversity-promoting strategy to improve token coverage across sampling rounds, yielding aggregated signals less affected by dependency-induced approximation error.

Result: ITS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across different models and datasets, achieving an AUC improvement of 0.18 on the ArXiv dataset while maintaining strong performance under limited query budgets.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that accounting for token dependencies in membership inference for dLLMs is crucial. By using attention-based proxies to approximate CMI-aware token selection and incorporating diversity-promoting sampling, ITS provides a practical and effective solution for detecting training data memorization in diffusion language models.

Abstract: Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) offer a compelling alternative to autoregressive models, yet they may expose sensitive training data during denoising. Detecting such usage is challenging because dLLMs lack the efficient one-pass probability decomposition of causal architectures. Existing methods rely on random masking to obtain tractable token-wise detection signals under limited query budgets, but fail to control dependencies among masked tokens. We demonstrate that this token-wise approximation introduces a non-negative structural estimation error, which is theoretically characterized by the cumulative conditional mutual information (CMI) among masked tokens and can obscure subtle memorization signals. This insight suggests that reliable detection requires masked token sets with weak internal dependency. To avoid the prohibitive cost of directly estimating CMI over token combinations, we propose \textit{Independent Token Sampling} (ITS), a query-efficient framework that uses an attention-derived pairwise dependency proxy to approximate the CMI-aware selection criterion. ITS further incorporates a diversity-promoting strategy to improve token coverage across sampling rounds, yielding aggregated token-wise signals that are less affected by dependency-induced approximation error. Experiments on multiple datasets show that ITS consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across different models and datasets, achieving an AUC improvement of 0.18 on the ArXiv dataset while maintaining strong performance under limited query budgets. The code is available at https://github.com/Chrisqcwx/DLLM-MIA .

</details>


### [57] [GRRR: The Geometry of Reshaping, Rotation, and Routing in Decoder LLM post-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22146)
*Jianing Qi,Hao Tang,Zhigang Zhu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper analyzes how post-training (SFT and RL) modifies LLM weights by decomposing weight updates using SVD. It finds that the diagonal component (singular value changes) is largely dispensable—most post-training gains on math tasks persist even when this component is removed, suggesting post-training primarily reconfigures existing pathways rather than fundamentally altering the model's singular value structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding the mechanistic basis of post-training is critical for improving LLM development. While post-training methods like SFT and RL are widely used, it remains unclear how they actually modify model weights relative to pretrained weights—whether they fundamentally reshape the model's singular value structure or reconfigure existing pathways.

Method: The authors analyzed 12 post-training chains (combining SFT and RL) and expressed each weight update in the pretrained matrix's singular value decomposition (SVD) frame. This decomposition separates weight changes into three geometrically distinct components: (1) diagonal values that reshape singular values, (2) off-diagonal values that rotate coupling between pretrained input/output directions, and (3) null-space values that route outside the matrix's original nonzero SVD core.

Result: On a math evaluation suite, removing the diagonal component (which affects singular values) usually preserves most of the gains from post-training. This indicates that the gains are not primarily driven by changes to singular values.

Conclusion: Post-training gains are carried primarily by reconfiguring and extending pretrained pathways rather than by substantially changing the singular values of pretrained models. This provides insight into the mechanistic nature of post-training modifications.

Abstract: We study how post-training changes the weights of Large Language Models (LLMs) relative to their pretrained weights. Across 12 post-training chains with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL), we express each weight update in the pretrained matrix's singular value decomposition (SVD) frame. This decomposition separates the changes of three geometrically distinct components: diagonal values, which reshapes singular values; off-diagonal values, which rotates the coupling between pretrained input and output directions; and null-space values, which routes outside the matrix's original nonzero SVD core. On a math evaluation suite, we find that removing the diagonal component usually preserves most of the gains from post-training. These results suggest that post-training gains are carried primarily by reconfiguring and extending pretrained pathways rather than by substantially changing singular values of pre-trained models.

</details>


### [58] [SafeTune: A Unified Faithful Library for Auditing and Repairing Safety Drift in Fine-Tuned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22153)
*Pratinav Seth,Saisab Sadhu,Anshul Kaushal,Vinay Kumar Sankarapu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SafeTune is a source-available library that unifies four safety intervention paradigms for addressing safety drift in fine-tuned LLMs, providing a consistent workflow while preserving each paradigm's distinct requirements.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety drift in fine-tuned LLMs is addressed by scattered methods with incompatible implementations, making adoption and comparison difficult. There is a need for a unified framework.

Method: SafeTune unifies four intervention paradigms (post-hoc weight recovery, safety-constrained fine-tuning, gradient-based unlearning, inference-time steering) with shared utilities, a modular registry, and configuration-driven workflow.

Result: SafeTune enables controlled comparisons and deployment case studies in finance and medical domains, characterizing safety drift and evaluating interventions on refusal-behavior and capability evaluations.

Conclusion: SafeTune provides a unified, modular framework for safety intervention in LLMs, supporting new methods and domains without pipeline redesign, and enabling calibrated or layered mitigation strategies.

Abstract: Methods for addressing safety drift in fine-tuned Large Language Models (LLMs) are scattered across incompatible implementations, lifecycle stages, and evaluation protocols, making them difficult to adopt and compare. We introduce SafeTune, a source-available library that unifies four intervention paradigms: post-hoc weight recovery, safety-constrained fine-tuning, gradient-based unlearning, and inference-time steering, alongside shared interpretability, evaluation, and deployment utilities. SafeTune provides a consistent configuration-driven workflow while preserving the distinct inputs and intervention points each paradigm requires. Its modular registry supports new methods, benchmarks, judges, models, and fine-tuning domains without redesigning the surrounding pipeline. We demonstrate SafeTune through controlled comparisons and finance and medical deployment case studies, showing how it characterizes safety drift, evaluates feasible interventions on common refusal-behavior and capability evaluations, and supports calibrated or layered mitigation.

</details>


### [59] [A Comparative Framework for Evaluating Foundation Models on Tabular Data: A Case Study in Healthcare](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22154)
*Majid Lotfian Delouee,Sjors G. J. G. In 't Veld,Martijn C. Schut*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comparative evaluation framework (\system{}) for scoring and ranking tabular foundation models (TFMs) across six clinically meaningful dimensions: generalization, privacy protection, data efficiency, scalability, interpretability, and fairness. Includes a taxonomy of 45 TFMs organized by architecture, demonstrated on iron deficiency screening and heart failure prediction use cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surveys catalog capabilities of tabular foundation models but fail to provide structured comparison for real clinical applications. Clinicians and data scientists lack guidance on which model to choose for specific healthcare tasks.

Method: The framework scores TFMs across six dimensions, each broken into measurable sub-components. Optional "super-metrics" combine groups of sub-components for diagnostic views. Applied to two healthcare use cases: iron deficiency screening and heart failure prediction.

Result: The same set of metrics leads to different model rankings depending on clinical context. Provides taxonomy of 45 TFMs organized by underlying architecture.

Conclusion: The framework enables clinicians and data scientists to select appropriate TFMs based on specific task requirements, addressing the practical challenge of model selection in healthcare settings.

Abstract: Tabular data is the most common format in clinical practice, encompassing laboratory results, medication records, diagnostic codes, and patient demographics. As foundation models for tabular data have grown in number and variety, a practical question has become harder to answer: which model should a clinician or data scientist actually choose for a given task, and why? Existing surveys catalogue what these models can do, but they stop short of providing a structured way to compare them against the specific demands of a real application. We introduce \system{}, a comparative evaluation framework that scores and ranks tabular foundation models (TFMs) across six clinically meaningful dimensions: how well a model generalizes to new datasets, how effectively it protects patient privacy, how much data it needs to perform well, how it scales with growing datasets and feature spaces, how interpretable its predictions are to clinicians, and how fairly it performs across patient subgroups. Each dimension is broken down into measurable sub-components, and groups of sub-components can optionally be combined into supplementary compound scores, called super-metrics, that provide a diagnostic view of how a model performs across several dimensions simultaneously. To show how the framework works in practice, we apply it to two healthcare use cases, screening for iron deficiency and predicting heart failure, demonstrating how the same set of metrics leads to different model rankings depending on what matters most in each clinical context. We also provide a taxonomy of 45 TFMs organized by their underlying architecture, which serves as a reference for researchers and practitioners looking to navigate this rapidly expanding field.

</details>


### [60] [From Latent Biomarkers to Clinical Rules: Embedding-Guided Rule Mining and Attribution-Based Translation for Interpretable Tabular Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22155)
*Majid Lotfian Delouee,Hamed Ayoobi,Sjors G. J. G. In 't Veld,Martijn C. Schut*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents a pipeline that mines decision rules in the latent space of an FT-Transformer (deep learning model) and translates them back into interpretable clinical features. The approach identifies latent biomarkers, extracts rules using decision trees, and translates rules using gradient-input saliency and CLS attention attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clinical decision support tools require both accuracy and interpretability. Rule-based models provide transparency but may miss complex patterns from variable interactions. This work aims to bridge the gap between high-performing deep learning models and interpretable clinical rules.

Method: Four-step pipeline: (1) FT-Transformer creates latent embeddings, (2) embedding dimensions that consistently separate patient groups are identified as latent biomarkers, (3) small decision trees mine rules in latent space, (4) selected rules are translated back to clinical features using gradient-input saliency and CLS attention attribution. Evaluated on 6 public datasets at 4 embedding dimensions.

Result: Translated rules outperformed raw-feature rules in 5 of 6 datasets, with mean AUROC gains of 0.04-0.23. However, on the heart disease dataset, embedding-space rules achieved 0.98 AUROC but translation reduced this to 0.72, demonstrating that some high-performing latent rules cannot be represented by simple raw-feature conditions.

Conclusion: Latent-space rule discovery can uncover predictive patterns that raw-feature rules miss, while translating them into clinically measurable features that clinicians can evaluate. However, there are cases where latent patterns cannot be adequately expressed through simple clinical feature conditions.

Abstract: Clinical decision support tools are most useful when accurate predictions are accompanied by understandable explanations. Rule-based models provide transparency, but rules derived directly from raw clinical measurements may miss patterns arising from interactions between multiple variables. We present a four-step pipeline that mines decision rules in the latent space of an FT-Transformer and translates them back into measurable clinical features. Embedding dimensions that consistently separate patient groups are treated as latent biomarkers, rules are mined using small decision trees, and selected rules are translated using gradient-input saliency and CLS attention attribution. We evaluate the framework on six public clinical and population health datasets at four embedding dimensions. Translated rules outperformed raw-feature rules in five of six datasets, with mean AUROC gains ranging from 0.04 to 0.23. On the heart disease dataset, embedding-space rules reached 0.98 AUROC, but translation reduced this to 0.72, showing that high-performing latent rules cannot always be represented by simple raw-feature conditions. These results show that latent-space rule discovery can uncover predictive patterns while translating them into clinically measurable features that can be evaluated by clinicians.

</details>


### [61] [The Limits of Speculation: Bounding Speculative Decoding in Mixture-of-Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22156)
*Aidar Amankulov,Denis Mamatin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper addresses unstable verification costs in speculative decoding for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models by formulating speculation-budget selection as an offline Stochastic Shortest Path (SSP) problem and using counterfactual simulation to build a diagnostic Oracle. Analysis in "Delta Space" reveals that rejected candidates form a strict linear boundary, demonstrating that global optimization is locally governed by balancing marginal cost against expected progress.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The core problem is that speculative decoding in MoE models suffers from unstable verification costs due to input-dependent expert loading. This creates challenges for efficient inference since the computational cost varies unpredictably with different inputs.

Method: The authors formulate speculation-budget selection as an offline Stochastic Shortest Path (SSP) problem over reference sequences. They build a diagnostic Oracle that uses counterfactual simulation to account for MoE verification cost. The analysis is performed on the Qwen3-Coder and EAGLE-3 pairing in the space of marginal deltas ("Delta Space").

Result: The key finding is that rejected candidates form a strict linear boundary in Delta Space. This demonstrates that a complex global optimization problem is locally governed by a necessary condition that balances marginal cost against expected progress, expressed as (ΔE[Cost])/(ΔE[a]).

Conclusion: This work provides a rigorous mathematical reference point for designing future adaptive online heuristics. The linear boundary discovered represents a fundamental necessary condition that can guide the development of more efficient speculative decoding strategies for MoE models.

Abstract: Speculative decoding in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models faces the problem of unstable verification cost caused by input-dependent expert loading. To study the physics of this process, we formulate speculation-budget selection as an offline Stochastic Shortest Path (SSP) problem over reference sequences and build a diagnostic Oracle that uses counterfactual simulation to account for MoE verification cost. A detailed analysis of the Oracle's decisions on the Qwen3-Coder and EAGLE-3 pairing, in the space of marginal deltas (Delta Space), shows that rejected candidates form a strict linear boundary. This result demonstrates that a complex global optimization is locally governed by a necessary condition balancing marginal cost against expected progress ($\frac{Δ\mathbb{E}[Cost]}{Δ\mathbb{E}[a]}$), providing a rigorous mathematical reference point for designing future adaptive online heuristics.

</details>


### [62] [PAGE: Partition-Aware Gated KV-Cache Eviction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22157)
*Pankaj Kumar,Subhankar Mishra*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PAGE is a per-input safeguard for KV-cache eviction in LLM inference that predicts whether eviction will be harmful for a given input before any decoding occurs. It uses a single scalar metric (early-to-late drop in pairwise top-k head agreement) from prefill attention to classify inputs into capacity-bound (eviction catastrophic) or dilution-prone (eviction safe) classes, applying eviction only when safe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current KV-cache eviction methods decide which tokens to keep but not whether to evict at all. This hides a class of inputs where compression drives accuracy from 99% to 0%. The benchmark mean can mask this catastrophic failure on specific inputs, making it necessary to treat eviction as a per-input admission decision rather than a uniform policy.

Method: The method computes a single label-free scalar from prefill attention: the early-to-late drop in pairwise top-k head agreement. This metric predicts whether an input belongs to the capacity-bound class (where eviction is catastrophic) or dilution-prone class (where eviction is safe). PAGE thresholds this drop—applying any base evictor when the drop is large and retaining the full cache otherwise. No training or accuracy labels required. A per-model unlabeled pilot of ~100 inputs recalibrates the threshold for new model families.

Result: PAGE reduces the harm rate on the capacity-bound regime from 0.75 to 0.026, a 29× reduction, across four evictors, four models, and two benchmarks. It transforms a 99%→0% accuracy collapse into a flat 89% without retraining the evictor. The safeguard is inert wherever eviction is already safe. Realized compression is 1.8–3.4× (mean 2.9×) against a nominal 16× budget, decaying toward unity by batch 16 under static provisioning.

Conclusion: PAGE provides a targeted safety gain for KV-cache eviction by identifying and protecting the capacity-bound class of inputs—a small, identifiable minority—while allowing eviction where safe. The early-to-late drop in attention head agreement consistently orders inputs by eviction safety across four architecture families. A trained evictor wins at matched memory, and PAGE serves as a valuable safeguard that can be combined with any base evictor without modification.

Abstract: KV-cache eviction methods decide which tokens to keep but not whether to evict at all, so a benchmark mean can hide a class of inputs on which compression drives accuracy from 99\% to 0\%. We reframe eviction as a per-input admission decision and show that inputs separate into a capacity-bound class, where eviction is catastrophic at every budget, and a dilution-prone class, where eviction is safe or beneficial. A single label-free scalar computed from prefill attention, the early-to-late drop in pairwise top-$k$ head agreement, predicts this class before any decoding. PAGE thresholds this drop: it applies any base evictor when the drop is large and retains the full cache otherwise, with no training and no accuracy labels. The drop orders inputs by eviction safety consistently across four architecture families, and a per-model unlabeled pilot of about 100 inputs recalibrates the threshold for a new family. Used as a safeguard, PAGE cuts the harm rate on the capacity-bound regime from 0.75 to 0.026, a 29 $\times$ reduction, across four evictors, four models, and two benchmarks, turning a 99\% to 0\% collapse into a flat 89\% without retraining the evictor. The gate is inert wherever eviction is already safe, and the capacity-bound class it protects is a small, identifiable minority of inputs, so the benefit is a targeted safety gain rather than an average one. PAGE is a per-input safeguard, not a compressor: realized compression is $1.8 - 3.4 \times$ (mean 2.9$\times$) against a nominal 16$\times$ budget and decays toward unity by batch 16 under static provisioning, and a trained evictor wins at matched memory.

</details>


### [63] [StepKV: Step-Aware KV Cache Compression for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22158)
*Boyu Feng,Jiahong Liu,Yifan Li,Wenhao Yu,Zexuan Qiu,Yuliang Sun,Ming Shen,Xiang Li,Quanyu Dai,Irwin King*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: StepKV addresses KV cache compression for multi-step LLM agents by treating reasoning steps as first-class retention units rather than treating the cache as a flat token stream. It combines token-level saliency with trajectory-derived step utility scores to globally rank prunable tokens, maintaining accuracy under low KV budgets where token-level baselines fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The problem is that existing KV cache pruning methods treat the cache as a flat token stream and rank tokens by recency or attention saliency. This creates a mismatch between the unit of compression (individual tokens) and the unit of reasoning (reasoning steps). Early observations or intermediate decisions may receive little recent attention but remain essential for later evidence synthesis. This failure mode is termed "Reasoning Continuity Disruption."

Method: StepKV associates cache entries with their generating reasoning steps, estimates step utility from trajectory-derived signals, and combines this utility with token-level saliency. The resulting scores globally rank prunable tokens, from which StepKV retains the top-scoring entries under a target budget.

Result: Across multi-hop QA and long-horizon web reasoning tasks, StepKV sustains accuracy under low KV budgets where token-level baselines degrade sharply.

Conclusion: StepKV offers a more robust efficiency-accuracy trade-off for multi-step agent inference by providing a step-centric perspective for KV cache compression.

Abstract: Key-value (KV) caching is essential for efficient autoregressive large language model (LLM) inference, but the cache grows linearly with context length, increasing storage and decoding costs. KV cache compression mitigates this cost by retaining only a subset of cached tokens. This challenge is particularly important for multi-step LLM agents, where a query expands into trajectories of reasoning, tool interactions, and retrieved observations. Existing pruning methods typically treat the cache as a flat token stream and rank tokens by recency or attention saliency. This creates a mismatch between the unit of compression and the unit of reasoning: token-level pruning removes individual entries, whereas useful information in multi-step agents is often organized into reasoning steps with uneven and delayed importance. Consequently, an early observation or intermediate decision may receive little recent attention yet remain essential for later evidence synthesis. We term this failure mode Reasoning Continuity Disruption.These observations motivate KV cache compression that jointly considers token- and reasoning-step-level information. StepKV addresses this goal by treating reasoning steps as first-class retention units. It associates cache entries with their generating steps, estimates step utility from trajectory-derived signals, and combines this utility with token-level saliency. The resulting scores globally rank prunable tokens, from which StepKV retains the top-scoring entries under a target budget. StepKV thus provides a step-centric perspective for agent KV cache compression. Across multi-hop QA and long-horizon web reasoning tasks, StepKV sustains accuracy under low KV budgets where token-level baselines degrade sharply, offering a more robust efficiency-accuracy trade-off for multi-step agent inference.

</details>


### [64] [Uncertainty and Business-Aware Remaining Useful Life Estimation for Semiconductor Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22160)
*Davide Frizzo,Francesco Borsatti,Gian Antonio Susto*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A predictive maintenance framework for semiconductor manufacturing that combines Deep Learning sequence models with Simultaneous Quantile Regression (SQR) for uncertainty-aware Remaining Useful Life (RUL) estimation. The approach uses Diagonal State Spaces (S4D) architecture which achieves superior RUL estimates across quantiles and significantly reduces business costs by avoiding premature interventions compared to traditional Preventive Maintenance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Semiconductor manufacturing involves tightly interconnected components where a single failure can disrupt the entire production pipeline. Early identification of assets likely to fail is essential, but maintenance planning must balance unexpected failures against prematurely interrupted operating life. This creates a need for accurate RUL estimation that enables risk-aware maintenance decisions.

Method: The paper proposes a Predictive Maintenance (PdM) framework combining Deep Learning sequence models with Simultaneous Quantile Regression (SQR) for uncertainty-aware RUL estimation. Multiple architectures are compared on ion-milling data from the 2018 PHM Data Challenge, including architectures based on State Space Models (SSM). Evaluation uses both prediction metrics (quantiles) and business metrics: Unexpected Breaks (UB), Unexploited Lifetime (UL), and a cost-weighted objective.

Result: Diagonal State Spaces (S4D) delivers the best RUL estimates across quantiles. Compared to Preventive Maintenance (PvM) baselines, the framework substantially lowers business cost by avoiding systematically early interventions while maintaining reliable failure prediction.

Conclusion: The S4D-based framework with uncertainty-aware quantile regression enables cost-sensitive maintenance planning in semiconductor production. It successfully balances the tradeoff between unexpected failures and unexploited equipment lifetime, providing practical value for industrial maintenance scheduling.

Abstract: Semiconductor manufacturing relies on tightly interconnected components, so early identification of the assets most likely to fail is essential to prevent a single breakdown from disrupting the entire production pipeline. Maintenance planning must therefore balance unexpected failures against prematurely interrupted operating life. We present a Predictive Maintenance (PdM) framework combining Deep Learning (DL) sequence models and Simoultaneous Quantile Regression (SQR)
  for uncertainty-aware Remaining Useful Life (RUL) estimation and risk-aware maintenance decisions. Several architectures are compared on ion-milling data from the 2018 PHM Data Challenge (PHM18), including architectures based on State Space Models (SSM), using prediction and business metrics: Unexpected Breaks (UB), Unexploited Lifetime (UL), and a cost-weighted objective. Diagonal State Spaces (S4D) delivers the best Remaining Useful Life (RUL) estimates across quantiles and, relative to Preventive Maintenance (PvM) baselines, substantially lowers business cost by avoiding systematically early interventions. The results support uncertainty-aware, cost-sensitive maintenance planning in semiconductor production.

</details>


### [65] [TARGet: Topology-Aware Fusion-based Radio Frequency Circuit Functional Modeling using Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22165)
*Soroosh Noorzad,Sebastian Bodero,Morteza Fayazi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TARGet is a topology-aware RF circuit modeling method that uses GNNs and sub-circuit connectivity-aware neural networks with S-parameter representations to enable efficient cross-topology learning for automatic synthesis of analog and RF circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML approaches for RF circuit modeling require separate training data for each topology, overlook circuit topology information, and rely on flat feature representations needing substantial training data. There is a need for a topology-aware modeling method that can learn across multiple circuit topologies efficiently.

Method: TARGet proposes a fusion-based architecture combining Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and sub-circuit connectivity-aware neural networks. It considers circuits at two levels: sub-circuits (using S-parameter representations for reusable behavioral abstraction) and overall circuit topology. This enables learning across multiple topologies with explicit topology information incorporation.

Result: TARGet achieves sub-1% prediction error while reducing required training data by up to 35.5x compared to SOTA approaches. It achieves 9.7x higher prediction accuracy under a strict 1% error threshold. In zero-shot transfer evaluation to unseen sub-circuit topologies, TARGet reduces NMAE by up to 45%.

Conclusion: TARGet demonstrates that topology-aware modeling with S-parameter representations and GNN-based architecture can significantly improve data efficiency and enable zero-shot transfer to unseen circuit topologies, representing a promising advance in automatic RF circuit synthesis.

Abstract: Automatic synthesis of analog and Radio Frequency (RF) circuits is an emerging area that requires an efficient circuit modeling method. In recent years, Machine Learning (ML) solutions have played a promising role in this regard. However, many existing ML approaches require separate training data for each circuit topology, even when a single circuit component is added or removed. In addition, they overlook circuit topology information, which limits their ability to capture complex component interactions. Furthermore, they rely on fully connected neural networks with flat feature representations, which require substantial amounts of training data. In this work, we propose an open-source topology-aware RF circuit modeling method, TARGet. Our model considers the circuit at two levels: sub-circuits and the overall circuit topology. At the sub-circuit level, TARGet leverages S-parameter representations to capture sub-circuit behavior rather than relying on individual circuit components, providing a reusable behavioral abstraction for RF building blocks. Moreover, TARGet explicitly incorporates circuit topology information into the model, enabling it to learn across multiple topologies. TARGet introduces a novel fusion-based architecture that integrates Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) and sub-circuit connectivity-aware neural networks to improve data efficiency. Experimental evaluation across multiple RF circuit topologies demonstrates that TARGet achieves sub-1% prediction error while reducing the required training data by up to 35.5x compared to state-of-the-art (SOTA) approaches. Furthermore, TARGet achieves 9.7x higher prediction accuracy under a strict 1% error threshold relative to SOTA models. A held-out matching-network evaluation further demonstrates zero-shot transfer to an unseen sub-circuit topology, where TARGet reduces NMAE by up to 45%.

</details>


### [66] [Can Coding Agents Reproduce Official Statistics? Metadata, Retry Budget and the Limits of Execution Feedback in a Controlled Eurostat Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22222)
*Sabina-Cristiana Necula*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This study evaluates whether large language models can reliably generate valid Eurostat official statistics code by testing different conditions: task-only, task+metadata, metadata+execution feedback repair loop, and metadata+no diagnostics. The research finds that execution diagnostics alone are insufficient - reliable statistical coding requires semantic validation against frozen specifications, fully specified output contracts, and retry budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can generate executable data-analysis code, but successful execution doesn't guarantee valid official-statistics results. The authors investigate whether authoritative metadata and execution feedback improve reproducibility of Eurostat answers produced by a coding agent.

Method: A benchmark of 30 natural-language tasks covering seven domains, seven Eurostat datasets, and four difficulty tiers was run under four conditions: (A) task only, (B) task plus frozen dataset metadata card, (C) metadata plus repair loop driven by sanitized execution feedback, (D) metadata plus same attempt budget with no diagnostics. Claude Sonnet 5 generated Python through Anthropic Messages API in three independent replicates (360 task-runs). A companion experiment tested under-specified output contracts. Exact correctness required successful execution, correct dataset, filters, output shape, values, and unit.

Result: Condition C (metadata + execution feedback) performed significantly worse under under-specified output contracts, understated by 23.4 points. This reveals that evaluator and contract design can dominate measured agent error. The study shows execution diagnostics alone are insufficient for reliable statistical coding.

Conclusion: Reliable statistical coding agents need three key components: (1) semantic validation against frozen specifications, (2) a fully specified output contract, and (3) a retry budget. Execution diagnostics alone are not sufficient - proper validation requires explicit semantic specifications and clear output contracts rather than relying on runtime feedback.

Abstract: Large language models can generate executable data-analysis code, but successful execution is not equivalent to a valid official-statistics result. This study asks whether authoritative metadata and execution feedback improve the reproducibility of Eurostat answers produced by a coding agent, and isolates what execution feedback actually contributes. A benchmark of 30 natural-language tasks covering seven domains, seven Eurostat datasets and four difficulty tiers was run under four conditions: task only (A), task plus a frozen dataset metadata card (B), metadata plus a repair loop driven by sanitized execution feedback (C), and metadata plus the same attempt budget with no diagnostics of any kind (D). Claude Sonnet 5 generated Python through the Anthropic Messages API in three independent replicates, yielding 360 task-runs. Exact correctness required successful execution, the correct dataset, filters, output shape, values and unit. A companion experiment run under an under-specified output contract, in which the required ranking key and unit representation were never stated to the model, understated condition C by 23.4 points, showing that evaluator and contract design can dominate measured agent error. Reliable statistical coding agents need semantic validation against frozen specifications, a fully specified output contract, and a retry budget - not execution diagnostics.

</details>


### [67] [Clustering-Based Collective Anomaly Detection in IoT Systems: A Graph Neural Network Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22166)
*Dalila Khettaf,Djamel Djenouri,Zeinab Rezaeifar,Youcef Djenouri*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel framework UGCAD for detecting collective anomalies in IoT network traffic using variational graph autoencoder (VGAE) and enhanced clustering, demonstrating superior performance over existing methods on CICIoT2023 and ToN-IoT datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid expansion of IoT devices has increased network traffic and cyberattack opportunities. While point anomaly detection is well-explored, collective anomaly detection remains relatively under-explored, creating a need for effective CAD methods in IoT networks without prior knowledge of group labels.

Method: UGCAD leverages a variational graph autoencoder (VGAE) to learn graph representations from IoT network data, which are then used to enhance a clustering algorithm for node grouping. Normal clusters are aggregated and refined, after which anomaly scores are applied to detect collective anomalies.

Result: UGCAD demonstrates effectiveness in both clustering and collective anomaly detection on CICIoT2023 and ToN-IoT network datasets. Comparative evaluations against traditional and state-of-the-art clustering-based CAD approaches confirm its superiority in accurately detecting collective anomalies.

Conclusion: UGCAD represents a superior approach for detecting collective anomalies in IoT network traffic, operating effectively without any prior knowledge of group labels or membership.

Abstract: The rapid advancement of Internet of Things (IoT) technology has led to the widespread deployment of smart, interconnected devices across a range of domains. However, this expansion has also resulted in a substantial increase in network traffic, creating more opportunities for malicious actors to launch cyberattacks and compromise sensitive information, thereby increasing the need for effective anomaly detection. The state-of-the-art in anomaly detection has predominantly focused on point anomalies. In contrast, the detection of collective anomalies remains relatively under-explored in the literature. In this paper, we introduce Unsupervised Graph Collective Anomaly Detection (UGCAD), a novel frame- work designed to identify collective anomalies in IoT network traffic. Unlike many existing methods, UGCAD operates on graph-structured data without any prior knowledge of group labels or membership. It leverages a variational graph autoencoder (VGAE) to learn the graph representation, which is subsequently used to enhance a clustering algorithm for effective grouping of nodes. To detect collective anomalies, clusters identified as normal are first aggregated and refined, after which anomaly scores are applied to detect collective anomalies. Extensive experiments conducted on the CICIoT2023 and ToN-IoT network datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of UGCAD in both clustering and collective anomaly detection (CAD). Furthermore, comparative evaluations against several traditional and state-of-the-art clustering-based CAD approaches confirm the superiority of UGCAD in accurately detecting collective anomalies.

</details>


### [68] [Role-Aware Morgan Fingerprints for Reaction Yield Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22167)
*Chinmay Mirji,Prashant Shekhar,Foram Madiyar,Hao Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MFP is a reaction yield prediction method using role-aware Morgan fingerprints combined with transformation-sensitive difference features, achieving R2 = 0.878 on Suzuki-Miyaura and R2 = 0.969 on Buchwald-Hartwig benchmarks while training an order of magnitude faster than graph- or Transformer-based methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ML methods for reaction yield prediction (graph neural networks, Transformer encoders) rely on learned representations that require heavy preprocessing overhead and break when input formatting is inconsistent. There is a need for a more efficient and robust approach.

Method: MFP computes count-based circular fingerprints (Morgan fingerprints) for each reaction component, aggregates them by chemical role (reactant, reagent, product), and combines them with transformation-sensitive difference features into a fixed-length reaction descriptor. This descriptor is fed to a feed-forward neural regressor. The approach folds all representation cost into a one-time preprocessing step, eliminating per-epoch message-passing overhead.

Result: MFP achieves R2 = 0.878 on Suzuki-Miyaura and R2 = 0.969 on Buchwald-Hartwig benchmarks. It trains an order of magnitude faster than graph- or Transformer-based alternatives (YieldBERT, GNAN). Ablation studies show radius-2 representations at nBits = 2048 provide the best balance of accuracy, speed, and cross-split stability.

Conclusion: MFP establishes itself as an effective, reproducible, and efficient baseline for reaction yield prediction, offering a simpler alternative to complex neural architectures while maintaining high accuracy and significant computational advantages.

Abstract: Predicting reaction yield from molecular structure and reaction context can cut experimental trial-and-error and speed up condition screening in synthetic chemistry. Recent methods for this task use learned representations such as graph neural networks or Transformer encoders over reaction SMILES (Simplified Molecular Input Line Entry System), but these approaches carry heavy preprocessing overhead and can break when input formatting is inconsistent. We propose MFP, a reaction yield prediction method built on role-aware Morgan fingerprints where count-based circular fingerprints are computed for each reaction component, aggregated by chemical role (reactant, reagent, product), and combined with transformation-sensitive difference features into a fixed-length reaction descriptor fed to a feed-forward neural regressor. We test MFP against state of the art methods such as YieldBERT (with and without data augmentation) and GNAN (graph neural network) on the Suzuki-Miyaura and Buchwald-Hartwig benchmarks using a shared preprocessing and evaluation protocol. MFP reaches R2 = 0.878 on Suzuki-Miyaura and R2 = 0.969 on Buchwald-Hartwig while training an order of magnitude faster than graph- or Transformer-based alternatives. A formal complexity analysis confirms that MFP folds all representation cost into a one-time preprocessing step, removing the per-epoch message-passing overhead that graph methods carry. An ablation over fingerprint radius and folded vector length shows that radius-2 representations at nBits =2048 give the best balance of accuracy, speed, and cross-split stability on both datasets. These results establish MFP as an effective, reproducible, and efficient baseline for reaction yield prediction.

</details>


### [69] [Multiple latent orderings better predict language model preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22170)
*Aviral Chawla,William H. W. Thompson,Jean-Gabriel Young*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper challenges the assumption that LLM preferences can be explained by a single latent ordering, proposing instead that observed intransitivity (inconsistent choice cycles like A > B > C > A) reflects the aggregation of multiple coherent preference orderings. The authors introduce a mixture Bradley-Terry model that infers latent preference components from pairwise comparisons, demonstrating across multiple models and tasks that aggregate preferences often hide heterogeneous underlying orderings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work treats LLM preference inconsistencies as sampling noise around a single latent ordering. This paper questions that assumption, arguing that intransitivity may be a structural feature rather than noise, with implications for how we evaluate and align language models.

Method: The authors first prove theoretically that observed inconsistencies cannot be explained by a single ordering under any monotone link function. They then introduce a noise-augmented mixture Bradley-Terry (MBT) model that infers latent preference components from repeated pairwise comparisons. This is tested across seven language models and four different tasks, with a case study on Moral Machine dilemmas.

Result: A mixture of orderings often explains structural inconsistencies better than single-utility models across seven models and four tasks. Aggregate preferences hide underlying preference heterogeneity. Models that disagree on aggregate orderings can still share latent components (shown in Moral Machine case study).

Conclusion: LLMs reflect plural preferences rather than a single coherent ordering. Current alignment and evaluation pipelines that treat LLM preferences as a single function risk averaging over coherent orderings that different users may endorse differently, potentially losing important nuance in preference modeling.

Abstract: Language models are frequently employed in settings where they are asked to make value judgments and choices. These observed choices often exhibit intransitivity: A model may prefer item $A$ to $B$ and $B$ to $C$, while also preferring $C$ to $A$. Existing work that models LLM preferences treats such inconsistencies as sampling noise around a single latent ordering. We instead propose that intransitivity reflects the aggregation of multiple latent, internally consistent orderings. We first show that observed inconsistencies cannot be explained by a single ordering under any monotone link function. We then introduce a noise-augmented mixture Bradley-Terry (MBT) model that infers latent preference components from repeated pairwise comparisons. Across seven models and four tasks, a mixture of orderings often explains structural inconsistencies better than single-utility models. We find that aggregate preferences often hide underlying preference heterogeneity. A case study on Moral Machine dilemmas shows that models which disagree on aggregate orderings can still share latent components. Together, these results suggest that LLMs reflect plural preferences. Alignment and evaluation pipelines that treat LLM preferences as a single function, therefore, risk averaging over coherent orderings that different users may endorse differently.

</details>


### [70] [Industrial Kinematic Trajectory Model (IKTM): Coordinate-Free Autoregressive Generator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22173)
*Max Amiri,David Eyers*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IKTM is a coordinate-free trajectory generator for industrial vehicles that uses kinematic sequences (speed/heading changes) without absolute spatial references. It employs an autoregressive causal transformer with probabilistic mixture heads and duration-conditioning, enabling zero-shot transfer across different industrial sites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing trajectory models rely on absolute coordinates, road-network tokens, or semantic zones - representations that are site-specific and poorly suited to unstructured industrial terrain (ports, mines, airports). There was a need for a site-agnostic trajectory model.

Method: Autoregressive causal transformer with probabilistic mixture heads, extending a prior coordinate-free Markovian model with deep sequence modeling and explicit duration-conditioning signal. Termination is duration-conditioned rather than spatial.

Result: Trained on one site and evaluated zero-shot on three unseen sites. Per-site mean Jensen-Shannon divergence: 0.035-0.050 bits (oracle-length protocol) and 0.032-0.042 bits (zero-shot prior-length protocol). Oracle-length values are 5.3-6.0x lower than the prior Markovian model. 100% termination accuracy with zero length-tracking error.

Conclusion: IKTM successfully achieves coordinate-free trajectory generation that generalizes across industrial sites without retraining. The duration-conditioned termination ensures exact length tracking. The model matches empirical turn-rate distributions and significantly outperforms the prior Markovian approach.

Abstract: Mobility simulation supports logistics, safety, and communications planning in industrial environments such as ports, mines, and airports. Existing trajectory models, however, rely on absolute coordinates, road-network tokens, or semantic zones: representations that are site-specific and not well suited to unstructured industrial terrain. We introduce the Industrial Kinematic Trajectory Model (IKTM), a coordinate-free trajectory generator that represents industrial vehicle motion through kinematic sequences (speed and heading change) with no absolute spatial reference. IKTM uses an autoregressive causal transformer with probabilistic mixture heads and extends our prior coordinate-free Markovian model with deep sequence modelling and an explicit duration-conditioning signal. Trained on one site and evaluated zero-shot on three unseen sites, it matches the small-turn shape of the empirical turn-rate distributions of held-out telematics; across all four sites, the per-site mean Jensen-Shannon divergence over 100 sampling seeds spans approximately 0.035-0.050 bits under an oracle-length duration-target protocol and approximately 0.032-0.042 bits under a fully zero-shot prior-length protocol, with similar ranges whether out-of-distribution (OOD) duration targets are drawn from each held-out site's empirical length distribution or from the Site A prior. Both protocols stay above the metric's sampling-noise floor (<=0.0049 bits). Paired by site, the oracle-length values are 5.3-6.0x lower than those of a re-implementation of our prior Markovian model under the same 1 Hz protocol. Termination is duration-conditioned rather than spatial: rollouts stop on 100% of trials with a length-tracking error of +0.0 +/- 0.0 s against the sampled target (100 of 100 exactly on target; N=100, T=0.2, untouched in-distribution test split).

</details>


### [71] [Contrastive World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22175)
*Bonnie Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Contrastive World Models replaces pixel reconstruction in world models (like Dreamer) with a Deep InfoMax-like contrastive objective that maximizes mutual information between state-action sequences and future observation features. This approach learns state representations predictive of the future without reconstructing irrelevant visual details, making models robust to visual distractors and backgrounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models trained via pixel reconstruction struggle in visually complex environments where irrelevant information dominates the objective and distracts from planning-relevant content. Pixel reconstruction forces the model to waste capacity on visually irrelevant details rather than focusing on information predictive of future states for control.

Method: Building on the Dreamer framework, the method replaces observation reconstruction loss with a Deep InfoMax-like lower bound. This objective maximizes mutual information between state-action sequences and local patch features of future observations, encouraging state representations to retain information predictive of the future without requiring pixel-level reconstruction.

Result: The method matches Dreamer and a momentum prediction baseline in the default setting. It substantially outperforms both when distractors or natural video backgrounds are introduced, while also training more efficiently by removing the pixel decoder entirely.
