Conclusion: Contrastive, infomax-based objectives provide a principled and promising direction for building world models robust to visual nuisance factors. This approach is general and makes minimal assumptions beyond access to state-action sequences and future observations, making it particularly relevant for transferring model-based RL agents to real-world settings.

Abstract: World models trained via pixel reconstruction can struggle in visually complex environments, where irrelevant information dominates the objective and distract the model from information relevant to planning and control. We present Contrastive World Models, an approach for learning latent dynamics models without pixel reconstruction. Building on Dreamer, we replace observation reconstruction in the standard world model objective with a Deep InfoMax-like lower bound that maximizes the mutual information between state-action sequences and local patch features of future observations, encouraging state representations to retain information that is predictive of the future without requiring the model to reconstruct visually irrelevant details. We evaluate our approach in small-scale experiments across three settings of increasing visual complexity. Our method matches Dreamer and a momentum prediction baseline in the default setting, and substantially outperforms both once distractors or natural video backgrounds are introduced, while also training more efficiently by removing the pixel decoder entirely. Our approach is general and makes minimal assumptions beyond access to state-action sequences and future observations. These results suggest that contrastive, infomax-based objectives are a principled and promising direction for building world models that are robust to visual nuisance factors, a property particularly relevant for transferring model-based RL agents to the real world.

</details>


### [72] [OpenBlock: Constructive and Verified Content Generation for Adaptive Tile-Matching Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22177)
*Jiang Jun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This paper presents an adaptive tile-matching puzzle game platform with a dual-track content-generation architecture: a deterministic rule-based generator and an optional neural generator, both verified for full placeability. A self-play RL agent identifies that late-game failure is driven by board-state degeneration (not content difficulty), with 33-56% of long-bar pieces having no legal placement at 70-75% board fill. Online deployment with 48,000 players showed 1.8pp improvement in day-1 retention and 7% longer session duration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tile-matching puzzle games have hundreds of millions of players, but content-generation algorithms remain proprietary with no open platform for studying adaptive difficulty. The authors aimed to create an open platform and address the dominant failure mode in these games.

Method: The authors developed a dual-track content-generation architecture with: (1) a deterministic rule-based generator, and (2) an optional learned generator—both subject to a verification gate ensuring full placeability. They used a self-play reinforcement learning placement agent supervised by auxiliary tasks exposing per-shape placeability. Methods included: exhaustive sequential-placement search for verification, controlled simulation, and a 14-day online gray rollout with 48,000 players (CUPED-adjusted analysis).

Result: The RL agent achieved 35.6% win rate (median score 4,200) across 234,000+ self-play episodes. At 70-75% board fill, 33-56% of long-bar pieces have no legal placement. Spawn difficulty distributions were statistically indistinguishable between won and lost games, indicating board-state degeneration (not content difficulty) drives late-game failure. Per-shape placeability supervision (not aggregate difficulty features) drove representation gains. Online: day-1 retention increased by 1.8 percentage points, session duration increased by 7%.

Conclusion: The neural track provides asymmetric upside in live play: it maintains constructive-feasibility guarantees while improving retention and engagement. The dominant failure mode is board-state degeneration rather than content difficulty, specifically related to long-bar piece placeability at high board fill. Per-shape placeability supervision is crucial for the learned generator's effectiveness.

Abstract: Tile-matching puzzle games serve hundreds of millions of players, yet the content-generation algorithms that decide which pieces to present at each turn remain proprietary, and no open platform exists for studying adaptive difficulty in this genre. We present an adaptive tile-matching platform whose central algorithmic contribution is a dual-track content-generation architecture: a deterministic rule-based generator that is always available, and an optional learned generator, both subject to a common verification gate that establishes, by exhaustive sequential-placement search, that every delivered piece set is fully placeable so the learned track can never degrade the constructive-feasibility guarantee of the rule track. A self-play reinforcement-learning placement agent, supervised by auxiliary tasks that expose per-shape placeability to shared representations, is used to diagnose the game's dominant failure mode: at high board fill, long-bar pieces lose the majority of their legal placements. Across 234,000+ self-play episodes the agent reaches a 35.6\% win rate (median score 4,200), and controlled simulation shows that at board fill rates of 70--75\%, 33--56\% of long-bar pieces have no legal placement, while spawn difficulty distributions are statistically indistinguishable between won and lost games---evidence that board-state degeneration, not content difficulty, drives late-game failure. Head-to-head ablations show that per-shape placeability supervision---not aggregate difficulty features---drives the representation gain, and a 14-day online gray rollout (48,000 players; sample-ratio verified, CUPED-adjusted) lifts day-1 retention by 1.8 percentage points and session duration by 7\% over the rule track alone, quantifying the neural track's asymmetric upside in live play.

</details>


<div id='cs.SD'></div>

# cs.SD [[Back]](#toc)

### [73] [Causal limits and optimal allocation for passive frequency-selective hearing protection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22395)
*Geonhwi Hwang,Jaewoo Lee,Hyunjun Kim,Hyeonwoo Na*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper presents an optimization framework for passive Helmholtz-resonator earplugs that balance noise attenuation with speech audibility. The finite cavity volume of ear-sized shells is treated as a design resource, with a conserved integral of transmission loss derived from causality constraints. The method uses hazard-weighted water-filling with a speech-cost price to allocate the audio-band budget.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Passive earplugs must attenuate hazardous noise while maintaining speech audibility. The challenge is optimizing the limited air volume available in ear-sized shells to achieve both goals. Previous approaches lacked a systematic framework for allocating this finite resource between noise protection and speech transparency.

Method: The authors derive a conserved integral of transmission loss from causality constraints for a vented bore with reactive side branches. They incorporate viscous and flanking constraints, then allocate the budget using hazard-weighted water-filling with a continuous speech-cost price. The audio-band budget formula B_ad = (20/ln10) π²V/2cS relates cavity volume V to bore area S. They tested three form factors with different frequency selection strategies.

Result: The audio-band budget is determined by the ratio of cavity volume to bore area. The f² price signal favors frequencies above the source peak. The exchange rate is approximately 1.3 dB of protection per decibel of speech cost. Low-frequency-selective designs achieve ~1 dB protection, while hazard-matched mid/high-frequency designs reach ~12 dB. The binding constraints are bore area and speech allowance, not resonator count.

Conclusion: The optimization framework shows that bore area and speech allowance are the limiting factors in earplug design, not the number of resonators. The speech-cost price approach provides a practical design tool that quantifies the trade-off between protection and speech audibility. This enables systematic design of earplugs matched to specific noise hazards while maintaining speech communication.

Abstract: Passive Helmholtz-resonator earplugs aim to attenuate hazardous noise while keeping speech audible; the air volume of an ear-sized shell is finite, and we treat it as the resource a design spends. For a vented bore transparent at zero frequency and loaded by reactive side branches, causality fixes a conserved integral of transmission loss. We derive it in device variables, add viscous and flanking constraints, and allocate the budget by hazard-weighted water-filling with a continuous speech-cost price. The audio-band budget B_ad = (20/ln10) pi^2 V / 2cS is set by the ratio of total cavity volume V to bore area S; occluding plugs are exempt. The f^2 price signal favours frequencies well above the source peak. Replacing strict transparency by a speech-cost price gives an exchange rate of about 1.3 dB of protection per decibel of speech cost under the present model, with the budget setting where it saturates. Across three form factors, low-frequency-selective designs stay near a decibel while hazard-matched mid/high-frequency designs reach twelve. The binding variables are bore area and speech allowance, not resonator count.

</details>


### [74] [SEABED: SouthEast Asian Benchmark for Evaluating Audio Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22586)
*Harshit Rajgarhia,Asif Shaik,Rachuri Lokesh,Sushanta Kumar Pani,Abhishek Mukherji*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: SEABED is a new benchmark dataset for evaluating audio-language models' reasoning capabilities on Southeast Asian (SEA) speech, comprising 5,404 question-answer pairs across 6 audio-reasoning tasks. Current state-of-the-art models achieve only ~50% accuracy, highlighting the need for better audio reasoning evaluation in non-English, non-general-domain contexts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing audio-reasoning benchmarks are English-focused and test recognition rather than true reasoning over SEA speech. The paper addresses the gap in evaluating whether models can actually reason from SEA audio rather than just transcribe it.

Method: The authors created SEABED by building six audio-reasoning tasks from real, openly available SEA speech corpora. They evaluated six frontier and region-specific audio LLMs on this benchmark, assessing both answer accuracy and whether models' reasoning is grounded in audio evidence.

Result: Even the best model (Gemini 3.5 Flash) achieves only 50.3% weighted average accuracy on SEABED. This indicates significant room for improvement in audio reasoning for SEA languages.

Conclusion: SEABED provides a valuable benchmark for evaluating audio reasoning in non-English, non-general-domain contexts. The low baseline performance demonstrates the need for further development of audio-language models that can truly reason over Southeast Asian speech.

Abstract: Modern audio-language models are no longer judged only on what words they can transcribe, but on whether they can reason over what they hear: recovering meaning that lives in tone and prosody, telling dialects and regional languages apart, and resolving ambiguity that the written form leaves open. This capability is now measured by a growing family of audio-reasoning benchmarks, but almost entirely in English and on general-domain audio. Southeast Asia (SEA) is served instead by benchmarks that inherit an English task taxonomy of recognition, translation, and paralinguistic classification, and therefore test whether a model hears SEA speech rather than whether it can reason from it. We introduce SEABED, an audio-first question answering dataset designed to benchmark language and audio reasoning models on SEA speech. SEABED comprises a suite of six audio-reasoning tasks built entirely from real, openly available SEA speech corpora, yielding 5,404 question-answer pairs. We evaluate six frontier and region-specific audio LLMs: even the state-of-the-art model Gemini 3.5 Flash achieves only 50.3% weighted average accuracy. SEABED evaluates not only answer accuracy, but also whether models' stated reasoning is grounded in the audio evidence. A sample of the benchmark data is available here: https://huggingface.co/datasets/CentificAIResearch/SEABED.

</details>


### [75] [ParA-LLM: A Unified Approach to Paralinguistic and Acoustic Speech Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22771)
*Nishit Anand,Jiaqi Su,Ke Chen,Yunyun Wang,Dinesh Manocha,Ramani Duraiswami,Rithesh Kumar,Zeyu Jin*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces ParA-LLM, a framework for Audio LLMs to capture paralinguistic characteristics (speaker traits, expressive variations, acoustic conditions). It proposes a framework of 22 paralinguistic characteristics, a dataset of 1.2M Audio-QA pairs, and a benchmark (ParA-Bench) with 6,000 questions. ParA-LLM uses a two-stage curriculum learning approach and outperforms GPT-4o-Audio by 7.5% on ParA-Bench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current Audio LLMs achieve human-level speech recognition but fail to capture paralinguistic aspects like speaker traits, expressive variations, and environmental acoustic conditions, limiting their ability to understand real-world audio.

Method: The authors designed a framework of 22 paralinguistic characteristics and created a dataset of over 1.2M Audio-QA pairs. They developed ParA-LLM trained with a two-stage curriculum: first on single-attribute questions to build foundational knowledge, then on multi-attribute questions for joint reasoning. They also released ParA-Bench, a benchmark with 6,000 multiple-choice questions across speaker-speech, acoustic, and mixed categories.

Result: ParA-LLM surpasses state-of-the-art Audio LLMs like GPT-4o-Audio by 7.5% on ParA-Bench, with additional gains of 1.13% on MMAU-Pro Speech and 7.49% on MMAR Speech. Frontier models like GPT-4o-Audio achieve only 36% accuracy on ParA-Bench.

Conclusion: The two-stage curriculum learning approach effectively enables Audio LLMs to jointly reason over speaker and acoustic characteristics, significantly improving paralinguistic understanding. The introduced benchmark and dataset provide valuable resources for future research in this direction.

Abstract: Recent advances in Audio LLMs have achieved human-level speech recognition, yet existing systems struggle to capture paralinguistic aspects such as speaker traits, expressive variations, and environmental acoustic conditions. To address this, we design a framework of 22 paralinguistic characteristics and create a dataset of over 1.2M Audio-QA pairs. We develop ParA-LLM, trained with a two-stage curriculum: first on single-attribute questions to build foundational knowledge, then on multi-attribute questions for joint reasoning over speaker and acoustic characteristics. We also release ParA-Bench, a benchmark of 6,000 multiple-choice questions across speaker-speech, acoustic, and mixed categories, where frontier models like GPT-4o-Audio achieve only 36% accuracy. ParA-LLM surpasses state-of-the-art Audio LLMs like GPT-4o-Audio by 7.5% on ParA-Bench, with additional gains of 1.13% on MMAU-Pro Speech and 7.49% on MMAR Speech.

</details>


### [76] [Discrete vs. Continuous: A Comprehensive Study of Unified Audio Understanding in LALMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22851)
*Jing Peng,Zichao Nie,Zhisheng Zhang,Jingran Xie,Zhiyong Wu ID*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates continuous vs discrete audio representations for Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) across speech, sound, and music domains, using the UniARC framework with models ranging from 135M to 8B parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The field lacks consensus on the optimal representation paradigm (continuous features vs discrete tokens) for general audio understanding in LALMs. Existing benchmarks are narrow in scope and evaluate encoders outside LALM contexts, creating a gap in understanding which representation is superior.

Method: The researchers employed the UniARC framework with dual evaluation strategies, testing models across different scales (SmolLM2-135M to Llama-3-8B). They analyzed the dynamic relationships between data volume, model capacity, and computational efficiency across speech, sound, and music domains.

Result: Key findings: 1) Semantic constraints in tokenization play a pivotal role in audio understanding; 2) Scaling up model backbones cannot compensate for information loss in audio representation; 3) This limitation is particularly pronounced in data-limited tasks.

Conclusion: The study provides practical guidance for balancing semantic density, fidelity, and efficiency in future LALM development. The findings suggest that tokenization design with proper semantic constraints is crucial, and simply increasing model size cannot overcome fundamental limitations in audio representation choices.

Abstract: Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) utilize either continuous features or discrete tokens, yet the optimal representation paradigm for general audio understanding remains debated. Existing benchmarks often focus on narrow domains or evaluate encoders outside LALM contexts. To address these gaps, we systematically evaluate continuous and discrete representations across speech, sound and music. Utilizing our UniARC framework with dual evaluation strategies across model scales from SmolLM2-135M to Llama-3-8B, we analyze the dynamic relationships of data volume, model capacity, and computational efficiency. Our results reveal the pivotal role of semantic constraints in tokenization for audio understanding and demonstrate that scaling backbones fail to compensate for information loss in audio representation, especially in data-limited tasks. These findings offer practical guidance for balancing semantic density, fidelity, and efficiency in future LALMs.

</details>


### [77] [Bearings: Self-Supervised Soundfield Embeddings from First-Order Ambisonics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23152)
*Goksenin Yuksel,Marcel van Gerven,Kiki van der Heijden*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: Bearings is a self-supervised framework that learns soundfield embeddings from first-order Ambisonics to address the spatial blindness of existing self-supervised audio encoders. It uses a masked auto-encoder paired with a decoder conditioned on frozen acoustic embeddings, enabling plug-and-play integration with frozen acoustic encoders for sound event localization and detection tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing self-supervised audio encoders learn powerful general-purpose representations of sound scenes but are spatially blind—they lack spatial information about sound sources. This limits their effectiveness for tasks requiring spatial awareness, such as sound event localization and detection (SELD).

Method: Bearings employs a masked auto-encoder architecture for first-order Ambisonics (FOA) input. The decoder is conditioned on frozen acoustic embeddings from an off-the-shelf single-channel audio encoder. The resulting soundfield embeddings can be attached to frozen acoustic encoders via a lightweight trainable fusion head without requiring retraining of either model.

Result: On sound event localization and detection tasks, concatenating soundfield embeddings with acoustic representations significantly improved performance. On TAU-NIGENS 2021, the location-dependent F-score increased from below 4 to 50. On STARSS23, the F-score reached 39. The embeddings serve as a reusable stream that plugs into frozen acoustic encoders without retraining.

Conclusion: Bearings is the first self-supervised soundfield encoder whose embeddings can plug into frozen acoustic encoders without retraining either model. It successfully provides the missing spatial information needed for joint sound event detection and localization, demonstrating the value of combining acoustic and spatial representations learned through self-supervised pre-training.

Abstract: Recently proposed self-supervised audio encoders learn powerful general-purpose representations of sound scenes, yet they are spatially blind. To supply the missing spatial representation of sound scenes, we introduce Bearings. Bearings is a self-supervised framework that learns soundfield embeddings from unlabeled first-order Ambisonics. We pre-train a masked auto-encoder paired with a decoder conditioned on frozen acoustic embeddings from an off-the-shelf single-channel audio encoder. Our results show that the resulting soundfield embeddings form a reusable stream that can be attached to frozen acoustic encoders with a lightweight trainable fusion head. On sound event localization and detection, concatenating our soundfield embeddings with acoustic representations provides the missing spatial information and enables joint detection and localization, raising the location-dependent F-score from below 4 to 50 on TAU-NIGENS 2021 and 39 on STARSS23. To our knowledge, Bearings is the first self-supervised soundfield encoder whose embeddings plug into frozen acoustic encoders without retraining either model.

</details>


### [78] [EquiSELD: Efficient training of equivariant sound event localization and detection networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23156)
*Goksenin Yuksel,Marcel van Gerven,Kiki van der Heijden*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: EquiSELD is an equivariant attention network for Sound Event Detection and Localization (SELD) that processes First-order Ambisonics (FOA) signals by leveraging O(3) spatial symmetry. It processes FOA as paired streams of O(3)-invariant scalars and equivariant intensity vectors, producing invariant activity magnitude and equivariant DOA outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior attempts to utilize the spatial symmetry of FOA for SELD either learned only approximations through rotation-based augmentation or relied on computationally expensive methods. Prior work also focused exclusively on SO(3) equivariance, leaving the potential of O(3) equivariance unexplored. This work aims to address these limitations by developing an efficient O(3)-equivariant network.

Method: EquiSELD processes FOA as paired streams of O(3)-invariant scalars and equivariant intensity vectors, producing an invariant activity magnitude and an equivariant DOA with a Multi-ACCDOA readout. A matched SO(3)-only variant was designed to compare the impact of O(3) versus SO(3)-equivalence.

Result: EquiSELD outperforms prior equivariant networks on both simulated scenes with measured RIRs and recordings of real-world sound scenes at a fraction of the training cost. It also surpasses non-equivariant SELD networks of similar size on simulated real-world sound scenes and achieves competitive performance on real-world sound scenes.

Conclusion: Incorporating O(3) equivariance (including reflections) provides benefits for SELD tasks. The proposed EquiSELD achieves superior performance with lower computational cost compared to prior approaches, demonstrating the value of full O(3) symmetry utilization in ambisonics-based sound event detection and localization.

Abstract: First-order Ambisonics (FOA) signals exhibit exact O(3) symmetry: The rotation or reflection of the FOA signal modifies the direction of arrival of the sound sources, while preserving the sound sources themselves. Prior attempts to utilize this spatial symmetry of FOA to improve the efficiency and robustness of sound event detection and localization (SELD) systems either learned only an approximation of the symmetry through rotation-based augmentation or relied on computationally expensive methods to integrate equivariance. Furthermore, prior work focused exclusively on SO(3) equivariance , leaving the potential of incorporating O(3) equivariance for SELD tasks unclear. To address these limitations, we developed EquiSELD. This equivariant attention network processes first-order Ambisonics as paired streams of O(3)-invariant scalars and equivariant intensity vectors, producing an invariant activity magnitude and an equivariant DOA with a Multi-ACCDOA readout. To compare the impact of O(3) versus SO(3)-equivariance, we designed a matched SO(3)-only variant. EquiSELD outperforms prior equivariant networks on both simulated scenes with measured RIRs and recordings of real-world sound scenes at a fraction of the training cost. EquiSELD additionally surpasses the performance of non-equivariant SELD networks of a similar size on the simulated real-world sound scenes and achieves competitive performance on the real-world sound scenes.

</details>


### [79] [OmniEcho: Spatial Audio Understanding for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23407)
*Ruixun Liu,Yuxuan Wang,Jiacheng Xie,Yuhuan You,Donghua Cai,Junming Lin,Xiong-Hui Chen,Zhifang Guo,Yunfei Chu,Qize Yang,Xize Cheng,Jin Xu,Yiwu Zhong*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: A benchmark (OmniEchoBench) and model (OmniEcho) for spatial audio-visual perception and sound-guided navigation in embodied agents. The benchmark includes 197 scenes, 2,972 Q&A pairs, and 900 navigation samples with first-order ambisonics audio. The model achieves SOTA on spatial audio-visual perception and demonstrates spatial audio's value for embodied reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humans effortlessly localize sound sources and integrate with visual cues, but embodied agents struggle with this task. There is a lack of effective evaluation methods and models for spatial audio understanding in embodied settings.

Method: 1) Developed OmniEchoBench with six tasks over 197 real-world spatial audio-visual scenes, 2,972 Q&A pairs, and 900 navigation samples using first-order ambisonics (FOA) audio from 30 environments. 2) Created a controllable rendering pipeline preserving geometric consistency among sound sources, visual observations, and agent trajectories. 3) Proposed OmniEcho model with an FOA spatial encoder alongside a pretrained semantic audio pathway.

Result: OmniEcho achieves state-of-the-art performance on spatial audio-visual perception. For sound-guided navigation, OmniEcho reaches performance close to traditional vision-language navigation.

Conclusion: Spatial audio serves as a valuable signal for embodied scene reasoning and navigation. Important open challenges remain: fine-grained spatial localization and distance estimation.

Abstract: Humans can effortlessly localize the direction of a sound source and integrate it with visual cues for reasoning, yet this remains challenging for embodied agents. In particular, it is still unclear how to effectively evaluate and model spatial audio understanding in embodied settings. To address this gap, we introduce \textbf{OmniEchoBench}, a unified benchmark for spatial audio-visual perception and audio-vision-language navigation. OmniEchoBench comprises six tasks over 197 real-world spatial audio-visual scenes, 2,972 question-answer pairs, and 900 navigation samples with first-order ambisonics (FOA) audio collected from 30 real-world environments. To enable scalable training supervision, we develop a controllable rendering pipeline for spatial audio. It preserves geometric consistency among sound sources, visual observations, and agent trajectories. Building on this, we propose \textbf{OmniEcho}, a spatially aware omni-modal model. It introduces an FOA spatial encoder alongside a pretrained semantic audio pathway. Extensive experiments show that OmniEcho achieves state-of-the-art performance on spatial audio-visual perception. For our sound-guided navigation, OmniEcho reaches a performance level close to that of traditional vision-language navigation. These results demonstrate that spatial audio can serve as a valuable signal for embodied scene reasoning and navigation, while also highlighting fine-grained spatial localization and distance estimation as important open challenges.

</details>


### [80] [Misrecognition or Abstraction? Rethinking Outputs of Sound Event Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23411)
*Naoya Tomida,Yuki Okamoto,Keisuke Imoto*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes a new output representation for sound event recognition that combines traditional class labels with confidence scores and onomatopoeic descriptions. This addresses the limitation of conventional systems that assume sounds can always be clearly identified, whereas real-world sounds are often ambiguous.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional sound recognition systems output deterministic labels, assuming the sound class can always be correctly identified. However, in real listening situations, sound event classes are often ambiguous. Humans can understand their surroundings from ambiguous sounds without identifying the exact class, motivating a redesign of system outputs to handle this uncertainty.

Method: The proposed method combines three components: (1) sound event class, (2) confidence score, and (3) onomatopoeic description of the sound. This hybrid representation preserves conventional class-based recognition while providing additional onomatopoeic descriptions of acoustic characteristics that remain informative even when class prediction is uncertain.

Result: Experiments on ESC-50 and ESC-50-Onomatopoeia datasets show: (1) comparable sound recognition performance to conventional recognition-only systems; (2) LLM-as-a-judge evaluation and subjective listening experiments indicate the proposed output is preferred over conventional deterministic outputs, particularly for supporting environmental understanding.

Conclusion: The proposed output representation makes sound event recognition more informative and communicative under uncertainty. By adding onomatopoeic descriptions alongside class labels and confidence scores, the system can provide useful information even when class prediction is uncertain, better supporting human understanding of surrounding environments.

Abstract: Conventional general sound recognition systems typically output deterministic sound event labels, implicitly assuming that the target sound class can be correctly identified from the input audio. However, in real listening situations, the sound event class is not always clearly identifiable. Human listeners may nevertheless understand their surroundings from an ambiguous sound without identifying its exact sound event class. This motivates a discussion of how the outputs of sound recognition systems should be redesigned under such uncertainty. As a basis for this discussion, this paper proposes an output representation for sound event recognition that combines a sound event class, its confidence score, and an onomatopoeic description of the sound. The proposed representation preserves conventional class-based recognition while providing an additional onomatopoeic description of acoustic characteristics that can remain informative even when the class prediction is uncertain. Experiments using ESC-50 and ESC-50-Onomatopoeia show that the proposed method achieves sound recognition performance comparable to that of a conventional recognition-only system. In addition, an LLM-as-a-judge evaluation and subjective listening experiments indicate that the proposed output is preferred over conventional deterministic outputs based on the sound event label, particularly when used to support understanding of the surrounding environment. These results suggest that such output representations can make sound event recognition more informative and communicative under uncertainty.

</details>


### [81] [MuLA-Bench: A Multilingual Long-Form Audio Understanding Benchmark via Multi-Tier Auditing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23416)
*Zeyu Yang,Xinyu Zhang,Zibo Bi,Pei Zhang,Xize Cheng,Jin Xu,Baosong Yang,Satoshi Nakamura*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MuLA-Bench is a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating long-form audio-language models, featuring 5,038 open-ended questions across 1,769 recordings (1,377.9 hours) covering 16 languages and 8 domains. It reveals that single long-context metrics fail to capture conditional failure patterns in audio-language models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluation metrics (context length and aggregate accuracy) obscure how language, evidence, and task jointly shape difficulty in long-form audio performance, limiting understanding of model capabilities and limitations.

Method: Created a benchmark with two tracks: (1) balanced Language x Domain semantic track for controlled comparisons, and (2) acoustic track preserving naturally occurring non-speech evidence. Used evidence-grounded generation, shortcut checks, and language-expert review to ensure auditable questions without translation or injected sounds. Evaluated ten audio-language models with pooled diagnostics on an eight-model cohort.

Result: Language rankings change across domains and tasks; acoustic-semantic performance gaps vary with requested operation; temporal errors persist after correct event identification. Long-range retrieval is comparatively strong, while precise clock alignment and factual grounding of natural acoustic events remain fragile.

Conclusion: MuLA-Bench exposes conditional failure patterns that a single long-context score does not capture, highlighting the need for multi-dimensional evaluation of audio-language models across language, domain, and task dimensions.

Abstract: Long-form audio performance is often summarized by context length and aggregate accuracy, obscuring how language, evidence, and task jointly shape difficulty. We introduce MuLA-Bench: 5,038 open-ended questions over 1,769 in-the-wild recordings totaling 1,377.9 hours, covering 16 languages and eight domains. A balanced Language x Domain semantic track supports controlled comparisons, while a complementary acoustic track preserves naturally occurring non-speech evidence. Evidence-grounded generation, shortcut checks, and language-expert review provide auditable questions without translating a shared source set or injecting target sounds. We evaluate ten audio-language models and conduct pooled diagnostics on a fixed eight-model cohort. Language rankings change across domains and tasks; acoustic-semantic performance gaps vary with the requested operation; and temporal errors can persist after the correct event is identified. Long-range retrieval is comparatively strong, while precise clock alignment and factual grounding of natural acoustic events remain fragile. MuLA-Bench thus exposes conditional failure patterns that a single long-context score does not capture.

</details>


### [82] [LiteCASS: A Lightweight End-to-End Network for Real-Time Stereo Cinematic Audio Source Separation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23453)
*Yuanxin Guo,Qiang Ji,Mengmei Liu,Yuhan Lv,Ningning Pan,Gongping Huang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: LiteCASS is the first lightweight end-to-end network for real-time stereo cinematic audio source separation, using only 1.06M parameters and 0.72G MACs/s while achieving highest averaged SI-SDR among baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing CASS methods have two critical limitations: 1) reliance on heavily parameterized networks and GPU-class hardware, making them unsuitable for real-time and resource-constrained scenarios, and 2) overwhelming focus on monaural signals, leaving stereo scenarios largely unexplored.

Method: LiteCASS combines deterministic STFT subband rearrangement with two jointly trained compact U-Nets: the first extracts dialogue, and the second separates music and SFX from the predicted non-speech component. A multi-task waveform-domain L1 loss supervises all stems.

Result: LiteCASS-K8 achieves the highest averaged SI-SDR among compared CASS baselines while using only 1.06M parameters and 0.72G MACs per second.

Conclusion: LiteCASS demonstrates that efficient real-time stereo CASS is achievable with compact neural networks, addressing both the computational constraints and stereo processing gaps in prior work.

Abstract: Cinematic audio source separation (CASS) decomposes a soundtrack into dialogue, music, and sound-effects (SFX) stems. Existing CASS methods, however, suffer from two critical limitations: they rely on heavily parameterized network architectures and GPU-class hardware, limiting their use in real-time and resource-constrained scenarios, and they are overwhelmingly designed for monaural signals, leaving the stereo scenario largely unexplored. We present LiteCASS, to our knowledge the first lightweight end-to-end network for real-time stereo CASS. LiteCASS combines deterministic STFT subband rearrangement with two jointly trained compact U-Nets: the first extracts dialogue, and the second separates music and SFX from the predicted non-speech component. A multi-task waveform-domain L1 loss supervises all stems. On a spatialized stereo extension of DnR v3, LiteCASS-K8 uses only 1.06M parameters and 0.72G MACs per second, while achieving the highest averaged SI-SDR among the compared CASS baselines.

</details>


### [83] [Beyond Encoder Fusion: Multi-View Discrete Token Augmentation for LLM-Based ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23525)
*Paul Moïse Gangbadja,Mickael Rouvier,Fabrice Lefèvre*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes multi-view discrete token augmentation to improve speech recognition by training an LLM decoder on diverse token sequences from multiple SSL encoders (HuBERT, WavLM, MMS-300M), while only requiring a single encoder at inference time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discrete speech token-based ASR systems are sensitive to the choice of encoder. Single-tokenization approaches lack robustness because they depend heavily on one specific encoder's representation.

Method: Multi-view discrete token augmentation: augment each training utterance with alternative token sequences from fixed SSL encoders (HuBERT, WavLM, MMS-300M). These multiple tokenizations serve as complementary training views for a shared LLM decoder, exposing it to more diverse discrete speech representations without requiring multi-encoder inference. At test time, only a single encoder is needed.

Result: On LibriSpeech, the approach consistently improves all encoders over baselines. WavLM achieves 3.30% WER on test-clean and 8.13% on test-other. ROVER over multi-view hypotheses further improves results to 3.03% and 7.38%. Budget-matched controls confirm gains come from encoder diversity rather than data volume.

Conclusion: Multi-view discrete token augmentation effectively improves ASR performance by leveraging encoder diversity during training while maintaining single-encoder inference. The approach is encoder-agnostic and provides consistent improvements across different SSL encoders.

Abstract: Discrete speech tokens provide a compact interface between speech encoders and large language models for automatic speech recognition, but single-tokenization systems remain sensitive to the chosen encoder. We propose multi-view discrete token augmentation, a simple strategy that augments each training utterance by generating alternative token sequences from fixed SSL encoders, such as HuBERT, WavLM, and MMS-300M. These tokenizations are treated as complementary training views for a shared LLM decoder, exposing it to more diverse discrete speech representations without requiring multi-encoder inference. At test time, the model can operate with a single encoder. On LibriSpeech, the approach consistently improves all encoders over independently trained baselines, with WavLM reaching 3.30% WER on test-clean and 8.13% on test-other. Budget-matched controls show that the gains come from encoder diversity rather than data volume. ROVER over multi-view hypotheses further improves WER to 3.03% and 7.38%.

</details>


### [84] [ArtifactBench: Lineage-Aware Evaluation of AI-Generated Music Detectors under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23550)
*Heewon Oh*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: ArtifactBench is a lineage-aware evaluation suite for AI-generated music detectors that addresses limitations in current benchmark practices by tracking generator families, versions, real-music domains, and inference coverage. It groups source recordings by content identity, separates calibration from testing, and reports uncertainty in addition to aggregate metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI-generated music detectors are compared using aggregate scores on benchmarks where training overlap, generator lineage, source provenance, and audio-transformation history are only partially observable. This limits understanding of detector behavior across different conditions.

Method: ArtifactBench evaluates detectors across generator families and versions, real-music domains, collection-cohort shift, and inference coverage. It groups source recordings and derived variants by content identity, separates calibration from final testing, records inference failures independently from classification errors, and reports source-level performance with uncertainty alongside aggregate metrics. Multiple publicly available detectors were evaluated under a version-pinned common protocol.

Result: On the 562-track common-success test intersection, ArtifactNet achieved 0.982 AUROC and 0.918 balanced accuracy, while the public Deezer detector obtained 0.761/0.776. SpecTTTra and CLAM fell below 0.30 AUROC. The results exposed substantial generator- and real-domain shifts that aggregate scores alone conceal.

Conclusion: The evaluation reveals significant performance variations across generators and real domains that are hidden by aggregate metrics. ArtifactBench provides a more comprehensive assessment by incorporating lineage-awareness, uncertainty quantification, and separate reporting of inference failures.

Abstract: AI-generated music detectors are commonly compared using aggregate scores on benchmarks whose training overlap, generator lineage, source provenance, and audio-transformation history are only partially observable. This paper introduces ArtifactBench, a lineage-aware evaluation suite for measuring detector behavior across generator families and versions, real-music domains, collection-cohort shift, and inference coverage. The benchmark groups source recordings and their derived variants by content identity, separates calibration from final testing, records inference failures independently from classification errors, and reports source-level performance with uncertainty in addition to aggregate metrics. We evaluate multiple publicly available detectors under a version-pinned common protocol and examine how leakage control, cohort availability, threshold policy, and model-specific missingness alter measured performance and model ranking. On the 562-track common-success test intersection, ArtifactNet obtains 0.982 AUROC and 0.918 balanced accuracy, compared with 0.761/0.776 for the public Deezer detector; SpecTTTra and CLAM fall below 0.30 AUROC under this shifted cohort. These results also expose substantial generator- and real-domain shifts that aggregate scores alone conceal.

</details>


### [85] [Listen Then Reason: Perception-Grounded Test-Time Reinforcement Learning for Large Audio-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23589)
*Jiaheng Dong,Xiaofeng Yu,Jean Honorio,Abhirup Ghosh,Hong Jia,Ting Dang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper addresses a gap in understanding how large audio-language models (LALMs) integrate acoustic evidence during reasoning, and proposes Perception-Grounded TTRL (PG-TTRL) to align test-time optimization with perceptually grounded reasoning. The method encourages models to rely more heavily on audio input during reasoning, demonstrating consistent improvements across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address the underexplored issue of perceptual capability in LALMs - specifically how much acoustic evidence is integrated and relied upon during reasoning, and how this impacts task performance. This gap limits the development of effective post-training methods like Test-Time Reinforcement Learning (TTRL) for audio reasoning tasks.

Method: The authors first analyze how audio information is integrated and utilized during the reasoning process by quantifying layer-wise perceptual reliance. They then propose Perception-Grounded TTRL (PG-TTRL), which aligns label-free test-time optimization with perceptually grounded reasoning, encouraging the model to structure its reasoning more strongly on the audio input.

Result: Experiments across multiple LALMs and benchmarks show that PG-TTRL consistently improves reasoning performance over both base models and standard TTRL. The study also demonstrates that stronger acoustic reliance is associated with higher accuracy and a larger performance gain attributable to the audio input.

Conclusion: Perceptual-grounding optimization is valuable for test-time audio reasoning. The proposed PG-TTRL method successfully encourages models to leverage audio input more effectively during reasoning, leading to improved performance across various benchmarks and LALM architectures.

Abstract: Large audio-language models (LALMs) are increasingly used for a broader range of audio reasoning tasks. These models typically incorporate audio representations into a large language model (LLM) backbone to enable multimodal reasoning. Recent test-time reinforcement learning (TTRL) methods further improve LLM reasoning capability by leveraging unlabelled test data after pre-training. However, the importance of the perceptual capability of LALMs remains underexplored, particularly how much acoustic evidence is integrated and relied upon during reasoning, and how this contributes to final task performance. This gap limits the development of effective post-training methods like TTRL for audio reasoning. In this work, we first analyse how audio information is integrated and utilised during reasoning process. We quantify layer-wise perceptual reliance and show that stronger acoustic reliance is associated with higher accuracy and a larger performance gain attributable to the audio input. Building on this, we propose Perception-Grounded TTRL (PG-TTRL), which aligns label-free test-time optimisation with perceptually grounded reasoning, encouraging the model to structure its reasoning more strongly on the audio input. Experiments across LALMs and benchmarks show that PG-TTRL consistently improves reasoning performance over both the base models and standard TTRL, showing the value of perceptual-grounding optimisation for test-time audio reasoning.

</details>


### [86] [Which Constraints Are Missing? Ask the Verifier: Graded Rewards for Constraint-Following Music Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23665)
*Haoyue Liu,Ye Chen,Zhichao Wang,Xiaoyu Ma,Haoran Shou,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper introduces MusicConstraintBench, a benchmark for evaluating constraint-following music generation, and MusicRLVR, a reinforcement learning method that uses graded per-property rewards with a validation gate to improve model performance on multi-constraint music generation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No existing benchmark isolates the capability of generating music that satisfies multiple user-specified properties (key, meter, length, range, final note, rhythm, motion, and form) simultaneously. Current models fail when constraints are combined, and existing reinforcement learning approaches with binary rewards leave most training groups without a learning signal.

Method: The authors construct MusicConstraintBench with 2,180 items across eight constraint families. They propose MusicRLVR, which uses: (1) a hard validation gate that rejects malformed outputs, (2) graded per-property credit for partial satisfaction, and (3) a joint-satisfaction bonus. This requires no human annotation, learned reward model, or music-domain fine-tuning.

Result: MusicRLVR lifts Qwen3-4B-Instruct from 0.160 to 0.807 on mixed constraints, outperforming all zero-shot baselines including Llama-3.1-70B (0.380). It generalizes to property combinations unseen in training and to out-of-range parameter values.

Conclusion: Verifiable rewards without presupposing a target output can effectively train models for multi-constraint music generation, achieving strong performance and generalization capabilities.

Abstract: Constraint-following music generation asks a score to satisfy several user-specified properties at once, each checkable programmatically (key, meter, length, range, final note, rhythm, motion and form), yet no existing benchmark isolates this capability. We construct MusicConstraintBench, 2,180 items over eight constraint families, on which current models fail once a few constraints are combined. The natural remedy is reinforcement learning with these verifiers as reward, yet we observe that a reward paid only when every property holds leaves most training groups without a learning signal: over the first 50 updates, 0.550 of rollout groups score identically and receive no gradient, even though a failing score typically misses only one requested property. Under the joint criterion, rollouts for a prompt tend to fail together, so a binary reward cannot separate a nearly correct score from a malformed one. We therefore introduce MusicRLVR, which pays graded per-property credit behind a hard validation gate that rejects malformed outputs, plus a joint-satisfaction bonus, requiring no human annotation, learned reward model, or music-domain fine-tuning. On MusicConstraintBench, MusicRLVR lifts Qwen3-4B-Instruct from 0.160 to 0.807 on mixed constraints and leads every zero-shot baseline including Llama-3.1-70B at 0.380. It also generalises to property combinations unseen in training and to out-of-range parameter values, showing that verifiable rewards need not presuppose a target output.

</details>


### [87] [Entropy-aware logistic regression for fusion of large-scale speaker recognition systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23727)
*Pierre-Michel Bousquet,Mickael Rouvier*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes an uncertainty-aware score-level fusion method for speaker recognition that incorporates entropy-based uncertainty measures from deep learning models into logistic regression fusion, achieving robust performance in large-scale scenarios with variable speech characteristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional score-level fusion using logistic regression assigns fixed system-dependent coefficients and fails to account for variations in the reliability of individual enrollment and test utterances, which limits performance in large-scale speaker recognition tasks with highly variable speech signals.

Method: The proposed method incorporates an uncertainty component into the fusion process by exploiting the entropy of deep learning-based speaker recognition models. This approach combines system-level complementarity with utterance-dependent uncertainty information.

Result: The method achieves robust performance in large-scale speaker recognition tasks involving highly variable characteristics of speech signals, demonstrating that model-entropy information provides a valuable complementary cue.

Conclusion: Model-entropy information serves as a valuable complementary cue for score-level fusion in large-scale speaker recognition scenarios, enabling more reliable fusion by accounting for utterance-dependent reliability variations.

Abstract: Score-level fusion based on logistic regression is widely used in speaker recognition to combine complementary systems. However, conventional approaches assign fixed system-dependent coefficients and do not explicitly account for variations in the reliability of individual enrollment and test utterances. Drawing on recent research on the entropy of deep learning-based speaker recognition models, this study incorporates an uncertainty component into the fusion process. By exploiting both system-level complementarity and utterance-dependent uncertainty, the method achieves robust performance in large-scale speaker recognition tasks that involve highly variable characteristics of the speech signal. These results demonstrate that model-entropy information provides a valuable complementary cue in large-scale scenarios.

</details>


### [88] [TTS-Guard: Black-Box Ownership Verification of Text-to-Speech Models via Adaptive Adversarial Speaker-Pair Fingerprints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23729)
*Xubin Yue,Zhenhua Xu,Zhebo Wang,Mengting Li,Zijie Zhou,Wenpeng Xing,Dezhang Kong,Meng Han*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: TTS-Guard is a black-box ownership verification framework for zero-shot Text-to-Speech (TTS) models that uses adversarial speaker-pair fingerprints to detect unauthorized replication, fine-tuning, and resale of proprietary speech models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid advancement of zero-shot TTS models has made high-quality voice cloning widely accessible, creating concerns about unauthorized use. However, ownership verification for TTS remains challenging because speech is continuous waveforms easily perturbed by signal processing, and the human auditory system has tighter perceptual constraints than vision.

Method: TTS-Guard uses three key components: (i) selecting key speaker pairs in a dual embedding space for architecture-agnostic stealth; (ii) optimizing perturbations through an adaptive curriculum of shadow models covering fine-tuning, pruning, quantization, and distillation; (iii) aggregating black-box queries into a calibrated Verification Confidence Score.

Result: TTS-Guard achieves an average Fingerprint Success Rate of 96.4% at a False Positive Rate of 5.8% across five mainstream TTS systems, while preserving intelligibility and naturalness. The fingerprint remains effective against ten audio attacks, six model modifications, and two state-of-the-art adversarial purifiers.

Conclusion: TTS-Guard provides a practical solution for TTS model ownership verification, demonstrating robustness against various attacks and model modifications while maintaining audio quality.

Abstract: The rapid maturation of zero-shot Text-to-Speech (TTS) models has turned high-quality voice cloning into a widely available capability, raising acute concerns over unauthorised replication, fine-tuning and resale of proprietary speech models. Yet ownership verification for TTS remains largely open: speech is a continuous waveform whose perturbations are easily destroyed by routine signal processing, and the human auditory system imposes a much tighter perceptual budget than vision. We present \textbf{TTS-Guard}, a black-box ownership verification framework for TTS models built on \emph{adversarial speaker-pair fingerprints}. TTS-Guard(i) selects key speaker pairs in a \emph{dual} embedding space for architecture-agnostic stealth;(ii) optimises a perturbation through an \emph{adaptive curriculum} of shadow models covering fine-tuning, pruning, quantisation and distillation; and (iii) aggregates black-box queries into a calibrated \emph{Verification Confidence Score}. On five mainstream TTS systems, TTS-Guard reaches an average Fingerprint Success Rate of $96.4\%$ at a False Positive Rate of $5.8\%$, while preserving intelligibility and naturalness. The fingerprint remains effective against ten audio attacks, six model modifications, and two state-of-the-art adversarial purifiers.

</details>


### [89] [ART-NAD: An Articulatory Inversion-based Neural Acoustic Distance for Pathological Speech Intelligibility Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24046)
*Bence Mark Halpern,Thomas Tienkamp,Defne Abur,Tomoki Toda*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: ART-NAD is a reference-audio intelligibility metric for pathological speech that uses vocal-tract constriction variables (TVs) instead of self-supervised features, achieving the same correlation (r=0.71) as NAD while providing interpretable visualizations of where articulation breaks down.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing reference-audio measures like NAD achieve high correlation with listener intelligibility scores but operate on hard-to-interpret self-supervised features, limiting their clinical adoption for speech pathology assessment.

Method: ART-NAD replaces wav2vec2 features of NAD with vocal-tract constriction variables (tract variables, TVs) predicted from audio by a speaker-independent acoustic-to-articulatory inversion model trained on wav2vec2 features. It computes the multivariate Dynamic Time Warping distance between nine-channel quasi-TV trajectories of test and reference audio, with silence trimming (ART-NAD-FA).

Result: Across 20 reference-audio protocols spanning six pathological-speech datasets and five languages, ART-NAD-FA achieves the same average speaker-level Pearson correlation as NAD-FA (both r=0.71), with no significant per-protocol difference (Wilcoxon p=0.18). It is the strongest reference-audio metric on 6 of the 20 protocols.

Conclusion: ART-NAD provides interpretable information about which constriction deviates from the reference over time through each TV channel visualization, while maintaining comparable correlation with listener intelligibility scores. This approach bridges the gap between accuracy and interpretability in clinical speech assessment tools.

Abstract: Speech assessment tools for speakers with speech pathology must be both accurate and interpretable if they are to be adopted in clinical practice. Existing reference-audio measures such as the Neural Acoustic Distance (NAD) reach high speaker-level correlations with listener intelligibility scores but operate on self-supervised features that are hard to interpret, providing only frame-level explanations. We propose ART-NAD, a reference-audio intelligibility metric that replaces the \texttt{wav2vec2} features of NAD with vocal-tract constriction variables (tract variables, TVs) predicted from audio by a speaker-independent acoustic-to-articulatory inversion model trained on the same \texttt{wav2vec2} features. ART-NAD is computed as the multivariate Dynamic Time Warping distance between the nine-channel quasi-TV trajectories of the test and one or more references. Across 20 reference-audio protocols spanning six pathological-speech datasets and five languages, ART-NAD with silence trimming (ART-NAD-FA) reaches the same average speaker-level Pearson correlation as NAD-FA (both $r=0.71$) on the same self-supervised backbone, with no significant per-protocol difference (Wilcoxon $p=0.18$), and is the strongest reference-audio metric on 6 of the 20 protocols. Beside the score itself, each TV channel visualizes which constriction deviates from the reference over time, providing interpretable information as to where articulation breaks down.

</details>


### [90] [MECT: Mixture of Experts with CNN-Transformer Network for Speaker verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24061)
*Yu Zheng,Jinghan Peng,ChangHao Zhang,Jian Liu,Weiqiang Wang*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: MECT is a speaker verification model that integrates Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) mechanism into a CNN-Transformer backbone, achieving state-of-the-art performance on VoxCeleb1 and strong results on CN-Celeb with compact parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To develop a speaker verification model that achieves high performance while maintaining compact parameters and low computational complexity, by leveraging the MoE mechanism to enhance the CNN-Transformer architecture.

Method: 1) Integrate MoE mechanism into CNN-Transformer backbone with optimized block structure and stacking scheme; 2) Investigate four MoE variants (utterance-level/frame-level granularity with dense/sparse routing); 3) Scale model to various sizes; 4) Implement streaming inference via causal retraining for 100ms chunk size.

Result: MECT-B2 achieves state-of-the-art performance on VoxCeleb1 and delivers strong results on CN-Celeb. The MoE mechanism proves effective over baseline with only a small increase in parameters. Streaming inference maintains strong performance at 100ms chunk size.

Conclusion: The MoE mechanism effectively enhances speaker verification performance with minimal parameter overhead. MECT demonstrates effectiveness across diverse datasets (VoxCeleb1, CN-Celeb) while maintaining compact parameters and low computational complexity, with successful streaming inference capability.

Abstract: In this paper, we propose MECT, a speaker verification model that integrates the Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) mechanism into a CNN-Transformer backbone with optimized block structure and stacking scheme. Specifically, we investigated four MoE variants that span utterance-level and frame-level granularity with dense and sparse routing strategies. The MoE mechanism proves to be effective over the baseline without MoE with only a small increase in parameters. We further scale MECT to a series of model sizes, all maintaining compact parameters and low computational complexity. In particular, MECT-B2 achieves state-of-the-art performance on VoxCeleb1 and delivers strong results on CN-Celeb, demonstrating its effectiveness across diverse datasets. In addition, we establish a streaming inference paradigm through causal retraining, which maintains strong performance at a chunk size of 100ms.

</details>


### [91] [Understanding Hyperspherical Geometry of ECAPA-TDNN Embedding and Its Impact on Zero-Shot Voice Conversion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24688)
*Mathilde Abrassart,Nicolas Obin,Axel Roebel*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper investigates the geometric organization of ECAPA-TDNN classifier prototypes on the unit hypersphere for voice conversion applications. The authors identify that standard training causes angular concentration and reduced effective dimensionality, then propose two geometric regularization methods (hinged Riesz log-energy and effective-dimension maximization) to achieve more uniform hyperspherical coverage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Angular-margin speaker encoders are widely used in voice conversion, but the geometry of their classifier prototypes remains poorly understood. The authors aim to analyze and characterize the organization of ECAPA-TDNN classifier prototypes to address issues of angular concentration and reduced effective dimensionality that arise from standard training.

Method: The authors analyze ECAPA-TDNN classifier prototypes as points on the unit hypersphere using rotation-invariant angular statistics and global/local effective dimensionality measures. They investigate two geometric regularization strategies: (1) hinged Riesz log-energy and (2) effective-dimension maximization, both applied to classifier prototypes to encourage more uniform hyperspherical coverage.

Result: The regularized prototype sets exhibit higher effective dimensionality and improved isotropy. The effects on speaker-recognition performance are configuration-dependent. When used as speaker encoders for Fast-VGAN in zero-shot voice conversion, the regularized systems show improved robustness, particularly for previously unseen speakers.

Conclusion: Geometric regularization of classifier prototypes can improve the performance of speaker encoders in voice conversion, especially for zero-shot scenarios with unseen speakers. The proposed methods achieve more uniform hyperspherical coverage, leading to enhanced robustness in voice conversion tasks.

Abstract: Angular-margin speaker encoders are widely used in voice conversion, yet the geometry of their classifier prototypes remains poorly understood. We analyze ECAPA-TDNN classifier prototypes as points on the unit hypersphere and characterize their organization using rotation-invariant angular statistics together with global and local effective dimensionality measures. Our analysis shows that standard training can induce angular concentration and a substantial reduction in effective dimensionality. To address this, we investigate two geometric regularization strategies (hinged Riesz log-energy and effective-dimension maximization) applied to classifier prototypes to encourage more uniform hyperspherical coverage. The resulting prototype sets exhibit higher effective dimensionality and improved isotropy, with configuration-dependent effects on speaker-recognition performance. When the corresponding ECAPA-TDNN models are used as speaker encoders for Fast-VGAN, the regularized systems also exhibit improved robustness in zero-shot voice conversion, particularly for previously unseen speakers.

</details>


### [92] [CycleSpeech: Reciprocal Alignment for Instruction-Controlled Speech Synthesis and Paralinguistic Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24771)
*Huan Liao,Haonan Han,Xingwen Han,Dekun Chen,Yuancheng Wang,Zhizheng Wu*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: CycleSpeech introduces a framework that connects speech synthesis and paralinguistic understanding through a shared structured voice profile, enabling reciprocal feedback between generation and understanding tasks without human preference annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Instruction-controlled speech synthesis and paralinguistic understanding are typically trained independently, leaving the reciprocal relationship between these two tasks underexplored. This gap limits the potential for mutual improvement between generation and understanding.

Method: CycleSpeech uses a shared structured voice profile as a common interface. It employs two feedback cycles: (1) Forward cycle - verifies if synthesized speech matches intended attributes by comparing recovered and target profiles; (2) Backward cycle - evaluates if profiles inferred from real speech can reconstruct the source speaking style. The method uses CycleGRPO with alternating policy updates based on reciprocal rewards, with fixed target profiles anchoring feedback. A bilingual dataset of 20,046 examples was constructed with instructions, target speech, speaker references, and structured profiles.

Result: CycleSpeech improves instruction adherence and profile recovery while maintaining competitive synthesis quality. Compared to Step-Audio-2-mini, it achieves 4.50 and 10.06 percentage points improvement in instruction-match accuracy for Chinese and English respectively. Controlled ablations confirm the contribution of cycle feedback to generation control.

Conclusion: Structured voice profiles can serve as an effective interface for reciprocal training between speech generation and paralinguistic understanding, eliminating the need for human preference annotations or additional preference-trained reward models.

Abstract: Instruction-controlled speech synthesis and paralinguistic understanding are often trained independently, leaving reciprocal feedback between the two tasks underexplored. We introduce CycleSpeech, a framework that connects generation and understanding through a shared, structured voice profile that serves as a common target for supervision and reciprocal feedback. The forward cycle assesses whether synthesized speech expresses the intended attributes by comparing recovered and target profiles. The backward cycle evaluates whether profiles inferred from real speech can guide reconstruction of the source speaking style. To support both directions, we construct a bilingual dataset of 20,046 examples pairing instructions, target speech, speaker references, and structured profiles. Building on joint supervised fine-tuning, CycleGRPO alternates policy updates using reciprocal rewards grounded in profile consistency and speaking-style reconstruction. Fixed target profiles anchor feedback from the evolving counterpart. This procedure requires neither human preference annotations nor an additional preference-trained reward model. Evaluations on Chinese and English benchmarks show improved instruction adherence and profile recovery while maintaining competitive synthesis quality. Compared with Step-Audio-2-mini, CycleSpeech improves instruction-match accuracy by 4.50 and 10.06 percentage points in Chinese and English, respectively. Controlled ablations further support the contribution of cycle feedback to generation control. These results support structured voice profiles as an interface for reciprocal training between speech generation and paralinguistic understanding. An online demo is available at https://cyclespeech.github.io.

</details>


### [93] [Fast Time-Varying Exponentiated Convolution Methods for Generative Direction Dependent Reverberation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24809)
*Yuancheng Luo*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes time-varying exponentiated convolution methods to augment small datasets of spherical harmonic encoded acoustic sound-fields for room impulse responses. The approach transforms Gaussian noise and impulse responses into reverberation and modified spectral-decay fields using recursive algorithms that extend into the spherical harmonic domain.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High costs of multi-microphone measurements and numerical simulations create a need for alternative data augmentation and synthetic data generation methods to supplement small collections of room impulse responses for spatial audio reproduction.

Method: The paper introduces two recursive and fast convolution algorithms that: (1) extend into the spherical harmonic domain, (2) model smooth reverberation time distributions with non-stationary Gaussian processes, and (3) realize an optimal filter design. These methods transform both Gaussian noise and existing impulse responses into reverberation and modified spectral-decay fields.

Result: Experiments demonstrate computational performance of the proposed algorithms and validate that out-of-distribution generated impulse responses can be produced successfully.

Conclusion: The time-varying exponentiated convolution methods provide an effective solution for augmenting small acoustic sound-field datasets, enabling more affordable development of spatial audio reproduction systems without relying solely on expensive measurements or simulations.

Abstract: Spherical harmonic encoded acoustic sound-fields capture directional characteristics of room impulse responses that are useful for accurate spatial audio reproduction. However, high costs of multi-microphone measurements and numerical simulations motivate alternative data-set augmentation and synthetic data generation methods that supplement small collections. This paper introduces time-varying exponentiated convolution methods that transform both Gaussian noise and impulse responses into reverberation and modified spectral-decay fields respectively. We derive two recursive and fast convolution algorithms that extend into the spherical harmonic domain, model smooth reverberation time distributions with non-stationary Gaussian processes, and realize an optimal filter design. Experiments evaluate computational performance, and validate out-of-distribution generated impulse responses.

</details>


### [94] [Automated Assessment of L2 Speech Rhythm Using Low-Frequency Amplitude Modulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24818)
*João Lima,Lucas Ueda,Paula Costa*

Main category: cs.SD

TL;DR: This paper proposes an acoustics-based approach using CNN to assess non-native speech rhythm directly from speech amplitude envelope, eliminating the need for error-prone segmental duration alignment. The method leverages low-frequency modulations linked to rhythm perception.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current rhythm metrics in automated speaking assessment rely on segmental duration requiring alignment, which is error-prone for non-native speech containing disfluencies and mispronunciations. There is a need for alignment-free alternatives that better align with human perception of prosody.

Method: A CNN-based model extracts rhythm features directly from speech amplitude envelope. Models are trained on a proficiency score regression task using the speechocean762 dataset. The amplitude envelope's first derivative is used as input. Performance is compared against duration-based models.

Result: The model using amplitude envelope's first derivative achieves the highest correlation with human-assigned scores on Fluency and Prosody dimensions. It produces significantly lower errors than segment duration-based models, particularly for less fluent speakers.

Conclusion: Acoustic envelope features serve as robust, alignment-free alternatives for L2 rhythm assessment, outperforming traditional duration-based methods in evaluating non-native speech prosody.

Abstract: Automated Speaking Assessment of non-native speech must effectively evaluate prosody, including speech rhythm, to align with human perception. However, commonly employed rhythm metrics rely on segmental duration, requiring an additional alignment step, which is error-prone in non-native speech containing disfluencies and mispronunciations. We propose an acoustics-based assessment approach that employs a convolutional neural network to extract rhythm features directly from the speech amplitude envelope, motivated by evidence linking low-frequency modulations to rhythm perception. The proposed models are trained on a proficiency score regression task using the speechocean762 dataset and compared against duration-based models. Our results show that a model using the amplitude envelope's first derivative achieves the highest correlation with human-assigned scores on the Fluency and Prosody dimensions, producing significantly lower errors than one using segment durations among less fluent speakers. The findings support acoustic envelope features as robust, alignment-free alternatives for L2 rhythm assessment. Code is released publicly.

</details>


<div id='cs.GR'></div>

# cs.GR [[Back]](#toc)

### [95] [Edge-centric Brain Transformer: An Edge-centric Functional Connectivity Learning Framework for fMRI-based Brain Disorder Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23782)
*Dengyi Zhao,Zhiheng Zhou,Mengyao Zhou,Yunping Wang,Xingqin Qi*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper proposes Edge-centric Brain Transformer (EBT), a novel deep learning framework that reinterprets resting-state fMRI analysis as functional connection representation learning rather than traditional node-centric approaches. EBT uses edge time-series, structure-aware transformers, and orthogonal clustering to achieve superior brain disorder diagnosis performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing deep learning methods for rs-fMRI analysis predominantly use node-centric representations (brain regions as primary units), which may overlook discriminative alterations embedded in functional connections. This limitation motivates the shift to an edge-centric perspective.

Method: EBT constructs edge time-series representations to capture dynamic co-fluctuation patterns of functional connections, organizes discriminative connections into a line graph for explicit connection-to-connection modeling, and employs a structure-aware transformer to learn both local dependencies among anatomically related connections and global interactions across distributed functional networks. An edge-level orthogonal clustering readout module derives subject-level representations and identifies latent connectivity modules.

Result: Evaluations on multiple neuroimaging datasets demonstrate that EBT consistently outperforms representative graph neural networks, brain transformers, and conventional connectivity-based approaches. Interpretability analyses reveal stable disease-associated functional connections and connectivity modules aligning with known pathological network alterations.

Conclusion: The edge-centric perspective provides a promising framework for rs-fMRI-based brain disorder diagnosis and discovering interpretable connectivity biomarkers. The approach establishes a new paradigm that focuses on functional connections rather than brain regions.

Abstract: Resting-state functional magnetic resonance imaging (rs-fMRI) enables the characterization of functional interactions among distributed brain regions and has shown promise for brain disorder diagnosis. However, existing deep learning methods predominantly rely on node-centric representations, where brain regions serve as the primary learning units, potentially overlooking discriminative alterations embedded in functional connections. Here, we propose an edge-centric brain transformer (EBT) framework that reformulates rs-fMRI analysis as functional connection representation learning. Instead of modeling brain regions independently, EBT constructs edge time-series representations to capture dynamic co-fluctuation patterns of functional connections and organizes discriminative connections into a line graph for explicit connection-to-connection modeling. A structure-aware transformer is developed to learn both local dependencies among anatomically related connections and global interactions across distributed functional networks. Furthermore, an edge-level orthogonal clustering readout module is introduced to derive subject-level representations and identify latent connectivity modules associated with brain disorders. Evaluations on multiple neuroimaging datasets demonstrate that EBT consistently outperforms representative graph neural networks, brain transformers, and conventional connectivity-based approaches. Interpretability analyses further reveal stable disease-associated functional connections and connectivity modules that align with known pathological network alterations. These findings establish an edge-centric perspective for rs-fMRI-based brain disorder diagnosis and provide a promising framework for discovering interpretable connectivity biomarkers. The source code is publicly available at: https://github.com/Zdy12/Edge-centric-Brain-Transformer.

</details>


### [96] [MoSAT: Human Motion Generation from Spatial Audio and Textual Description](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23797)
*Shuyang Xu,Zhiyang Dou,Yiduo Hao,Zekun Li,Liang Pan,Jingbo Wang,Cheng Lin,Yuan Liu,Wenping Wang,Mingmin Zhao,Taku Komura*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: This paper introduces STAM, a dataset of motion sequences paired with spatial audio and textual annotations, and MoSAT, a latent flow-matching framework for full-body motion generation jointly conditioned on natural language intent and directional spatial audio cues using hierarchical cross-attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human motion is shaped by both external acoustic events (spatial audio conveys environmental cues that elicit or guide responses) and behavioral intent (text specifies desired actions). This novel task of joint conditioning on spatial audio and natural language has been largely overlooked in previous research.
