Method: The authors propose MoSAT, a latent flow-matching framework with hierarchical cross-attention that processes natural-language intent and directional spatial-audio cues before generating motion. They also develop tri-modal evaluators for comprehensive evaluation on this novel task.

Result: MoSAT achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) performance by leveraging spatial audio's intrinsic motion-shaping properties alongside textual semantics, enabling precise and diverse motion generation in various scenarios. The hierarchical cross-attention design enhances temporally coherent and semantically aligned motion sequences.

Conclusion: The combination of spatial audio and natural language provides complementary cues for human motion synthesis. MoSAT effectively leverages both modalities to generate high-quality, diverse human motions, demonstrating the value of this novel tri-modal conditioning approach.

Abstract: Human motion is shaped by both external acoustic events and behavioral intent: spatial audio conveys environmental cues that elicit or guide a response, while text specifies the desired action and how it should be performed. In this paper, we study the novel task of human motion synthesis jointly conditioned on spatial audio and natural language, a problem that has been largely overlooked in previous research. To support this task, We introduce STAM, a dataset of motion sequences paired with spatial audio and detailed textual annotations whose rich vocabulary affords precise and nuanced specification of human motions. We further introduce MoSAT, a latent flow-matching framework for full-body motion generation jointly conditioned on natural-language intent and directional spatial-audio cues through hierarchical cross-attention before generating motion. Such a hierarchical design enhances temporally coherent and semantically aligned motion sequences. We also develop tri-modal evaluators for comprehensive evaluation on this novel task. Extensive experiments show that MoSAT achieves the SOTA performance by leveraging spatial audio's intrinsic motion-shaping properties alongside textual semantics, enabling precise and diverse motion in various scenarios.

</details>


### [97] [Constrained Program Generation for 3D Reaction Animation with a 0.8B Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24457)
*Hongyuan Wang,Daming Luo,Nico Pietroni,Christy Liang,Andrew McDonagh*

Main category: cs.GR

TL;DR: ChemXRG is a domain-specific language framework that enables the creation of accurate, verifiable 3D chemical reaction animations by representing reactions as executable programs with persistent atom identifiers and explicit molecular operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visualizing chemical reactions requires making molecular changes visible while maintaining fidelity to stated chemistry. Existing tools (equations, structural diagrams, molecular viewers) provide complementary but fragmented descriptions, and assembling interactive 3D explanations still requires specifying changes and checking their consistency—a significant gap in current workflows.

Method: The approach uses a DSL framework where reaction animations are represented as executable programs. Key innovations include: (1) Persistent atom identifiers and explicit bond/charge/grouping operations that connect symbolic reactions to displayed transformations; (2) A shared representation enabling generation, verification, and rendering to operate on the same account; (3) Reaction-grounded constraints that fix input-determined facts and restrict action choices; (4) Execution checks that validate transformations before geometry and frames are constructed. Implementation includes a reaction-program corpus and ChemQwen, a trained 0.8B DSL generator.

Result: Paired and component evaluations demonstrate improved compiler acceptance and normalized full-program agreement under input-conditioned constraints. The system identifies remaining failures that require execution checks. A public browser application demonstrates the connection from symbolic reaction descriptions to inspectable programs and interactive 3D animations.

Conclusion: The executable program paradigm for reaction animation provides a systematic approach to generating, verifying, and rendering chemical reaction visualizations. While the approach shows improved performance over baselines, some failures still require execution checks, indicating areas for future improvement in the DSL generator and constraint system.

Abstract: Visualizing a chemical reaction requires making its molecular changes visible while keeping the animation faithful to the stated chemistry. Equations, structural diagrams and molecular viewers provide complementary descriptions, but assembling an interactive three-dimensional explanation still requires specifying the changes and checking their consistency. We present ChemXRG, a domain-specific language (DSL) framework that addresses this gap by representing a reaction animation as an executable program. Persistent atom identifiers and explicit bond, charge and grouping operations connect the symbolic reaction to the displayed transformation. This shared representation lets generation, verification and rendering operate on the same account of what changes. Given known, atom-mapped reactant and product structures, reaction-grounded constraints fix input-determined facts and restrict action choices; execution checks validate the resulting transformation before geometry and frames are constructed. We implement this paradigm with a reaction-program corpus and ChemQwen, a trained 0.8B DSL generator. Paired and component evaluations show improved compiler acceptance and normalized full-program agreement under input-conditioned constraints, while identifying remaining failures that require execution checks. A public browser application demonstrates the connection from symbolic reaction descriptions to inspectable programs and interactive 3D animations.

</details>


<div id='cs.SE'></div>

# cs.SE [[Back]](#toc)

### [98] [When Who You Are Can Change the Code You Get: A Study of Persona-Induced Bias in LLM Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22102)
*Anubhav Gupta,Mayara Costa Figueiredo,Leticia Santos Machado,Tanner Wright,Ivan Beschastnikh,Cleidson R. B. de Souza,Gema Rodríguez-Pérez*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether user demographic information (nationality, gender, experience level) influences the quality of code generated by LLMs, using Gemini 2.5 Pro and GPT-OSS-120B models across 18 personas and 35,000+ programs. Results show demographic markers frequently appear in outputs (65% responses, 70% reasoning), with divergent model behavior: Gemini shows decreased correctness while GPT-OSS shows improved accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to address an under-examined risk in LLM-assisted software development: whether demographic cues in user prompts introduce bias in code generation, potentially affecting technical quality even for purely technical tasks.

Method: The study uses a large-scale empirical approach with 18 demographic personas spanning nationality, gender, and experience level. They compare persona-induced prompts against a neutral baseline across 35,000+ generated programs, analyzing demographic marker leakage in reasoning and responses, and measuring functional correctness (LiveCodeBench), maintainability, code style, and security.

Result: Demographic markers appear in up to 65% of responses and 70% of reasoning traces. For Gemini, persona prompting decreased correctness by 1.54 percentage points on average (odds ratio = 0.51 for one persona). For GPT-OSS, accuracy improved by 3.4-5.7% across all personas (odds ratios = 1.8-3.0). Maintainability and code style show statistically significant but negligible effects (Cliff's δ < 0.15). Security vulnerabilities show no systematic patterns.

Conclusion: The presence of demographic information in prompts is associated with measurable variation in LLM reasoning and code quality, even for purely technical tasks. This effect varies across models (Gemini degraded, GPT-OSS improved) but is consistent across models. The study highlights an under-examined risk in LLM-assisted software development where demographic cues may influence technical outcomes.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely used as programming assistants, yet it remains unclear whether and how user's demographic information impacts the technical quality of generated code. We conduct a large-scale empirical study of persona-induced bias in LLM-based code generation, focusing a proprietary model (Gemini 2.5 Pro) and an open-weight model (GPT-OSS-120B). Using 18 demographic personas spanning nationality, gender, and experience level, we compare persona-induced prompts against a neutral baseline. Across 35,000+ generated programs, we analyze demographic marker leakage in reasoning and responses, as well as differences in functional correctness, maintainability, code style, and security.
  Our results show that demographic cues are frequently reflected in LLM reasoning and outputs. Demographic markers appear in up to 65% of responses and 70% of reasoning traces, despite being semantically irrelevant to the tasks. On LiveCodeBench, persona prompting were associated with lower correctness scores of the Gemini model by an average of 1.54 percentage points, with one persona exhibiting a decrease of 3.6% (odds ratio = 0.51). In contrast, the accuracy of the GPT-OSS model improved by 3.4 - 5.7% across all personas (odds ratios = 1.8 - 3.0). Maintainability and code style metrics show statistically significant but negligible effect sizes (all Cliff's δ < 0.15), and security vulnerabilities exhibit no systematic persona-specific patterns.
  Overall, our results show that the presence of demographic information about users is associated with measurable variation in LLM reasoning and code quality even in purely technical tasks, and that these effects hold across models. Our work highlights an under-examined risk in LLM-assisted software development.

</details>


### [99] [A Governance-Aware Large Language Model Orchestrated Agentic Digital Twin for Transmission System Operator Control Room Decision Support](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22476)
*Costas Mylonas,Magda Foti,Emmanouel Varvarigos*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper proposes a governance-aware agentic digital twin for transmission grid control rooms that addresses LLM limitations (hallucinations, uncontrolled tool use, weak traceability) through a mandatory governance layer enforcing four rules: whitelisted tools only, step budget limits, approval requirements for side-effect actions, and backend-sourced numbers with full metadata. The system achieves 96.5% tool selection and 93.7% task success on a 118-task benchmark with Greek transmission network digital twin, with rules holding 99.8%+ across 1416 runs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transmission system operators face rising complexity from renewable integration, reduced inertia, and tighter security margins. While LLMs offer natural-language decision support, their hallucinations, uncontrolled tool use, and weak traceability conflict with control room requirements for reliability and accountability.

Method: The system restricts LLMs to selecting and parameterizing only whitelisted analysis tools. Every proposed action passes through a governance layer that cannot be bypassed, enforcing four rules: (1) only whitelisted tools execute, (2) no run exceeds step budget, (3) no side-effect action executes without explicit operator approval, (4) every number in answers is rendered by the layer from backend results with unit, variable, and time. Evaluation uses a 118-task benchmark covering analytics, simulation, multi-step workflows, and twelve families of adversarial inputs on a Greek transmission network digital twin.

Result: Across 590 runs of the primary model: tool selection reaches 96.5%, task success reaches 93.7%, and all four rules hold without exception. In a separate three-repetition study across four LLMs (1416 total runs), rules hold on every run with ~99.8% lower bound. Without the governance layer, the same model executes all 45 approval-requiring runs without authorization and only 39.2% of answers contain backend-supported numbers. Enforcement costs 12-16ms per request.

Conclusion: The governance layer effectively constrains LLM behavior to meet control room requirements while maintaining high task performance. The four-rule enforcement is highly reliable across models and runs, with minimal overhead. The approach demonstrates that LLMs can be safely deployed in critical infrastructure control rooms when properly constrained, though operator approval remains essential for side-effect actions.

Abstract: Transmission system operators face rising complexity from renewable integration, reduced inertia, and tighter security margins. Large language models offer natural-language decision support, but their hallucinations, uncontrolled tool use, and weak traceability conflict with control room requirements. This paper presents a governance-aware agentic digital twin for transmission grid control rooms. The large language model only selects and parameterizes whitelisted analysis tools, and every proposed action passes through a governance layer that the model cannot bypass. The layer enforces four rules on every run. Only whitelisted tools execute. No run exceeds its step budget. No action with side effects executes without explicit operator approval. Every number in an answer is rendered by the layer from backend results with its unit, variable, and time. The rules are checked on a persistent audit trail for every run of a released 118-task benchmark, which covers analytics, simulation, multi-step workflows, and twelve families of adversarial inputs on a digital twin of the Greek transmission network. Across 590 runs of the primary model, tool selection reaches 96.5% and task success 93.7%, and all four rules hold without exception. In a separate three-repetition study across four large language models, 1416 runs in total, the rules again hold on every run, with an approximate 95% lower bound of 99.8%. Removing the layer makes the same model execute all 45 approval-requiring runs without authorization and leaves only 39.2% of its answers with backend-supported numbers. Enforcement costs 12 to 16 milliseconds per request.

</details>


### [100] [Identity or Prompt Noise? A Calibrated Invariance Audit of LLM Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22511)
*Maksim E. Eren,Ryan Barron,Eric Michalak,Charles Nicholas,Manish Bhattarai*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper audits 30.73 million Python code generations across seven model checkpoints on HumanEval+ and MBPP+ to investigate whether identity cues (gender, country, occupation) affect code generation outputs. Using within-task randomization and false-discovery-rate control, they find occupation is the most consistent structural axis, with small but reproducible changes in code form (CodeBLEU dispersion), while no stable disadvantage to named identities or downstream harm was demonstrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to investigate whether large language models for code generation produce biased outputs based on identity cues (gender, country, occupation). The authors note that while identity cues should be irrelevant to fixed programming specifications, raw counterfactual differences can arise from unequal samples and prompt wording. They aim to audit whether models systematically vary their outputs based on identity information.

Method: The researchers audited 30.73 million executed Python generations from seven checkpoints on HumanEval+ and MBPP+, supplemented by an exploratory 550B slice. They used model-assigned gender, country, and occupation personas. The methodology involved within-task randomization and false-discovery-rate control to identify structural axes of variation. They examined CodeBLEU dispersion (measuring code form differences) against exchangeability nulls, and tested replication across token similarity, length, comments, reference similarity, and complexity metrics. They also examined pass-rate dispersion (measuring functional differences).

Result: Key findings: (1) Occupation is the most consistent structural axis - CodeBLEU dispersion exceeds exchangeability null in 10/14 model-benchmark cells, remains significant in 8/12 full-coverage cells, and exceeds the country ratio in every paired cell (median excess = 0.141 points). (2) Country leads raw dispersion in 12/14 cells but has a median calibrated ratio of 1.00 (no real effect after calibration). (3) Gender-associated variation cannot be separated from persona wording. (4) In six high-pass-rate cells, occupation dispersion replicates across multiple metrics (token similarity, length, comments, reference similarity, complexity), while pass-rate dispersion is significant in none. (5) The effects are small and relate to code form, not functional correctness.

Conclusion: The evidence supports small, reproducible occupation-conditioned changes in code form, but not stable disadvantage to named identities or demonstrated downstream harm. The authors use this to motivate broader commentary on potential impacts of bias in code generation, including the possibility that models may condition outputs on identity information available from prior conversational context.

Abstract: Identity cues are irrelevant to a fixed programming specification, but raw counterfactual differences can arise from unequal samples and prompt wording. We audit 30.73 million executed Python generations from seven checkpoints on HumanEval+ and MBPP+, supplemented by an exploratory 550B slice, under model-assigned gender, country, and occupation personas. Within-task randomization and false-discovery-rate control identify occupation as the most consistent structural axis: CodeBLEU dispersion exceeds its exchangeability null in 10/14 model--benchmark cells, remains significant in 8/12 full-coverage cells, and exceeds the country ratio in every paired cell, although the median excess is only 0.141 points. In six high-pass-rate cells, occupation dispersion replicates across token similarity, length, comments, reference similarity, and complexity, while pass-rate dispersion is significant in none. Country leads raw dispersion in 12/14 cells but has a median calibrated ratio of 1.00. Gender-associated variation cannot be separated from persona wording in this design. Thus, the evidence supports small, reproducible occupation-conditioned changes in code form, not stable disadvantage to named identities or demonstrated downstream harm. We use this finding to motivate a broader commentary on the potential impacts of bias in code generation, including the possibility that models may condition their outputs on identity information available from prior conversational context.

</details>


### [101] [Does Order Matter? An Empirical Investigation into the Impact of File Ordering on Code Review Effectiveness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22610)
*Md Shamimur Rahman,Zadia Codabux,Chanchal K. Roy*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Large-scale empirical study analyzing 330,343 multi-file pull requests reveals that file ordering and pull request size significantly impact review effectiveness. Later files in PRs (position 30) have higher latent bug rates (61.5%) compared to position 1 (56.7%), and PRs with ~10 files have the lowest risk. Each additional file reduces review comment odds by 8.7%, indicating an attention-effectiveness gap where visible review activity doesn't prevent defects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern code review effectiveness depends on reviewer expertise, change characteristics, and tool presentation. Most platforms display modified files alphabetically by default, but prior work suggests this ordering is cognitively misaligned with how developers understand multi-file pull requests. This study investigates whether ordering-related attention patterns affect outcomes at scale.

Method: Large-scale mining study of 330,343 multi-file pull requests and 756,814 file instances from 182 GitHub projects across five programming languages. Examined associations between file position and later bug-fixing changes, PR size as a moderator, and reviewer attention (proxied by comments) aligned with latent bug outcomes.

Result: Statistically significant but modest associations found among file position, PR size, review activity, and latent bug likelihood. Latent bug rates increase from 56.7% at position 1 to 61.5% at position 30. PR size shows non-linear relationship: ~10 files has lowest risk, while very small and very large PRs have elevated rates. Each additional modified file reduces odds of receiving any review comment by ~8.7%.

Conclusion: Alphabetical file ordering is not a neutral interface default but a structural feature shaping attention allocation, review coverage, and confidence in review outcomes. Proposes four design interventions: context-aware file ordering, dependency-aware grouping, risk-aware prioritization, and per-file coverage indicators to make review attention more visible and actionable.

Abstract: Modern code review is central to software quality, but its effectiveness depends on reviewer expertise, change characteristics, and how tools present changes. Most platforms display modified files alphabetically by default, although our prior work shows that developers find this ordering cognitively misaligned with how they understand multi-file pull requests. Whether these ordering-related attention patterns affect outcomes at scale remains unclear. We present a large-scale study of file ordering and review effectiveness, mining 330,343 multi-file pull requests and 756,814 file instances from 182 GitHub projects in five programming languages. We examine whether file position is associated with later bug-fixing changes, whether pull request size moderates this relationship, and whether reviewer attention, proxied by comments, aligns with latent bug outcomes. Results show statistically significant but modest associations among file position, pull request size, review activity, and latent bug likelihood. Latent bug rates rise from 56.7% at position 1 to 61.5% at position 30. Pull request size has a non-linear relationship with latent bug likelihood: pull requests of about ten files have the lowest risk, while very small and very large ones have elevated rates. Hurdle models show that attention is diluted as pull request size grows: each additional modified file reduces the odds of receiving any review comment by about 8.7%. These findings reveal an attention-effectiveness gap: visible review activity does not necessarily prevent defects. Alphabetical ordering is therefore not a neutral interface default, but a structural feature shaping attention allocation, review coverage, and confidence in review outcomes. We propose context-aware file ordering, dependency-aware grouping, risk-aware prioritization, and per-file coverage indicators to make review attention more visible and actionable.

</details>


### [102] [Why Do Pull Requests Go Silent? Uncovering the Barriers to Contribution Completion in Open-Source Code Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22625)
*Md Shamimur Rahman,Farhana Akter,Md Mustakim Billah,Zadia Codabux,Chanchal K. Roy*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates pull request (PR) inactivity in open-source GitHub repositories, analyzing 14,234 stalled PRs and 164,562 review comments from 19 popular repositories. It identifies which PR types stall most often, the reasons for inactivity from both authors' and reviewers' perspectives, and how stalling affects future contributor and reviewer engagement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work has examined PR abandonment and review delays, but less is known about contribution types, discussion-level barriers, and post-stalling collaboration patterns. Understanding these factors is crucial to reduce wasted effort from contributors and maintainers.

Method: The study analyzed stalled PRs using stale-bot workflows from 19 popular GitHub repositories. An LLM-based voting classifier categorized PRs by contribution type. Quantitative analysis was combined with qualitative coding of general and inline review discussions.

Result: Feature-enhancement and issue-fixing PRs comprised over 77% of stalled PRs. General comments revealed stalling was mainly due to communication and coordination breakdowns (missing interaction, delayed feedback, unclear follow-up). Inline comments showed many PRs were blocked by technical issues (failing checks, configuration problems, compatibility concerns, environment mismatches). Only 39.56% of contributors submitted another PR, and reviewer re-engagement was approximately 21%.

Conclusion: PR inactivity is a socio-technical coordination problem involving communication, technical readiness, review ownership, and automation practices. Recommendations include type-aware triage, clearer feedback, explicit ownership of next actions, CI blocker management, and cause-aware stale-bot interventions.

Abstract: Pull requests (PRs) underpin pull-based software development by enabling distributed code review and collaborative contribution in open-source projects. Yet many become inactive before integration and are eventually abandoned or closed, wasting contributor and maintainer effort. Although prior work has examined PR abandonment and review delays, less is known about the contribution types, discussion-level barriers, and post-stalling collaboration patterns associated with inactivity. We investigate which PR types most often stall, why inactivity occurs from authors' and reviewers' perspectives, and how stalling relates to later contributor and reviewer engagement. We analyzed 14,234 stalled PRs and 164,562 review comments from 19 popular GitHub repositories using stale-bot workflows. An LLM-based voting classifier categorized PRs by contribution type, while quantitative analysis was combined with qualitative coding of general and inline review discussions. Feature-enhancement and issue-fixing PRs formed the largest share, together exceeding 77% of classified stalled PRs. General comments linked stalling mainly to communication and coordination breakdowns, including missing interaction, delayed feedback, and unclear follow-up. Inline comments showed that inactivity does not always reflect disengagement: many PRs were blocked by technical or dependency issues, including failing checks, configuration problems, compatibility concerns, and environment mismatches. Only 39.56% of contributors later submitted another PR, and reviewer re-engagement with the same contributors was approximately 21%. PR inactivity is a socio-technical coordination problem involving communication, technical readiness, review ownership, and automation practices. We recommend type-aware triage, clearer review feedback, explicit ownership of next actions, CI blocker management, and cause-aware stale-bot interventions.

</details>


### [103] [From Code to Requirements: Agentic Reverse Engineering of Business Rules at Enterprise Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22719)
*Garima Agrawal,Prasun Das,Priyanka L,Minisha N,Akshay Sarvade,Hemath Manivanan,Sravani Joshna,Prasad Kalyansundaram*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: An agentic framework with seven specialized AI agents autonomously generates Business Requirements Documents (bRDs) by reverse engineering undocumented enterprise software, achieving 98%+ cost reduction and sub-9-minute generation times compared to traditional manual approaches taking over two years.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Business requirements for enterprise software typically reside in unstructured forms (source code, configuration files, institutional memory) rather than formal documentation. When migration, extension, or audit is needed, the absence of formal requirements artifacts forces expensive, knowledge-losing manual reverse engineering.

Method: Seven specialized agents collaborate to: (1) discover user journeys, (2) extract business rules, and (3) synthesize a locked bRD. The framework uses static analysis tools, is guided by embedded expert practitioner cognitive models, and incorporates human reviewers at controlled escalation points. Evaluation used actual execution traces across a real enterprise deployment, with extracted rules corroborated against production defect records. Baseline costs derived using IFPUG complexity models and industry benchmark labor rates.

Result: The framework generates comprehensive bRDs in under nine minutes per service. Extracted business rules were independently corroborated against real production defect records. Cost reduction exceeded 98% compared to a prior migration to the same target architecture that took over two years.

Conclusion: The agentic framework effectively automates requirements extraction from undocumented enterprise software, dramatically reducing time and cost while maintaining accuracy through human oversight and validation against production data.

Abstract: Business requirements for enterprise software systems are rarely captured in structured form; the logic resides instead in source code, configuration files, and institutional memory. When these systems must be migrated, extended, or audited, the absence of formal requirements artifacts forces teams into expensive, knowledge-losing manual reverse engineering. This paper presents an agentic framework that autonomously generates Business Requirements Documents (bRDs) through reverse engineering of undocumented enterprise software. Seven specialized agents collaborate to discover user journeys, extract business rules, and synthesize a locked bRD from code, test suites, configuration, and available documentation, supported by static analysis tools, guided by embedded expert practitioner cognitive models, and overseen by human reviewers at controlled escalation points. Measured on actual execution traces across a real enterprise deployment, the framework generates comprehensive bRDs in under nine minutes per service, with extracted rules independently corroborated against real production defect records. In a comparable prior migration to the same target architecture, delivery required over two years; on the present programme the framework achieves a cost reduction exceeding 98% against a baseline derived from actual repository metrics using IFPUG complexity models and industry benchmark labor rates.

</details>


### [104] [Towards the Generalizability of Leveraging ChatGPT in APR via Self-enhancing: An Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22844)
*Qingyuan Li,Chuanyi Li,Yaopeng Yang,Ziwen Ge,Jidong Ge,Bin Luo*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper evaluates the stability of ChatGPT-enhanced Automated Program Repair (APR) methods across three benchmarks (Defects4J, HumanEval-Java, BugsInPy) using two different LLMs (GPT-3.5-Turbo, GPT-5.4-mini). The study reveals that enhancement gains vary significantly across benchmarks and models, and that benchmark-specific factors like code transformations and fine-tuning significantly impact results.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While ChatGPT-enhanced APR methods (using self-correction and autonomous agents) report strong results on Defects4J and SWE-bench, the stability of these gains across different benchmarks remains under-explored. There is a need to evaluate generalizability across benchmarks and models.

Method: The authors evaluated three ChatGPT-enhanced APR methods (SRepair, FixAgent, and one other unspecified method) on three representative long-standing benchmarks (Defects4J, HumanEval-Java, BugsInPy) using two LLMs (GPT-3.5-Turbo and GPT-5.4-mini). They investigated benchmark-related factors through code transformations and benchmark-specific fine-tuning, and compared direct error message/test provision against enhanced methods.

Result: With GPT-3.5-Turbo: SRepair achieves larger absolute gain on HumanEval-Java than Defects4J; SRepair and FixAgent without extrinsic information yield negative gains on BugsInPy. With GPT-5.4-mini: evaluated methods achieve larger absolute gains on Defects4J than HumanEval-Java; gains on BugsInPy are non-negative but limited. Code transformations reduce enhancement gains on Defects4J; benchmark-specific fine-tuning increases gains on BugsInPy. Directly supplying GPT-3.5-Turbo with error messages and triggering tests yields more correct repairs than evaluated methods on BugsInPy.

Conclusion: The findings highlight the need to evaluate generalizability across benchmarks and models using multiple metrics. When enhancement gains are limited, directly providing repair-specific extrinsic information (error messages, test results) may be more effective than enhancement methods like self-correction or autonomous agents.

Abstract: Automated Program Repair (APR) increasingly relies on Large Language Models (LLMs). ChatGPT-enhanced APR uses techniques such as self-correction and autonomous agents to improve repair without modifying model parameters. Although these approaches report strong results on Defects4J and SWE-bench, the stability of enhancement gains across benchmarks remains under-explored. We evaluate three ChatGPT-enhanced APR methods on three representative, long-standing benchmarks. With GPT-3.5-Turbo, SRepair achieves a larger absolute gain on HumanEval-Java than on Defects4J, while SRepair and FixAgent without extrinsic information yield negative gains on BugsInPy. With GPT-5.4-mini, the evaluated methods achieve larger absolute gains on Defects4J than on HumanEval-Java, while gains on BugsInPy are non-negative but limited. We investigate benchmark-related factors through code transformations and benchmark-specific fine-tuning. Code transformations reduce enhancement gains on Defects4J, while benchmark-specific fine-tuning increases gains on BugsInPy. Directly supplying GPT-3.5-Turbo with error messages and triggering tests yields more correct repairs than the evaluated ChatGPT-enhanced APR methods on BugsInPy. These findings highlight the need to evaluate generalizability across benchmarks and models using multiple metrics, and suggest that directly providing repair-specific extrinsic information may be more effective than enhancement methods when their gains are limited.

</details>


### [105] ["It Comes in Notebooks": Changes and Challenges when Operationalizing ML Prototypes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22903)
*Arumoy Shome,Luís Cruz,Diomidis Spinellis,Arie van Deursen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study investigates the engineering work involved in transitioning ML models from computational notebooks to production systems. Through interviews with 13 ML practitioners, researchers identified 23 engineering changes across 5 themes and introduced the concept of "oversight debt" - technical debt accumulated during prototyping that must be repaid during operationalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Machine learning prototyping in notebooks is widespread, but the engineering work required to move models to production and the underlying quality concerns remain poorly understood. The study aims to characterize this transition and identify the software quality issues driving these changes.

Method: Qualitative study using semi-structured interviews with 13 ML practitioners from industry and academia. Used reflexive thematic analysis to identify patterns and themes.

Result: Identified 23 engineering changes organized into 5 themes: code restructuring, data pipeline development, testing & validation, pipeline automation, and monitoring & observability. Also identified 20 software quality attributes across the ML development lifecycle. Introduced "oversight debt" concept - the deferred costs of human oversight in notebooks that become obligatory in production. Found 7 quality trade-offs that are inherent to the notebook-to-production transition rather than poor engineering practices.

Conclusion: The study provides a framework for understanding ML operationalization effort, establishes empirical links between engineering changes and software quality concerns, and offers implications for practitioners, tool designers, and researchers working on ML-enabled systems. The key insight is that operationalization involves repaying "oversight debt" by building automated substitutes for the interactive human oversight that notebooks provide.

Abstract: Machine learning practitioners commonly prototype models in computational notebooks before transitioning them to automated production systems. Despite its prevalence, the concrete engineering work involved in this transition and the software quality concerns that motivate it remain insufficiently characterized. We report on a qualitative study based on semi-structured interviews with 13 ML practitioners from industry and academia. Using reflexive thematic analysis, we identify 23 engineering changes organized into five themes: code restructuring, data pipeline development, testing & validation, pipeline automation, and monitoring & observability. We also identify 20 software quality attributes across the ML development lifecycle and map them to the engineering changes. A recurring pattern in our findings is that computational notebooks externalize oversight to the human practitioner, and defer costs that become obligatory at operationalization time. Operationalization constitutes the repayment of this technical debt accumulated during prototyping, which we refer to as oversight debt. Practitioners do not merely restructure notebook code, but repay this debt by constructing automated substitutes for the interactive oversight that notebooks provide. We further present seven quality trade-offs showing that these tensions are properties of the notebook-to-production transition, rather than symptoms of poor engineering practice. Our findings structure operationalization effort, establish empirical links between engineering changes and software quality concerns, and provide implications for practitioners, tool designers, and researchers working on ML-enabled software systems.

</details>


### [106] [Characterizing Feedback Statements in Machine Learning Jupyter Notebooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22912)
*Arumoy Shome,Lu\is Cruz,Diomidis Spinellis,Arie van Deursen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study analyzes feedback statements in ML Jupyter notebooks to understand how practitioners catch silent failures that don't surface as exceptions. The authors mine nearly 300K public notebooks, extract ~1M feedback statements, and develop a taxonomy through grounded theory analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many ML failures do not produce exceptions and thus escape traditional crash-based analyses. Understanding what practitioners check to detect these silent failures is crucial for improving ML development practices and tooling.

Method: The authors: 1) Mined 297,851 public Python Jupyter notebooks from GitHub and Kaggle; 2) Extracted 1,092,780 feedback statements; 3) Sampled 816 statements via proportional stratified sampling from CodeBERT embeddings; 4) Applied grounded theory and open coding for analysis.

Result: The study produced a taxonomy of feedback statements organized by functional intent and ML pipeline stage. Key findings: feedback is overwhelmingly exploratory, and the two platforms (GitHub/Kaggle) host qualitatively different modes of ML work. The taxonomy captures defensive practices against silent failures that crash analysis cannot observe.

Conclusion: Notebook source should be treated as a confounder in ML developer practice studies. The findings surface opportunities for notebook tooling and motivate empirical study of silent ML failures. The authors release the corpus of 1,092,780 feedback statements and codebook for replication and tooling research.

Abstract: Machine learning development in Jupyter notebooks is iterative and feedback-driven. Practitioners author statements that reveal information about program execution and use it to decide what to do next. We call these feedback statements and identify two forms: exploratory statements that display values for visual inspection, and validation statements that enforce conditions programmatically through assertions. Many ML failures do not surface as exceptions and thus escape the crash-based analyses that dominate prior work on ML notebooks. This study examines what practitioners check to catch failures that would otherwise pass silently, by characterizing feedback statements that encode the practitioner's mental model of what the code should do and what could go wrong. We mine 297,851 public Python Jupyter notebooks from GitHub and Kaggle and extract 1,092,780 feedback statements. We sample 816 statements through proportional stratified sampling from semantic clusters obtained from CodeBERT embeddings, and apply grounded theory and open coding to label and analyze each one. We contribute a taxonomy of feedback statements in ML notebooks, organized along the functional intent of the statement and the ML pipeline stage in which it appears. The taxonomy reveals that feedback is overwhelmingly exploratory, and that the two platforms host qualitatively different modes of ML work. Mapping our taxonomy to an existing crash taxonomy shows that it captures defensive practices against silent failures that crash analysis cannot observe. Our findings indicate that notebook source should be treated as a confounder in studies of ML developer practice, surface opportunities for notebook tooling, and motivate empirical study of silent ML failures. We release the corpus of 1,092,780 feedback statements and the codebook to support replication and tooling research.

</details>


### [107] [NostrAgent: A Decentralized Identity and Delegation Architecture for Sovereign Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22944)
*Oliver Aleksander Larsen,Mahyar Tourchi Moghaddam*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: NostrAgent is a decentralized architecture for autonomous AI agents that unifies five essential capabilities (persistent identity, scoped delegation, peer trust, discovery, and payment) over Nostr relays using three custom event kinds (Kinds 38100-38102), with L402 payment binding, eliminating centralized trust roots while enabling offline verification and auditable authorization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous AI agents increasingly operate across organizational boundaries, invoking third-party services, delegating tasks, and paying for resources. However, existing approaches fragment authority, trust, and payment across separate centralized systems, breaking continuity when keys rotate or delegations revoke. The lack of unified decentralized identity and delegation mechanisms prevents safe agent deployment.

Method: Python prototype with mixed-method evaluation: (1) ATAM quality analysis with two-round mini-Delphi panel, (2) STRIDE threat modeling across three trust boundaries, (3) eleven benchmarks with non-parametric statistics, and (4) nineteen failure modes analysis. Architecture uses three custom Nostr event kinds: Kind 38100 (identity declarations with BIP340 Schnorr signatures + pre-rotation), Kind 38101 (scoped delegation chains with narrowing capabilities), and Kind 38102 (peer attestations forming Sybil-deterrent trust graph), with Lightning HTTP 402 (L402) binding payment to agent identity.

Result: Sub-millisecond offline verification, linear delegation-chain scaling, and Lightning-settled L402 at 157 ms median on regtest. 17 of 19 failure modes pass empirically, one is bounded analytically, and one disclosed as architectural limitation.

Conclusion: NostrAgent demonstrates an auditable prototype substrate for trustworthy agentic systems without centralized trust roots. It provides operator-sovereign identity without registration authority, substitutable relays (transport only, not trust root), and replayable authorization decisions from signed events—addressing the fragmentation problem in decentralized agent deployment.

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents increasingly act across organizational boundaries on behalf of human operators: they invoke third-party services, delegate subtasks to other agents, and pay for metered resources. Deploying such agents safely requires five capabilities that today live in separate systems: persistent identity, scoped delegation, peer trust, discovery, and payment. Existing approaches root these in centralized authorities or cover only subsets, so authority, trust, and payment fracture exactly where autonomy needs continuity: when a key rotates or a delegation must be revoked. We present NostrAgent, a decentralized architecture that unifies all five over Nostr relays using three custom event kinds: Kind 38100 identity declarations authenticated by BIP340 Schnorr signatures with pre-rotation commitments, Kind 38101 scoped delegation chains whose every hop verifiably narrows granted capabilities, and Kind 38102 peer attestations forming a Sybil-deterrent trust graph, with Lightning HTTP 402 (L402) binding payment to agent identity. Identity remains operator-sovereign without any registration authority; relays are substitutable transport rather than a trust root; and every authorization decision is replayable offline from signed events. We evaluate a Python prototype with a mixed-method design: ATAM quality analysis with a two-round mini-Delphi panel, STRIDE threat modeling across three trust boundaries, eleven benchmarks with non-parametric statistics, and 19 failure modes. Results show sub-millisecond offline verification, linear delegation-chain scaling, and Lightning-settled L402 at 157 ms median on regtest. 17 of 19 failure modes pass empirically, one is bounded analytically, and one is disclosed as an architectural limitation. NostrAgent demonstrates an auditable prototype substrate for trustworthy agentic systems without centralized trust roots.

</details>


### [108] [Diversity-Guided Search-Based Testing of Large Language Model Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23209)
*Lev Sorokin,Ivan Vasilev,Ken E. Friedl,Andrea Stocco*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper presents a search-based testing framework for LLM-based applications that explicitly optimizes for failure diversity. The framework uses discretization along stylistic, content-related, and perturbation dimensions, maintains an archive of generated tests, rewards distance from that archive, and uses a repopulation operator to sustain exploration. Evaluation across three case studies (LLM safety, in-car navigation, in-vehicle function control) with five systems, eight LLMs, and 18 configurations shows that guided variants outperform baselines, with greedy repopulation offering the best tradeoff between failure detection and diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based applications are increasingly deployed across domains but are prone to inaccurate, fictitious, or harmful responses. The vast, high-dimensional input space makes systematic testing particularly challenging, necessitating new testing approaches that can detect diverse failures.

Method: The framework uses: (1) discretization along stylistic, content-related, and perturbation dimensions; (2) an archive of generated tests that rewards distance from existing tests; (3) a repopulation operator that periodically replaces non-failing tests to sustain exploration. Two sampling strategies are compared: uniform sampling and greedy distance maximization.

Result: All guided variants detect substantially more failures than random and combinatorial search in nearly all configurations. Diversified search detects fewer failures but covers a broader range of failure types. Greedy repopulation offers the best tradeoff between failure detection and diversity coverage.

Conclusion: The proposed diversified search-based testing framework effectively identifies failures in LLM-based applications while maintaining failure diversity. The greedy repopulation strategy provides the best balance between the number of failures detected and the range of failure types covered.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM)-based applications are increasingly deployed across domains including customer service, education, and mobility. These systems are prone to inaccurate, fictitious, or harmful responses, and their vast, high-dimensional input space makes systematic testing particularly challenging. In this paper, we present a search-based testing framework for LLM-based applications that incorporates failure diversity as an explicit optimization objective. Building on a discretization along stylistic, content-related, and perturbation dimensions, our framework maintains an archive of generated tests and rewards distance from that archive, while a repopulation operator periodically replaces non-failing tests in the population to sustain exploration. The repopulation operator is parameterized by its sampling strategy: replacement candidates are drawn either uniformly or by greedy distance maximization, two settings we compare empirically.
  We evaluate both against several baselines across three case studies---LLM safety, in-car navigation, and in-vehicle function control---covering five systems under test, eight LLMs, and 18 distinct test configurations, with over one million executed tests. Our results show that all guided variants detect substantially more failures than random and combinatorial search in nearly all configurations. Among them, diversified search detects fewer failures, but covers a broader range of failure types in all case studies, with greedy repopulation offering the best tradeoff.

</details>


### [109] [Specification Before Generation: A Pre-Registered, Five-Model Paired Evaluation of a Specification Frame for LLM-Generated Code in Money, Time, Idempotency, and Access Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23270)
*Sandeep Dhuri*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether adding a specification (a fixed preamble stating what must be true of the result) to prompts can improve the quality of code generated by large language models. Through testing 50 backend tasks across five frontier models, the study found that a 267-word specification frame significantly reduced defects in all models, with the frame arm winning 95% of the time and Bandit security scanner finding 53 medium-or-high issues in bare prompts vs. only 11 with the specification frame.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Code generated by LLMs passes security checks at a rate that has barely moved in four years. In regulated backends (finance, healthcare, insurance), key defect classes include money arithmetic, time handling, retry safety, and access control. While teams have tried instruction files, the largest controlled study found no benefit. This paper tests a narrower idea: whether adding a specification to the prompt can improve generated code quality.

Method: The researchers pre-registered hypotheses, refuters, analysis code, and a one-shot generation rule. They ran 50 realistic backend tasks from finance, healthcare, and insurance through five frontier models from five vendor lineages. Each task was run twice: bare (no specification) and with a 267-word filled specification frame. Nine deterministic AST-based checkers scored the outputs, and the Bandit security scanner scored them independently.

Result: The specification frame reduced defects in all five models (mean reduction 0.16 to 0.70 findings per task). Every Holm-adjusted sign test was significant, and every bootstrap confidence interval excluded zero. Where the arms differed, the frame arm won 95 of 100 times. It never made any model worse in any domain. Bandit found 53 medium-or-high issues in the bare arm and 11 in the frame arm. The effect was largest where a model's unprompted defaults were weakest.

Conclusion: The specification frame supplies the discipline that a model lacks. All 500 outputs, prompts, checkers, scoring code, and the pre-registration are published with a DOI for reproducibility, allowing any team to re-derive the result without trusting the author.

Abstract: Code generated by large language models passes security checks at a rate that has barely moved in four years. In regulated backends, the defect classes that matter most are money arithmetic, time handling, retry safety, and access control. Teams answer with instruction files, yet the largest controlled study of instruction files to date found no benefit. This paper tests a narrower idea: generated code improves when the prompt carries a specification, a fixed preamble stating what must be true of the result. We pre-registered hypotheses, refuters, analysis code, and a one-shot generation rule, then ran 50 realistic backend tasks from finance, healthcare, and insurance practice through five frontier models from five vendor lineages, each task twice: bare, and preceded by a 267-word filled specification frame. Nine deterministic AST-based checkers scored the outputs. The Bandit security scanner, which knows nothing of the frame, scored them independently. The frame reduced defects in all five models (mean reduction 0.16 to 0.70 findings per task, every Holm-adjusted sign test significant, every bootstrap confidence interval excluding zero). Where the arms differed, the frame arm won 95 of 100 times. It never made any model worse in any domain. Bandit found 53 medium-or-high issues in the bare arm and 11 in the frame arm, in the same direction for every model. The effect was largest where a model's unprompted defaults were weakest: the frame supplies the discipline a model lacks. All 500 outputs, prompts, checkers, scoring code, and the pre-registration are published with a DOI, so any team can re-derive the result without trusting the author.

</details>


### [110] [One to More, More to One: Category-Aware Iterative Expert Training for Software Engineering Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23377)
*Jie Zhao,Ziyu Jiang,Suhang Zheng,Minghui Shan,Xiaoxiao Xu,Lin Qu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses the "category see-saw" problem in repository-level software engineering (SWE) where pooled agentic reinforcement learning causes uneven progress across heterogeneous task categories. The authors propose a category-aware expert-training and policy-integration framework that uses category-specific RL, Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE) training, and label-routed multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) to consolidate multiple category experts into a single deployable model. The final MOPD policy achieves 58.04% resolution on Pro-618 and 59.00% on SWE-bench Multilingual, representing improvements of 5.39 and 2.78 percentage points over the base model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In repository-level software engineering, pooled agentic reinforcement learning leads to uneven progress across heterogeneous task categories: gains in some categories coincide with regressions in others, while aggregate resolution metrics obscure these instance-level changes. This "category see-saw" problem motivates the need for category-aware training approaches.

Method: The framework consists of: (1) Executable task construction and SWE Labeler for evidence-grounded multi-axis labeling to organize training pools; (2) Initial category-specific RL to improve average training success; (3) Same-origin category experts using long-horizon Agentic-miniRL with Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE) - where the updated policy refreshes instance mastery, reuses verified successful trajectories for Repair SFT, and reselects tasks for further RL; (4) Label-routed multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) to consolidate experts into one deployable student, with ReLU-gated reward extrapolation keeping only each teacher's improving direction over the reference. The approach requires no external model for solution trajectories or action targets.

Result: The final MOPD policy achieves mean resolution of 58.04% on Pro-618 and 59.00% on SWE-bench Multilingual, improving over the base model by 5.39 and 2.78 percentage points respectively. Evaluation includes aggregate and per-category resolution, minimum category lift over joint-RL baselines, and expert-gain recovery metrics.

Conclusion: The category-aware expert-training and policy-integration framework successfully addresses the category see-saw problem in SWE tasks. By combining category-specific RL with RRE training and MOPD distillation, the approach achieves meaningful improvements across heterogeneous task categories without requiring external models for guidance.

Abstract: Repository-level software engineering (SWE) comprises heterogeneous task categories, whose progress under pooled agentic reinforcement learning can be uneven: gains in some categories coincide with regressions in others, while aggregate resolution obscures these changes. Motivated by this category see-saw, we develop a category-aware expert-training and policy-integration framework. Executable task construction and SWE Labeler, an evidence-grounded multi-axis labeling system, organize the training pools. Initial category-specific RL improves average training success while leaving uneven instance-level progress, motivating explicit consolidation of successful behavior and policy-adaptive task selection. Same-origin category experts alternate long-horizon Agentic-miniRL with Refresh-Repair-Expand (RRE): the updated policy refreshes instance mastery, reuses its own verified successful trajectories for Repair SFT, and reselects tasks for further RL. Label-routed multi-teacher on-policy distillation (MOPD) consolidates the experts into one deployable student, with ReLU-gated reward extrapolation keeping only each teacher's improving direction over the reference. Expert training and policy integration require no external model to provide solution trajectories or action targets. We evaluate Pooled RL and Balanced RL, expert development, and single-model integration through aggregate and per-category resolution, the minimum category lift over each joint-RL baseline, and expert-gain recovery. The final MOPD policy achieves mean resolution of 58.04% on Pro-618 and 59.00% on SWE-bench Multilingual, improving over the base model by 5.39 and 2.78 percentage points, respectively.

</details>


### [111] [Omni2Web: Benchmarking Audiovisual Website Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23417)
*Minghao Han,Zhenghao Xing,Xize Cheng,Yuxuan Wang,Junming Lin,Ling Wang,Yinsong Yan,Yunfei Chu,Qize Yang,Jin Xu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Omni2Web is a bilingual benchmark for evaluating multimodal intent recovery in web editing tasks from screen recordings. It contains 918 instances with 13,907 edit steps across three tracks: Direct Editing, Instruction Recovery, and Instruction Utility. The benchmark addresses the challenge of weak deictic expressions in speech-recorded web editing requests.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Screen-recorded web editing requests contain ambiguous deictic expressions (e.g., "this," "there") whose referents depend on speech, cursor trajectories, page state, and edit history. Existing web-editing benchmarks assume explicit specifications, failing to handle these real-world ambiguous requests that require intent recovery beyond explicit text.

Method: The authors created a bilingual benchmark (Omni2Web) with three complementary evaluation tracks: (1) Direct Editing evaluates models' ability to edit webpages from recordings, (2) Instruction Recovery measures explicit intent recovery, and (3) Instruction Utility tests whether recovered instructions can drive a fixed code executor. They evaluated 17 open- and closed-source models using metrics including Edit Fidelity Score (EFS) and Instruction Recovery Score (IRS). Controlled ablations were conducted to assess the value of temporally aligned audiovisual evidence.

Result: The best models achieved 51.17 EFS on Direct Editing and 49.14 IRS on Instruction Recovery. With a fixed executor, the strongest recovered instructions reached 51.08 EFS, far below the 89.69 EFS achieved with oracle instructions. Key findings: (1) correct grounding does not guarantee successful edits, (2) some Omni models recover instructions that fixed coding models execute substantially better than direct edits, (3) temporally aligned audiovisual evidence is valuable, and (4) substantial headroom remains in multimodal intent recovery and code execution.

Conclusion: There is significant room for improvement in multimodal intent recovery and code execution for web editing tasks. The study highlights the promise of pairing Omni rewriters with coding models, as some recovered instructions outperform direct edits when executed by fixed coding models. The benchmark reveals that current models struggle with grounding deictic expressions in multimodal contexts, and future work should focus on better integrating audiovisual evidence with intent recovery.

Abstract: Screen-recorded web editing requests contain weak deictic expressions such as ``this'' and ``there,'' whose referents depend on speech, cursor trajectories, page state, and edit history. Such requests require intent recovery beyond the explicit specifications assumed by many existing web-editing benchmarks. We introduce Omni2Web, a bilingual benchmark of 918 instances spanning 13,907 edit steps. It defines three complementary tracks: Direct Editing evaluates webpage editing from recordings, Instruction Recovery measures explicit intent recovery, and Instruction Utility tests whether recovered instructions can drive a fixed code executor. We evaluate 17 open- and closed-source models. The best models attain 51.17 on the Edit Fidelity Score (EFS) for Direct Editing and 49.14 on the Instruction Recovery Score (IRS); under the fixed executor, the strongest recovered instructions reach 51.08 EFS, still far below the 89.69 EFS obtained with oracle instructions. Step-level analyses show that correct grounding does not guarantee successful edits, while some Omni models recover instructions that the fixed coding model executes substantially better than their direct edits. Controlled ablations further demonstrate the value of temporally aligned audiovisual evidence, while alternative judges preserve the leader and broad ordering. Together, these findings reveal substantial headroom in multimodal intent recovery and code execution and highlight the promise of pairing Omni rewriters with coding models.

</details>


### [112] [VSpector: Specification-Driven Bug Detection for RISC-V CPUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23517)
*Tianyu Jia,Zhaoyang Yu,Yuanliang Chen,Wei You,Jianjun Huang,Bin Liang*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VSpector is a specification-driven bug detection pipeline that uses official RISC-V specifications and LLMs to detect RTL design bugs in RISC-V CPU implementations without requiring predefined artifacts like reference models or formal properties. It achieves 68.2% precision, finding 42 previously unknown bugs that state-of-the-art fuzzers missed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional CPU bug detection approaches rely on predefined artifacts (reference models, formal properties, custom bug patterns). This paper proposes leveraging official natural-language RISC-V specifications as an alternative information source for bug detection, enabling detection without specialized construction of these artifacts.

Method: VSpector employs a four-stage pipeline with stepwise context refinement to address the trade-off between broad context scope and LLM reasoning accuracy: (1) Rule extraction from RISC-V specifications, (2) Implementation localization to relevant RTL code, (3) Candidate identification for potential violations, and (4) Sequential violation auditing. The approach uses LLMs to check whether RTL implementations adhere to official specification rules.

Result: Evaluated on two industrial-strength RISC-V CPUs (CVA6 and XiangShan): 217 reported candidates, 148 true violations confirmed (68.2% precision). These correspond to 73 distinct bugs, including 42 previously unknown bugs. Comparative experiments showed DiveFuzz (state-of-the-art CPU fuzzer) detected none of these new bugs during 24-hour runs per CPU. All 42 new bugs reported upstream; 19 fixed and 11 confirmed (30 total).

Conclusion: Specification-driven auditing using official RISC-V specifications and LLMs is a practical and complementary strategy for CPU bug detection. It can detect bugs that state-of-the-art fuzzers miss, demonstrating the value of leveraging natural-language specifications as an effective information source for bug detection without requiring specialized reference models or formal properties.

Abstract: Detecting RTL design bugs in open-source RISC-V CPU implementations is critical for ensuring system reliability. Traditional detection approaches inherently rely on predefined artifacts. In this paper, we leverage the official,natural-language RISC-V specifications as an effective information source for bug detection. We present VSpector, a specification-driven bug detection pipeline that directly checks whether CPU register-transfer level (RTL) implementations adhere to official specification rules, without requiring specialized construction of reference models, formal properties, or custom bug patterns. To resolve the key technical trade-off between broad context scope and model reasoning accuracy when using Large Language Models (LLMs), VSpector employs a stepwise context refinement scheme across a four-stage pipeline: rule extraction, implementation localization, candidate identification, and sequential violation auditing. We evaluate VSpector on two industrial-strength RISC-V CPUs, CVA6 and XiangShan. Out of 217 reported candidates, manual inspection confirmed 148 true violations, representing a 68.2% precision. These violations correspond to 73 distinct bugs, including 42 previously unknown bugs. In our comparative experiments, DiveFuzz, a state-of-the-art CPU fuzzer, detected none of these new bugs during 24-hour runs per CPU. All 42 new bugs have been reported upstream, with developers already fixing 19 and confirming an additional 11 (30 in total), demonstrating that specification-driven auditing is a practical and complementary strategy for CPU bug detection.

</details>


### [113] [VibeMemBench: Evaluating Memory Systems for Coding Agents on Real Repository Coding Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23570)
*Liyang Fan,Yingcheng Shi,Yongbin Li,Chenghao Sun,Xin Chen,Xander Xu,Hu Wei,Shiwen Ni,Min Yang,Jieping Ye*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: VibeMemBench is a benchmark for evaluating memory systems on 111 coding targets from 90 SWE-rebench V2 repositories. It tests whether memory systems can improve executable coding outcomes. The benchmark finds that while verified experience injection improves task resolution by 1.1-4.5 percentage points, four existing memory systems fail to exceed baseline in 11 of 12 solver-system pairings, exposing a gap between useful repository history and what memory systems actually deliver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations don't show whether memory systems improve executable repository work. Repository benchmarks test code changes but don't isolate memory, while memory benchmarks score recall without measuring downstream coding outcomes. There is a need to evaluate whether memory systems actually improve coding performance on real repository tasks.

Method: VibeMemBench uses 111 coding targets from 90 SWE-rebench V2 repositories with 3,634 history trajectories. Targets cover bug fixes, feature requests, interface changes, and configuration work. An agent edits each target under a declared memory condition. Executable tests decide task resolution. Targets are retained only when injected history experience improves executable outcome in a reference setting. The verified experience is transferred to five held-out solvers.

Result: Direct injection of verified experience raises observed task resolution on four of five solvers by 1.1 to 4.5 percentage points while lowering agent steps on all five. However, when four existing memory systems must construct and retrieve experience from the same history, eleven of twelve solver and system pairings fail to exceed the matched memory-off baseline.

Conclusion: VibeMemBench exposes a significant gap between the useful experience that repository history holds and the experience existing memory systems deliver for repository coding tasks. While verified experience can improve outcomes, current memory systems fail to effectively leverage repository history for coding tasks.

Abstract: Coding agents operate on real repository coding tasks, and persistent memory systems promise to reuse experience across tasks. Yet existing evaluations do not show whether those systems improve executable repository work. Repository benchmarks test code changes but do not isolate memory, while memory benchmarks score recall without measuring downstream coding outcomes. We introduce VibeMemBench, a benchmark for evaluating memory systems on 111 coding targets from 90 SWE-rebench V2 repositories and 3,634 history trajectories from the target repositories. The targets follow the SWE benchmark style and cover bug fixes, feature requests, interface changes, and configuration work. An agent edits each target codebase under a declared memory condition. Executable tests decide task resolution. Each target is retained only when injected history experience improves its executable outcome in a reference setting, so every target carries a prior experience whose usefulness is verified by execution in that setting. The frozen verified experience is then transferred to five held-out solvers. Direct injection raises observed task resolution on four of them by 1.1 to 4.5 percentage points while lowering agent steps on all five. Yet when four existing memory systems must construct and retrieve experience from the same history, eleven of twelve solver and system pairings fail to exceed the matched memory-off baseline. VibeMemBench exposes the gap between the useful experience that repository history holds and the experience existing memory systems deliver for repository coding tasks.

</details>


### [114] [Packaged, But Not Portable: Why Conforming to the Agent Plugin Standard Is Rare, and Why Conforming Would Not Be Enough](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23809)
*Tezan Sahu*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper studies the adoption of the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 open specification (standardized July 2026) by building a large corpus of 68,072 plugin bundles across 30,655 repositories. It answers two questions: (1) is the ecosystem adopting the standard? Only 6.2% validate, but 96.6% would load after adding one missing boilerplate field. (2) Would conforming plugins work together? No - 81% share names with no namespace/precedence rules, and 40.2% would load while client discards author-written fields. The paper proposes four concepts for a composition model and an additive v1.1 profile.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors seek to answer two practical questions: (1) Is the plugin ecosystem adopting the Agent Plugins v1.0.0 standard? (2) If a plugin conforms to the standard, would it work alongside other installed plugins? These are practical concerns for practitioners adopting the standard.

Method: The authors built "AgentPluginZoo" - a provenance-tracked corpus of 68,072 plugin bundles across 30,655 repositories. They developed discovery ledger, scoring code, and analysis tools to evaluate conformance and compatibility.

Result: Only 6.2% of plugins validate against the standard. However, the gap is shallow: 96.6% would load after adding one missing boilerplate field. For compatibility: 40.2% would load while the specification requires clients to discard fields authors wrote (capability declarations). Additionally, 81% of capability-exporting bundles share names with other plugins, with no namespace or precedence rule to resolve conflicts.

Conclusion: The community standardized a packaging format when composition needs a model. The paper identifies four concepts a composition model must add: qualified capability identity, declared capability surface, precedence rule, and inter-plugin relations. These can fit an additive v1.1 profile of the same specification. Key recommendation: conformance must be made observable before it can become common. The corpus and code are available on GitHub.

Abstract: Coding agents are extended by plugins: installable bundles that ship skills, sub-agents, commands, hooks, and tool servers. On 24 July 2026, an open specification (Agent Plugins v1.0.0) standardised how such a bundle is laid out and described, so that one plugin could run on any agent. We ask the two questions a practitioner would ask of it: is the ecosystem adopting the standard, and if a plugin did conform, would that be enough to make it work alongside the other plugins a user has installed?
  We answer both by building AgentPluginZoo, a provenance-tracked corpus of 68,072 plugin bundles across 30,655 repositories, released with its discovery ledger, scoring code, and analysis. Only 6.2% validate, but the gap is shallow rather than structural: 96.6% would load after adding one missing boilerplate field. The real cost lands elsewhere. 40.2% would load while the specification obliges the client to discard fields their authors wrote, mostly declarations of what the plugin ships. Moreover, conformance settles nothing for the second question: 81% of capability-exporting bundles share a name with another plugin, with no namespace or precedence rule to decide which one answers.
  This paper argues the community standardised a packaging format when composition needs a model, names the four concepts such a model must add - qualified capability identity, a declared capability surface, a precedence rule, and inter-plugin relations - and shows they fit an additive v1.1 profile of the same specification rather than a competing standard. Recommendations follow for practitioners packaging extensions today and for the people evolving the standard, chief among them that conformance must be made observable before it can become common. The corpus and code are available at https://github.com/tezansahu/agentpluginzoo

</details>


### [115] [MCPGen: Benchmarking LLMs on Executable MCPWorkflow Development](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23925)
*Yingxuan Yang,Jiaqi Liu,Lirui Guan,Jiaye Gao,Weiwen Liu,Weinan Zhang,Ying Wen*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper introduces MCPGen, an executable benchmark for evaluating LLMs' ability to generate consistent and executable Model Context Protocol (MCP) workflow artifacts across multiple layers (graph structure, tool implementation, schema bindings, runtime wiring). The benchmark contains 100 MCP projects across 16 domains and evaluates three tasks: workflow reconstruction, tool creation, and backward-compatible workflow extension. Evaluation of 11 LLMs reveals a significant gap between structural correctness (88.5%) and end-to-end execution success (max 57%), highlighting integration as a major bottleneck.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate LLM capabilities for workflow generation in isolation or use trajectory-level proxies, leaving open whether generated workflow artifacts can actually execute end-to-end. Cross-layer consistency is critical - a workflow may appear structurally plausible but fail due to misaligned tool implementations, schema bindings, or runtime execution.

Method: MCPGen benchmark with 100 self-contained MCP projects across 16 application domains. Evaluates 11 representative LLMs in a single-turn foundation-model setting across three diagnostic tasks: workflow reconstruction, tool creation, and backward-compatible workflow extension. Assessment through static analysis, unit and integration tests, and process-isolated end-to-end execution.

Result: Models achieve 88.5% on workflow reconstruction but no model exceeds 57% end-to-end execution success. Per-tool unit-test pass rates reach 63.8%, while project-level integration success does not exceed 45%. This reveals a significant gap between isolated component correctness and integrated system execution.

Conclusion: While LLMs can generate structurally correct workflow components, integration remains a major bottleneck. Even when isolated tool tests pass, project-level integration success is limited to 45%. This suggests that current LLMs struggle with maintaining cross-layer consistency required for end-to-end executable workflow artifacts, indicating a key challenge for LLM-based workflow generation systems.

Abstract: We study whether LLMs can produce executable workflow artifacts that remain consistent across graph structure, tool implementation, schema bindings, and runtime wiring. In this setting, correctness depends on cross-layer consistency: a workflow may be structurally plausible, yet still fail because tool implementations, schema bindings, or runtime execution do not align. Existing benchmarks largely evaluate these capabilities in isolation or rely on trajectory-level proxies, leaving open whether generated workflow artifacts execute end-to-end. We introduce \textbf{MCPGen}, an executable benchmark for Model Context Protocol (MCP) workflow development. MCPGen contains 100 self-contained MCP projects across 16 application domains and evaluates three diagnostic tasks: workflow reconstruction, tool creation, and backward-compatible workflow extension. We evaluate 11 representative LLMs in a single-turn foundation-model setting, assessing generated artifacts through static analysis, unit and integration tests, and process-isolated end-to-end execution. Models reach 88.5\% on workflow reconstruction, but no model exceeds 57\% end-to-end execution success. Per-tool unit-test pass rates reach 63.8\%, while project-level integration success does not exceed 45\%, suggesting that integration remains a major bottleneck even when isolated tool tests pass.

</details>


### [116] [A Carbon-Aware Quantum Computing Framework for LCA-Driven Sustainability in Quantum Cloud Services](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24224)
*Muhammad Umar,Nauman Arshad,Azeem Akbar,Arif Ali Khan*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study extends carbon-aware quantum computing (CQC) framework from platform-level to service-level, using LCA findings of a superconducting quantum computer to provide actionable guidance for Quantum Cloud Service (QCS) providers. The five-year footprint is 583 t CO2e (GKP) and 10,570 t (surface-code), dominated by embodied carbon (77.3-85.2%).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantum computing's environmental footprint is poorly understood relative to classical infrastructure. As quantum computing moves toward cloud delivery, QCS providers lack actionable guidance beyond platform-level carbon-accounting frameworks. This study addresses this gap by translating empirical LCA findings into service-level guidance.
