Method: The study modeled the CQC framework via: (1) service-level embodied-carbon allocation, (2) load-independent and load-proportional operational decomposition, and (3) workload-resolved application offset, based on cradle-to-grave LCA of a superconducting quantum platform.

Result: The five-year footprint is 583 t CO2e (GKP) and 10,570 t (surface-code), dominated by embodied carbon (77.3-85.2%). Operational-embodied parity is not reached until 17.0-28.7 years versus 2.7 years for classical comparators. Provider levers analysis shows: utilisation yields the largest gain (19.7x), followed by service life extension (59.9%) and electricity supply (6.5x), while operational efficiency and renewable procurement offer limited leverage.

Conclusion: Superconducting quantum computers are structurally embodied-carbon-dominated, inverting classical sustainability intuition. This motivates direct power measurement and cross-architecture validation as quantum infrastructure scales.

Abstract: Quantum computing's environmental footprint remains poorly understood relative to classical infrastructure, and as quantum computing moves toward cloud delivery, Quantum Cloud Service (QCS) providers lack actionable guidance beyond platform-level carbon-accounting frameworks. Objective: This study extends the carbon-aware quantum computing (CQC) framework from a platform-level to a service-level model that translates empirical life cycle assessment (LCA) findings of a superconducting quantum computer into guidance for QCS providers. Method: We modeled the CQC framework via service-level embodied-carbon allocation, load-independent and load-proportional operational decomposition, and a workload-resolved application offset on the basis of results acquired through a cradle-to-grave LCA of a superconducting quantum platform. Results: The five-year footprint is 583 t CO2e (GKP) and 10,570 t (surface-code), dominated by embodied carbon (77.3-85.2%), with operational-embodied parity not reached until 17.0-28.7 years versus 2.7 years for classical comparators. This reorders provider levers: utilisation yields the largest gain (19.7x), followed by service life extension (59.9%) and electricity supply (6.5x), while operational efficiency and renewable procurement offer limited leverage. Conclusion: Superconducting quantum computers are structurally embodied-carbon-dominated, inverting classical sustainability intuition and motivating direct power measurement and cross-architecture validation as quantum infrastructure scales.

</details>


### [117] [Fault-Class-Matched Test Oracles for Output-Invisible Quantum Transpiler Regressions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24230)
*Furqan Nasir,Arif Shah,Iftikhar Alam*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical gap in quantum transpiler testing: existing output-equivalence oracles miss ~28% of bugs that corrupt layout metadata, global phase, or reproducibility while output remains correct. The authors propose a fault-class-matched oracle family including a layout/permutation contract checker, a global-phase tracker, and a determinism runner. Verified on nine real Qiskit regressions with 1.00 sensitivity/specificity and successfully ported to pytket/tket, demonstrating cross-tool validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current quantum transpiler testing oracles (statevector, sampled distribution, or unitary comparison) are blind to faults that corrupt layout metadata, global phase, or run-to-run reproducibility while output remains correct. An empirical study shows ~28% of merged Qiskit transpiler bug-fixes repair such "invisible" faults, creating a significant testing gap.

Method: The authors developed three specialized oracles: (1) layout/permutation contract checker with metamorphic relation (MR-1) for metadata channel, (2) global-phase tracker for phase channel, and (3) determinism runner for reproducibility. They verified these on nine real merged Qiskit regressions (three per channel), performed a 675-configuration sweep for false positives, and conducted synthetic mutant testing (36 mutants each for contract/metadata and global-phase channels). The approach was ported to pytket/tket for cross-tool validation.

Result: The output-equivalence oracle is blind to all nine regressions, while the matched mechanism fires on every case. The contract checker costs 2-6 orders of magnitude less than plain output check. The global-phase tracker is comparably cheap within its exact tier. The metamorphic relation carries bounded cost. Synthetic mutants confirm 1.00 sensitivity and specificity across 36 mutants for contract/metadata and global-phase channels, and 1.00 sensitivity for reproducibility on three constructible circuits. Ported to pytket/tket, the global-phase mechanism achieves identical 1.00/1.00 result with phases recovered to double-precision accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed fault-class-matched oracle family effectively closes the testing gap for quantum transpilers. The high sensitivity/specificity results and successful cross-tool transfer to pytket/tket demonstrate the approach's generalizability beyond Qiskit-specific artifacts. The low overhead (2-6 orders of magnitude cheaper than output checking) makes practical deployment feasible.

Abstract: Test oracles for quantum transpilers typically judge correctness by comparing compiled output against a reference: a statevector, a sampled distribution, or a unitary compared modulo global phase. A companion empirical study measures how often that choice fails. Roughly 28% of merged Qiskit transpiler bug-fixes (95% Wilson CI 19-40%) repair a fault that corrupts layout metadata, global phase, or run-to-run reproducibility while output stays correct: invisible to a black-box output-equivalence oracle by construction. This paper closes that gap with a fault-class-matched, layout-aware, width-tiered oracle family: a layout/permutation contract checker and a contract-level metamorphic relation (MR-1) for the metadata channel, a global-phase tracker for the phase channel, and a determinism runner for reproducibility. Verified from source on nine real, merged Qiskit transpiler regressions (three per channel), the output-equivalence oracle is blind throughout and the matched mechanism fires on every case. A 675-configuration sweep of the contract/metadata invariant finds no false positive. Synthetic mutant families confirm reliability at scale: 1.00 sensitivity and specificity across 36 mutants apiece for the contract/metadata and global-phase channels, and 1.00 sensitivity (95% CI 0.44-1.00) for reproducibility on the three circuits where the mutation is constructible. The contract checker costs two to six orders of magnitude less than a plain output check, the global-phase tracker is comparably cheap within its exact tier, and only the metamorphic relation carries a bounded cost. Ported natively to pytket/tket, the global-phase mechanism transfers cleanly, an identical 1.00/1.00 result with phases recovered to double-precision accuracy, evidence against a Qiskit-specific artifact.

</details>


### [118] [Evaluating the effectiveness of class-level LLM-generated test suites in Python](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24341)
*Bilal Al-Ahmad,M. Harshvardhan,Khaled El-Fakih,Anas AlSobeh*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: This study evaluates how prompt strategy and model choice affect the quality of LLM-generated Python test suites, finding that model selection matters more than prompt tuning, and that executability should be treated as a prerequisite for reliable assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can generate unit tests quickly, but high structural coverage does not guarantee reliable execution or effective fault detection. Existing research treats coverage as the primary metric and rarely compares prompt strategies and models through mutation testing at the class level.

Method: Evaluated multiple prompt strategies across diverse LLM configurations on the ClassEval benchmark. Combined execution outcomes, line and branch coverage, Cosmic Ray mutation scores, and structural quality indicators. Primary analyses treated successful execution as a prerequisite; paired comparisons used only classes shared by executable subsets.

Result: Structural coverage is consistently near ceiling and offers little discrimination. Executability varies substantially across configurations. The proposed prompt performs strongly for mutation score, but no prompt dominates across all models. Model choice explains more variation than prompt choice, and their interaction shows prompt effectiveness depends on the selected model. Human and LLM suites are evaluated on unequal executable subsets, so relative mutation scores do not establish superiority.

Conclusion: Reliable assessment of LLM-generated tests should treat executability as a gate and combine coverage with mutation testing and structural quality indicators. In practice, model selection should precede prompt tuning.

Abstract: Context: Large language models (LLMs) can generate unit tests quickly, but high structural coverage does not establish that those tests execute reliably or detect faults. Existing evidence often treats coverage as the principal outcome and rarely compares prompt strategies and models through mutation testing at class level. Objective: This study examines how prompt strategy and model choice shape the executability, structural coverage, fault-detection effectiveness, and structural quality of LLM-generated Python test suites relative to human-written suites. Method: We evaluate multiple prompt strategies across a diverse set of current LLM configurations on the ClassEval benchmark. The evaluation combines execution outcomes, line and branch coverage, Cosmic Ray mutation scores, and structural quality indicators. Primary analyses treat successful execution as a prerequisite; paired comparisons use only classes shared by the relevant executable subsets. Results: Structural coverage is consistently near its ceiling and offers little discrimination among configurations. Executability varies substantially. The proposed prompt performs strongly for mutation score, but no prompt dominates across models. Model choice explains more variation than prompt choice, and their interaction shows that prompt effectiveness depends on the selected model. Human and LLM suites are evaluated on unequal executable subsets, so their relative mutation scores do not establish superiority. Conclusion: Reliable assessment of LLM-generated tests should treat executability as a gate and combine coverage with mutation testing and structural quality indicators. In practice, model selection should precede prompt tuning.

</details>


### [119] [A Lean and Spec-Driven AI-Assisted Software Development Lifecycle for Applied AI Education: The AI-SDLC Approach](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24348)
*Andreas Martin,Sandro Schwander*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: Summary generation failed


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Motivation analysis unavailable

Method: Method extraction failed

Result: Result analysis unavailable

Conclusion: Conclusion extraction failed

Abstract: AI coding agents increasingly support software development beyond code completion, including planning, implementation, testing, and repository-level task execution. Their practical use, however, often remains only weakly connected to established software engineering practices. The aim of this work is to develop and evaluate a lightweight, spec-driven lifecycle for governed agentic software engineering. The lifecycle combines established software engineering practices with repository-local guidance through specifications, AGENTS.md, and phase-specific agent skill files. The approach was developed in the context of the FHNW course AI-assisted Software Development and applied by students to business-oriented software use cases. Its educational and practical applicability is explored through a student survey combining closed rating items with open-ended questions. The contribution of this work is a process-oriented framework that enables AI coding agents to operate with bounded autonomy within an explicit, reviewable, and test-oriented software development lifecycle.

</details>


### [120] [Trajectory-Aware Benchmark Subset Selection for Cost-Efficient Software Engineering Agent Regression Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24928)
*Mahmoud Ayyad,Zehao Wang,Jiho Shin,Ying Zou,Bram Adams*

Main category: cs.SE

TL;DR: A trajectory-aware subset selection method for evaluating SWE-agents that uses trajectory embeddings to select representative benchmark instances, reducing token costs by ~90% while keeping median estimation error below 5%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluating autonomous software engineering agents requires re-running full benchmarks at enormous cost (hundreds of millions of LLM tokens), making evaluation a bottleneck. Simple random or stratified sampling approaches risk high variance and unrepresentative subsets.

Method: The approach groups test set instances by test outcome (pass/fail) from a recent full test run to preserve historical pass/fail rates, then selects subsets using trajectory embeddings by choosing instances closest to the centroid of each outcome group in the embedding space. Evaluated 76 configurations across random, embedding-based, clustering-based, and hybrid strategies across three regression scenarios.

Result: The centroid-based trajectory-aware method achieves the lowest estimation error. It reduces average estimation error by 3-11% and worst-case error by 4-11% relative to typical draws, and 38-46% relative to 95th-percentile draws of strongest baselines. A 10% subset keeps median estimation error below 5% while cutting token cost by roughly 90%.

Conclusion: Trajectory-aware subset selection using embedding space is effective for efficient evaluation of SWE-agents, achieving significant cost reduction (~90%) with acceptable accuracy loss (median error <5%).

Abstract: Autonomous software engineering agents (SWE-agents) automate coding tasks. Each agent update may require re-running the full benchmark to detect regressions and improvements, at a cost of hundreds of millions of LLM tokens per run, which makes evaluation a bottleneck. One solution is to evaluate only a subset of benchmark instances. Yet, simple approaches, such as random sampling or stratified random sampling based on past pass/fail outcomes, risk producing high variance and unrepresentative subsets. We turn to agent trajectories, the step-by-step record of the actions an agent took. We propose a trajectory-aware subset selection approach that replaces random sampling with deterministic selection based on trajectory embeddings. We first group test set instances by their test outcome in a recent full test run to preserve the historical pass/fail rate, then select the subset using the trajectory's embedding space.
  We evaluate 76 subset selection configurations, including random sampling, embedding-based selection, clustering-based selection, and hybrid shortlist-then-subsample strategies, across three regression scenarios: same-configuration reruns, model and configuration changes, and agent framework changes. Our best trajectory-aware method is the one selecting benchmark instances closest to the centroid of each outcome group in the embedding space. It achieves the lowest estimation error among all methods we evaluate. For instance, when evaluating a given agent version on a selected subset of 5% or 10% of the test instances, our approach reduces the average estimation error by 3--11% and the worst-case error by 4--11% relative to the typical draw and 38--46% relative to the 95th-percentile draw of the strongest baseline. Our results show that a 10% trajectory-aware subset keeps the median estimation error below 5% while cutting token cost by roughly 90%.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [121] [Didactic knowledge or Clinical Cases? How Data Types Shape Medical Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22161)
*Yuzheng Fan,Haochun Wang,Sendong Zhao,Xiao Han,Ming Ma,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study investigates how different ratios of didactic (textbooks) vs clinical (patient records) training data affect medical LLM performance across knowledge-intensive and clinic-oriented tasks, revealing asymmetric transfer effects and a "knowing-doing gap" between knowledge recall and clinical reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical LLMs are trained on mixed data sources, but the differential impact of didactic vs clinical data on model capabilities remains unclear, creating a gap in understanding how data composition shapes model performance.

Method: Token-matched experiments that systematically vary the didactic-to-clinical ratio in training data, analyzing performance, capability profiles, and error patterns across knowledge-intensive and clinic-oriented tasks.

Result: Clinical data improves clinic-oriented tasks while remaining competitive on knowledge-intensive tasks; didactic data mainly improves knowledge-intensive tasks. A "knowing-doing gap" exists where knowledge improvements don't generalize to clinical reasoning. Modest clinical data yields most EHR-grounded task gains, but optimal mixture ratios vary by task demands.

Conclusion: Medical LLM data curation should be application-driven, with higher proportions of clinical data preferred for reasoning-intensive use cases.

Abstract: Medical large language models are commonly trained on mixtures of didactic data (e.g., textbooks) and clinical data (e.g., patient records), yet how these data types differentially shape model capabilities remains unclear. We address this issue with token-matched experiments that vary the didactic-to-clinical ratio and analyze how data composition affects performance, capability profiles, and error patterns across knowledge-intensive and clinic-oriented tasks. We uncover an asymmetric transfer across task types: clinical data improves clinic-oriented tasks while remaining competitive on knowledge-intensive ones, whereas didactic data mainly improves knowledge-intensive tasks. Error analysis suggests a knowing-doing gap, where improvements in knowledge recall do not reliably generalize to clinical reasoning. We further observe that modest amounts of clinical data yield most of the gains on EHR-grounded tasks, while the optimal mixture ratio varies with the knowledge and clinical reasoning demands of downstream tasks. These findings suggest that medical LLM data curation should be application-driven, with higher proportions of clinical data preferred for reasoning-intensive use cases.

</details>


### [122] [An Affordable AI-Integrated Smart Cane for Multimodal Mobility Assistance of Visually Impaired Users](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22277)
*Ali Akarma,Adeel Ahmad,Toqeer Ali Syed*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An affordable ($88) fully offline AI-integrated smart cane for visually impaired individuals, using Raspberry Pi Zero 2W with RGB+ToF sensors, quantized SSD MobileNet V1, and multimodal feedback (vibrotactile + audio).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Over 2.2 billion people have visual impairment worldwide. Conventional white canes cannot detect elevated hazards or provide semantic context. Existing AI systems require expensive hardware or cloud connectivity, limiting accessibility in resource-constrained settings.

Method: The system uses RGB vision fused with ToF distance estimation, paired with an INT8-quantized SSD MobileNet V1 model. Multimodal feedback includes distance-aware vibrotactile feedback and real-time audio alerts. A multiprocessing architecture isolates sensor acquisition, neural inference, and haptic feedback into independent processes with fail-safe sensing support.

Result: Macro-averaged F1-score: 0.82 (precision: 0.85, recall: 0.81). Mean end-to-end latency: 330ms. Peak power draw: 2.8W. Preliminary usability study (12 participants): SUS score 78.5.

Conclusion: The prototype validates the feasibility of deploying privacy-preserving, edge-native assistive intelligence for cost-sensitive mobility assistance at an affordable price point ($88).

Abstract: Visual impairment affects over 2.2 billion people worldwide, yet conventional white canes cannot detect elevated hazards or provide semantic environmental context. Existing AI-assisted navigation systems typically rely on expensive hardware or cloud connectivity, limiting accessibility in resource-constrained settings. This paper presents an affordable (\$88 USD), fully offline AI-integrated smart cane designed for multimodal mobility assistance on an ultra-low-power Raspberry Pi Zero 2W. The system fuses RGB vision sensing with Time-of-Flight (ToF) distance estimation, pairing an INT8-quantized SSD MobileNet V1 model with distance-aware vibrotactile feedback and real-time audio alerts. To ensure operational robustness on constrained hardware, a multiprocessing architecture isolates sensor acquisition, neural inference, and haptic feedback into independent processes with fail-safe sensing support. Experimental evaluation across indoor mobility scenarios demonstrates a macro-averaged F1-score of 0.82 (precision: 0.85, recall: 0.81), a mean end-to-end latency of 330\,ms, and a peak power draw of 2.8\,W. A preliminary usability study with 12 participants (SUS: 78.5, NASA-TLX) demonstrated positive user perception and enhanced obstacle awareness. The proposed prototype validates the feasibility of deploying privacy-preserving, edge-native assistive intelligence for cost-sensitive mobility assistance.

</details>


### [123] [PAANI : On Device Visual Evidence Fusion and Explainable Guidance for River Robot Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22353)
*Savio Cardoz,Santhiya Rajan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PAANI is an on-device perception-to-guidance architecture for mobile river monitoring robots that combines YOLO11n object detection and MobileNetV3 Small semantic segmentation on resource-constrained Arduino UNO Q hardware. The system uses timestamp-aligned evidence fusion, bounded tracking for object persistence, and an explicit corridor policy to generate inspectable navigation advisories that expose their contributing evidence and policy reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mobile river monitoring robots need to interpret obstacles and water boundaries that geographic waypoints cannot describe. Converting imperfect visual predictions into timely and inspectable guidance on resource-constrained platforms is challenging, as traditional object labels or steering commands don't explain which evidence supports decisions or when evidence is unreliable.

Method: The architecture combines a project-trained YOLO11n detector with a custom MobileNetV3 Small semantic segmenter, using timestamp-aligned evidence fusion on Arduino UNO Q. Bounded tracking provides object persistence, while an explicit corridor policy combines surface labels, accepted detections, urgency, and mask uncertainty. ROS 2 interfaces connect the AI pipeline to a Gazebo vessel simulation and control testbed. Training used 10,000 WaterScenes images for 4-class detection and 1,127 MaSTr1325 images for segmentation (including 198 validation images).

Result: Selected FP32 ONNX models occupy 14.817 MB. Detector checkpoint achieved mAP@0.5 = 0.7388, while the rectangular ONNX export achieved validation mAP@0.5 = 0.7367. Segmentation achieved validation mIoU = 0.9750. A five-minute recording showed median pipeline latency of 467.8 ms and 95th percentile latency of 580.3 ms at 0.5 Hz cadence. Issues identified include black input misclassification and sampling rate mismatch preventing the diagnostic motion estimator from collecting sufficient evidence.

Conclusion: The results support an inspectable and reusable edge robotics foundation while clearly distinguishing model accuracy and on-board execution from validated on-water collision avoidance. The system provides explicit evidence and policy reasoning for each navigation advisory, enabling transparency in decision-making.

Abstract: Mobile river monitoring robots must interpret obstacles and water boundaries that geographic waypoints alone cannot describe. On resource constrained platforms, converting imperfect visual predictions into timely and inspectable guidance is a distinct challenge. An object label or steering command does not explain which evidence supports a decision or when that evidence is unreliable. We present PAANI, an on-device perception to guidance architecture that combines a project trained YOLO11n detector and a custom MobileNetV3 Small semantic segmenter with timestamp aligned evidence fusion on Arduino UNO Q. Bounded tracking supplies object persistence, while an explicit corridor policy combines surface labels, accepted detections, urgency and mask uncertainty. Each final advisory exposes its contributing evidence and policy reasons. ROS 2 interfaces connect the local AI pipeline to a separate Gazebo vessel, localization and control testbed. Training uses 10,000 WaterScenes images for four-class detection and 1,127 MaSTr1325 images for segmentation, including 198 segmentation validation images. The selected FP32 ONNX models occupy 14.817 MB. Detector checkpoint test mAP at 0.5 IoU is 0.7388, while the separately evaluated rectangular ONNX export achieves validation mAP at 0.5 IoU of 0.7367. Segmentation ONNX validation mIoU is 0.9750. A five-minute UNO Q recording produced median and 95th percentile pipeline latencies of 467.8 ms and 580.3 ms at a configured 0.5 Hz cadence. The evaluation also identifies black input misclassification and a sampling rate mismatch that prevents the diagnostic apparent motion estimator from collecting sufficient evidence. These results support an inspectable and reusable edge robotics foundation while clearly distinguishing model accuracy and on-board execution from validated on-water collision avoidance.

</details>


### [124] [Social Influence and the Allocation of Scientific Attention in AI Populations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22408)
*Maxim Chupilkin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates how AI agents distribute attention across scientific papers when exposed to social information, using a "Music Lab" experimental design adapted for academic markets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI systems increasingly evaluate scientific research, but it's unclear how human-derived signals (citations, downloads, popularity) affect artificial readers. This study explores the collective consequences of social information on AI attention allocation.

Method: Two experiments: Experiment 1 involved 1,000 AI agents choosing from 114 AER 2025 articles across 5 independent-choice and 5 social-influence communities (100 sequential agents each). Experiment 2 used 200 agents in 20 social communities to test random initial selections' effects.

Result: Social-information communities selected 17.2% fewer papers per agent, concentrated choices more heavily, and covered 73 papers versus 90 in independent conditions. Randomly assigning 5 initial selections raised subsequent selection rates by 45.55 percentage points. Choices showed modest correspondence with external citations but little with download counts.

Conclusion: Simple information rules significantly shape the volume, breadth, and distribution of scientific attention in artificial populations, with social information increasing concentration and between-community variation.

Abstract: AI systems are becoming participants in the evaluation and use of scientific research. They encounter citation counts, download statistics and lists of popular articles developed around human readers, but the collective consequences of these signals for artificial readers remain uncertain. This paper adapts the Music Lab design to a market for academic attention. In the first experiment, 1,000 AI agents choose papers from the titles and abstracts of all 114 regular research articles published in the American Economic Review in 2025. The experiment has five independent-choice communities and five social-influence communities, each with 100 sequential agents. Only agents in the social-influence condition observe earlier selections within their community. Agents may select any number of papers. Social-information communities select 17.2 percent fewer papers per agent, concentrate their choices more heavily, and collectively cover 73 papers, compared with 90 independently. Between-community variation is greater under social information. In a second experiment with 200 agents across twenty social communities, randomly assigning papers five initial selections raises their subsequent selection rate by 45.55 percentage points (95% CI: 41.20 to 49.90). Choices have modest correspondence with external citations and little correspondence with download counts. The results show how a simple information rule shapes the volume, breadth and distribution of scientific attention in an artificial population.

</details>


### [125] [Learning 3D biophysical cell properties from 2D images and cell-population statistics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22410)
*Santiago Hernández-Orozco,Hector Zenil*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A population-supervised deep learning framework that maps single 2D red blood cell images to latent biophysical quantities (volume, haemoglobin) and aggregates them to match population statistics from reference instruments, without explicit 3D reconstruction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inferring 3D cellular properties from 2D microscopy is challenging when reference instruments only provide population statistics rather than single-cell labels. This limits the ability to obtain individual cell biophysical measurements.

Method: The framework combines: (1) shared local inference for single-cell feature extraction, (2) a biophysically structured decoder for volume and haemoglobin, (3) learned instance weighting, and (4) device-specific calibration. The model is supervised by aggregate population statistics rather than individual cell labels.

Result: Development dataset: 390 specimens, 1,105 acquisitions across 6 devices. Pearson correlations of 0.86-0.98 against a Sysmex analyser. The paper also formalizes conditions under which aggregate observations identify instance predictors and explains why population agreement alone cannot identify single-cell properties or 3D geometry.

Conclusion: The framework provides a testable pathway from 2D images with population supervision to 3D cellular biophysics without explicit 3D reconstruction. It addresses the identification problem in population-supervised learning and derives the dispersion penalty from subset mean matching.

Abstract: Inferring 3D cellular properties from 2D microscopy is difficult when a reference instrument reports only population statistics rather than labels for individual cells. Here we develop a population-supervised framework that maps single 2D red-cell images to latent biophysical quantities and aggregates them to mean corpuscular volume, red-cell distribution width and mean corpuscular haemoglobin. The model combines shared local inference, a biophysically structured decoder for volume and haemoglobin, learned instance weighting and device-specific calibration. We formalise conditions under which aggregate observations identify restricted instance predictors, show why population agreement does not by itself identify single-cell properties or 3D geometry, and derive the dispersion penalty induced by subset mean matching. The development dataset comprises 390 specimens and 1,105 acquisitions across six devices, with reported Pearson correlations of 0.86--0.98 against a Sysmex analyser. The framework provides a testable route from 2D images and population supervision to 3D cellular biophysics without claiming explicit 3D reconstruction.

</details>


### [126] [Goal-driven Variant Categorization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22475)
*Daniel Calegari,Daniel Amyot*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a goal-driven approach to process variant categorization that uses LLM-based semantic reasoning to assign variants to business-meaningful categories defined by a pre-authored goal model, rather than relying on post-hoc structural clustering and manual interpretation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process discovery typically yields multiple variants that must be clustered by structural similarity and then manually interpreted to assign business meaning. This manual interpretation becomes increasingly difficult and judgment-intensive as the number and complexity of variants grow, since the partitions are not derived from organizational goals.

Method: The approach involves: (1) authoring an organization's goal model that defines categorization axes, (2) transforming each process variant into a textual narrative describing its behavior, (3) using a Large Language Model (LLM) to interpret variants in the context of the goal model and assign them to appropriate categories. This enables LLM-based semantic reasoning to connect low-level process behavior with analyst-defined business goals.

Result: The evaluation on three public logs (differing in scale and behavioral diversity) shows that goal-model guidance yields partitions that differ from unguided induction and respond to controlled edits to declared alternatives. The main cost is the upfront effort required to author the goal model.

Conclusion: The goal-driven approach reverses the traditional variant categorization workflow by first defining business goals, then using LLM-based semantic reasoning to assign variants to categories. The method produces different partitions than unguided induction and is responsive to goal model edits, but requires upfront investment in authoring the goal model.

Abstract: Process discovery rarely yields a single coherent process structure. For analysis, a common step is to cluster process variants based on structural similarity and then assign business meaning to the resulting groups. Since these partitions are not derived from the organization's goals, analysts must manually interpret and consolidate variants into business-meaningful categories. This judgment-intensive step becomes increasingly difficult as the number and complexity of variants grow. In this paper, we propose a goal-driven approach to variant categorization that reverses this workflow. We first author an organization's goal model that predefines the categorization axis. Each variant is transformed into a textual narrative describing its behavior, and a Large Language Model (LLM) interprets it in the context of the goal model and assigns the variant to the most appropriate category. LLM-based semantic reasoning connects low-level process behavior with analyst-defined business goals. We instantiate this approach end-to-end and evaluate it on three public logs differing substantially in scale and behavioral diversity. Goal-model guidance yields partitions that differ from those produced by unguided induction and respond to controlled edits to the declared alternatives, at the cost of authoring a goal model.

</details>


### [127] [Replication Without Persistence in Hosted LLMs: Measurement Sensitivity in Action-Time Belief Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22478)
*Bhushan Kashinath Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates three validation questions for evaluating hosted language models: replication (whether findings recur on fresh data), measurement sensitivity (whether endpoints change when configurations are rebuilt), and persistence (whether findings persist across different model identifiers). Using Regent Chess as the test environment, the study shows that these three validation approaches can yield different conclusions within a single evaluation, highlighting the need to explicitly index behavioral claims by tested identifier, serving period, measurement instrument, and inference configuration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Behavioral evaluations of hosted language models can vary because the evaluated service, measurement instrument, or both differ across runs. The authors aim to separate and investigate three distinct validation questions to understand how these variations affect evaluation results.

Method: The study uses Regent Chess, a sequential environment with hidden, mutable state that allows stated beliefs to be scored against ground truth at action time. Positive endpoint values indicate worse performance than a matched-uniform comparator. The authors conduct three types of validation: (1) testing replication of prior findings on fresh data under historical configuration, (2) comparing measurements under rebuilt versus historical configurations, and (3) testing persistence across different model identifiers under the same instrument.

Result: The previously reported Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite deficit recurred on fresh games (+0.0530, 95% CI [+0.0329,+0.0714]). In a same-day H/R comparison, the model-minus-uniform endpoint was 0.0429 lower under the rebuilt configuration (95% CI [+0.0182,+0.0667]). Under rebuilt R, the 4K comparison reversed sign between Gemini 3.1 and Gemini 3.7, which differ in release and product tier. The serving-period contribution remained unresolved (-0.0166, [-0.0483,+0.0157]).

Conclusion: Replication, measurement sensitivity, and persistence can yield different conclusions within one evaluation. This finding motivates explicit indexing of hosted-model behavioral claims by tested identifier, serving period, measurement instrument, and inference configuration to ensure reproducibility and accurate comparison of language model performance.

Abstract: Behavioural evaluations of hosted language models can vary because the evaluated service, the measurement instrument, or both differ across runs. We separate three validation questions: whether a prior finding recurs on fresh data under its historical configuration (replication), whether the endpoint changes when the evaluation-and-inference configuration is rebuilt under the same identifier (measurement sensitivity), and whether the finding persists across subsequently tested identifiers under one common instrument (persistence). We study these questions in Regent Chess, a sequential environment in which a hidden, mutable state is recorded exactly, allowing stated beliefs to be scored against ground truth at action time; positive endpoint values mean worse performance than a matched-uniform comparator. The previously reported Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite deficit recurs on fresh games under its historical configuration (+0.0530, 95% CI [+0.0329,+0.0714]). In a back-to-back same-day H/R comparison under the same public identifier, the model-minus-uniform endpoint is 0.0429 lower under the rebuilt configuration (95% CI for the H-minus-R contrast [+0.0182,+0.0667]); all six configuration components vary jointly, so no component is isolated. Under rebuilt R, the prospectively frozen, interleaved same-window 4K comparison reverses sign between Gemini 3.1 and Gemini 3.7, identifiers that differ in release and product tier; additional descriptive and exploratory cells show the same directional pattern. Any additional serving-period contribution remains unresolved (-0.0166, [-0.0483,+0.0157]). Replication, measurement sensitivity, and persistence can therefore yield different conclusions within one evaluation, motivating explicit indexing of hosted-model behavioural claims by tested identifier, serving period, measurement instrument, and inference configuration.

</details>


### [128] [The Wisdom of Artificial Deliberative Crowds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22497)
*Federico Barrera-Lemarchand,Mariano Sigman,Joaquin Navajas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether deliberation among large language models improves collective accuracy compared to passive aggregation. Using a three-stage deliberation paradigm across three model families and four domains (visual estimation, peer review, malicious behavior detection, sports forecasting), the study finds that deliberation reduces collective error beyond passive aggregation, with post-deliberation individual judgments retaining gains. However, model diversity is essential—groups of identical model clones showed no benefit from deliberation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While the wisdom of crowds typically relies on independent estimates, human studies show that deliberation (averaging consensus of small groups) produces even stronger effects. This study explores whether this deliberation advantage transfers to LLMs, addressing a gap in understanding how machine collectives can improve judgment.

Method: A three-stage deliberation paradigm (previously used with humans) was adapted for LLMs from three different model families. Tested across four domains of increasing real-world stakes: visual numerical estimation (Study 1), peer review of ML papers (Study 2), detection of hidden malicious AI behavior (Study 3), and sports forecasting against real prediction markets (Study 4).

Result: Deliberation reduced collective error beyond passive aggregation of independent responses. Post-deliberation individual judgments retained the collective gain. Critically, model diversity was required for the advantage—groups composed of clones of a single model did not benefit from deliberating.

Conclusion: Machine deliberation serves as a general-purpose aggregation mechanism for improving LLM collective intelligence. Diversity emerges as the active ingredient: without model diversity, deliberation provides no advantage. This establishes deliberation as a scalable method for enhancing AI judgment across diverse applications.

Abstract: The aggregation of many lay estimates often outperforms individual expert judgment, a phenomenon known as the wisdom of crowds. While this is usually attributed to the independence of estimates, an even stronger effect arises through deliberation: averaging the consensus estimates of small deliberating groups outperforms the classical wisdom of crowds, with individual judgments themselves also becoming more accurate after deliberation. Whether these improvements transfer to large language models deliberating amongst themselves is unknown. Here we adapt a three-stage deliberation paradigm previously used with human participants for use with large language models from three different families, and test it across four domains of increasing real-world stakes: visual numerical estimation (Study 1), peer review of machine-learning papers (Study 2), detection of hidden malicious behavior by an artificial intelligence agent (Study 3), and sports forecasting against a real prediction market (Study 4). Across domains, deliberation reduced collective error beyond passive aggregation of independent responses, and post-deliberation individual judgments retained this collective gain. Notably, the advantage required model diversity: groups composed of clones of a single model did not benefit from deliberating. These results establish machine deliberation as a general-purpose aggregation mechanism, and point to diversity as an active ingredient.

</details>


### [129] [Agreement Overstates Evidence: Error Dependence in LLM Judge Consensus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22512)
*Elias Hossain,Niloofar Yousefi,Ser-Nam Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM judges exhibit substantial error correlation (average pairwise correlation 0.21), meaning ten judges provide only ~3.5 independent judgments. In up to 28% of comparisons, ignoring shared errors leads to incorrect conclusions about which system is better. Error patterns matter—shared errors among most judges vs. concentrated errors affect consensus differently and favor different voting methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The assumption that LLM judges make errors independently is implicit in treating consensus as strong evidence for correctness. However, LLM judges are often trained and evaluated similarly, so they may make the same mistakes. This study investigates how this dependency affects the reliability of consensus.

Method: Evaluated error correlations across ten LLM judges (both open-weight and frontier models) using a bank of comparisons. Measured pairwise correlation between judge errors, calculated effective number of independent judges, and tested how conclusions change when accounting for shared errors vs. ignoring them.

Result: Average pairwise correlation between judge errors is 0.21. Ten judges provide only ~3.5 independent judgments. Dependency is stronger among high-accuracy frontier judges, including judges from different providers. In up to 28% of comparisons, ignoring shared errors leads to concluding one system is significantly better when accounting for them does not. Error patterns matter: errors shared by most judges vs. errors concentrated in a smaller group affect consensus differently and favor different voting methods.

Conclusion: Consensus among LLM judges is less reliable than commonly assumed. Measuring overall correlation alone is insufficient; the pattern of errors matters. Recommended approach: use a small set of trusted examples to estimate judge accuracy and identify shared mistakes, then consider these shared errors when analyzing results and choose voting methods using trusted examples before applying to new data.

Abstract: Consensus among LLM judges is often taken as strong evidence that a decision is correct. This assumes that judges make their errors independently. In practice, LLM judges are often trained and evaluated in similar ways, so they can make the same mistakes. We study how this dependency affects the reliability of consensus. We find substantial error correlation across both open-weight and frontier LLM judges. In our main bank of ten judges, the average pairwise correlation between judge errors is 0.21. As a result, the ten judges only provide roughly as much statistical information as 3.5 independent judges. The dependency is even stronger among the high-accuracy frontier judges we evaluate, including judges from different providers. In up to 28% of our comparisons, ignoring shared errors leads to the conclusion that one system is significantly better, while accounting for them does not. We also find that the pattern of errors matters. Errors shared by most judges and errors concentrated among a smaller group affect consensus differently and favor different voting methods. Measuring the overall amount of correlation alone is therefore insufficient. Our results suggest a simple approach: use a small set of trusted examples to estimate judge accuracy and identify shared mistakes. These shared errors should then be considered when analyzing the results, and the voting method should be chosen using trusted examples before it is applied to new data.

</details>


### [130] [IntLawNER: A Named Entity Recognition Dataset and Benchmark in International Law](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22529)
*Genis Skura,Roland Bouffanais,Didier Wernli*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IntLawNER is a novel NER benchmark dataset for international law texts, containing 2,987 gold-annotated sentences and 8,094 entity spans from ICJ decisions, UN Security Council resolutions, and ECtHR judgments, annotated with seven institution-specific entity types. The study reveals significant challenges in domain-specific NER, including discrepancies between inter-annotator agreement and actual performance, and demonstrates that few-shot prompting with label contrasts can substantially improve LLM performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: International law provides the normative framework for state coordination, armed conflict regulation, and human rights protection, yet its texts lack token-level NER resources. This gap hinders automated extraction of legal references and institutional information from international legal documents.

Method: The authors constructed IntLawNER using a cost-effective hybrid algorithmic-agentic pipeline: (1) Candidate retrieval reduced 468k source sentences to a compact annotation set; (2) LLM-based vetting identified potential entities; (3) Human review validated the final annotations. The pipeline achieved 89.6% silver-to-gold acceptance rate. The benchmark evaluated zero-shot span-based GLiNER, fine-tuned transformers, and various LLMs with zero-shot and few-shot prompting approaches.

Result: Key findings include: (1) Human-machine aggregate agreement metrics can be misleading—Cohen's kappa of 0.964 on boundary-matched spans masks a macro-F1 of 0.753 when missing entities, boundary errors, and label corrections are considered; (2) Zero-shot GLiNER collapses on entity types dependent on institutional function rather than surface form (0.243 micro-F1); (3) Fine-tuned transformers struggle on rare labels; (4) Carefully selected few-shot examples demonstrating label contrasts improve every LLM, with Claude Opus 4.6 achieving the best score of 0.873 micro-F1.

Conclusion: IntLawNER serves as a valuable benchmark and reusable resource for extracting references in international legal texts. The study highlights the importance of careful metric selection in domain-specific NER tasks and demonstrates that few-shot prompting with contrastive examples can significantly enhance LLM performance for legal NER tasks. The dataset addresses a significant gap in NLP resources for international law.

Abstract: International law provides the normative framework through which states coordinate action, regulate armed conflict, and protect human rights, yet its texts remain without token-level named entity recognition (NER) resources. We introduce IntLawNER, a NER dataset and benchmark for codified sources of international law, covering 2,987 gold-annotated sentences and 8,094 entity spans from International Court of Justice (ICJ) decisions, UN Security Council resolutions, and European Court of Human Rights (ECtHR) judgments, annotated with seven institution-specific entity types. We construct IntLawNER with a cost-effective hybrid algorithmic-agentic pipeline that reduces 468k source sentences to a compact annotation set through candidate retrieval, LLM-based vetting, and human review, with 89.6% of gold spans accepted unchanged from the silver layer. However, the silver-to-gold analysis reveals that human-machine aggregate agreement metrics can be misleading in domain-specific NER: Cohen's kappa=0.964 on boundary-matched spans masks a macro-F1 of 0.753 when missing entities, boundary errors, and label corrections are included. The benchmark shows that zero-shot span-based GLiNER collapses on entity types dependent on institutional function rather than surface form (0.243 micro-F1), while fine-tuned transformers struggle on rare labels. Carefully selected few-shot examples that demonstrate label contrasts improve every LLM over zero-shot prompting, with Claude Opus 4.6 reaching the best score of 0.873 micro-F1. We release IntLawNER as a benchmark and reusable resource for extracting references in international legal texts.

</details>


### [131] [EvidenT: Building Trustworthy Enterprise Assistants through Evidence Groundedness and Traceability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22537)
*Anubha Kabra,Katie Jooyoung Kim,Colin Zhiwei Kou,Helene Sajer,Yimei Fan,Radomir Cisar,Heather Greenhalgh,Gabriel Martinez Vidiri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvidenT is a lightweight pipeline that verifies extracted evidence against retrieved documents before answer generation in enterprise AI assistants, addressing citation drift, unsupported content, and weak source traceability issues in RAG systems without requiring model retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise AI assistants need to produce verifiable and traceable responses, but RAG over heterogeneous enterprise data suffers from citation drift (incorrect source attribution), unsupported content (hallucinations), and weak source traceability. Existing prompting baselines lack sufficient evidence verification mechanisms.

Method: EvidenT combines structured passage extraction with deterministic lexical alignment to: (1) filter unsupported content before answer generation, (2) correct citation drift by verifying evidence against retrieved documents, and (3) preserve source-span traceability through lexical matching between answers and source documents.

Result: On ~500 real enterprise queries: (1) 29% average improvement in gold-source hit rate over prompting baselines, (2) produces zero citations to non-retrieved URLs, (3) achieves near-saturated answer-to-source lexical coverage.

Conclusion: EvidenT demonstrates that a lightweight, retraining-free pipeline can significantly improve RAG reliability in enterprise settings by implementing deterministic evidence verification, effectively eliminating citation hallucinations and ensuring strong traceability between generated answers and source documents.

Abstract: Enterprise AI assistants must produce responses that are verifiable and traceable to source evidence. However, retrieval augmented generation (RAG) over heterogeneous enterprise data can suffer from citation drift, unsupported content, and weak source traceability. We present EvidenT (T = Trust + Transparency + Traceability), a lightweight pipeline that verifies extracted evidence against retrieved documents before answer generation, without model retraining. EvidenT combines structured passage extraction with deterministic lexical alignment to filter unsupported content, correct citation drift, and preserve source-span traceability. On approximately 500 real enterprise queries, EvidenT improves gold-source hit rate by an average of 29% over prompting baselines, produces no citations to nonretrieved urls, and achieves near-saturated answer-to-source lexical coverage.

</details>


### [132] [AutoGym: Blueprint-First Generation of Verifiable Agent Gyms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22592)
*Aarati Andrea Noronha,Kavya Ravikumar,Carly Xiaoyu Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AutoGym is a framework for automatically generating complete reinforcement learning gyms (tasks, executable environments, and verifiers) from minimal domain seeds or prior model trajectories. It addresses limitations of manual gym construction including expense, static nature, task set saturation, contamination, and unreliable LLM-based verification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training agents with reinforcement learning requires gyms comprising tasks, executable environments, and verifiers. Current gym construction is manual, expensive, static, and prone to contamination as models improve. Synthetic generation produces tasks with cosmetic difficulty that frontier models can solve despite convoluted phrasing, and correctness verification relies on unreliable post-hoc LLM judges.

Method: AutoGym introduces three mechanisms: (1) Blueprint-first generation specifies valid solution space, environment requirements, and verification criteria before environment materialization, making solvability a construction prerequisite; (2) Explicit generation parameters control task topology, interaction depth, capability axes, question obfuscation, and distractor composition for fine-grained difficulty steering; (3) Active curriculum synthesis uses performance-informed calibration to adjust parameter distributions as model capabilities evolve.

Result: AutoGym generates gyms spanning the capability spectrum across productivity and temporal-reasoning settings, including instances that challenge frontier models.

Conclusion: AutoGym provides a scalable, controllable solution for automated gym generation that can adapt to evolving model capabilities through active curriculum synthesis, producing diverse tasks with guaranteed solvability and reliable verification.

Abstract: Training agents with reinforcement learning requires a gym, comprising a task, an executable environment in which the task can be attempted, and a verifier that reliably distinguishes success from failure. Constructing such gyms remains manual, expensive, and static. Task sets saturate as models improve and are increasingly exposed to contamination. Synthetic generation offers scale, but single-pass synthesis produces tasks whose difficulty is largely cosmetic. Models comparable in capability solve them despite convoluted phrasing, and correctness must be adjudicated post-hoc by unreliable LLM judges. We present AutoGym, a framework that generates complete gyms (tasks, executable environments, and verifiers) from a minimal domain seed or prior model trajectories. AutoGym introduces three mechanisms. (1) Blueprint-first generation specifies the valid solution space, environment requirements, and verification criteria before the environment is materialized, making solvability a construction prerequisite rather than a property verified after the fact. (2) Explicit generation parameters control task topology, interaction depth, capability axes, question obfuscation, and distractor composition, enabling fine-grained difficulty steering. (3) Active curriculum synthesis uses performance-informed calibration to adjust the distribution over these parameters as model capabilities evolve. Across productivity and temporal-reasoning settings, AutoGym generates gyms spanning the capability spectrum, including instances that challenge frontier models.

</details>


### [133] [MAWILE: Multi-Axis Workbench for Inspecting LLM Evaluators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22599)
*Jackson Hassell,Farima Fatahi Bayat,Pouya Pezeshkpour,Estevam Hruschka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MAWILE is a developer workbench for auditing LLM judge sensitivity across four surfaces (judge prompt, rubric, target input/output). It constructs controlled perturbations to measure robustness to irrelevant variations and sensitivity to meaningful changes, without requiring gold labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judges provide flexible evaluation but their verdicts can be sensitive to incidental changes in responses, instructions, and rubrics. Existing systems only examine subsets of these failure modes, requiring comprehensive testing of both the judge instrument and evaluation items.

Method: MAWILE takes a user-supplied judge and representative evaluation items, then constructs and validates controlled perturbations across four surfaces: judge prompt, judge rubric, target-system input, and target-system output. It re-executes the judge and localizes sensitivity. Each perturbation declares expected invariance or directional change.

Result: MAWILE can audit binary, ordinal, and pairwise judges without requiring gold labels. It measures both robustness to irrelevant variations and sensitivity to meaningful changes through a unified framework.

Conclusion: MAWILE provides a systematic approach to audit LLM judge sensitivity, enabling developers to identify and diagnose reliability issues in judge configurations before deployment. The tool is open-source and available at github.com/megagonlabs/mawile-judge.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) judges provide a flexible and scalable method for evaluating model and agent outputs, but their verdicts can be sensitive to incidental changes in the evaluated response, judge instructions, and scoring rubric. Existing systems examine important subsets of these failure modes, but auditing a configured judge requires testing both the judge instrument and the items it evaluates. We introduce MAWILE, a developer-facing workbench for auditing judge sensitivity across four surfaces: the judge prompt, judge rubric, target-system input, and target-system output. Given a user-supplied judge and representative evaluation items, MAWILE constructs and validates controlled perturbations, re-executes the judge, and localizes the resulting sensitivity. Each perturbation declares whether the verdict should remain invariant or change in a specified direction, allowing the same system to measure both robustness to irrelevant variations and sensitivity to meaningful changes. MAWILE audits binary, ordinal, and pairwise judges without requiring gold labels. The code for this tool is available at: github.com/megagonlabs/mawile-judge.

</details>


### [134] [GaitVista: Reliability-Aware AI Measurement toward Accessible Longitudinal Gait Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22619)
*Nethmi Jayasinghe,Mihir Parashar,Amit Ranjan Trivedi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GaitVista is a reliability-aware measurement layer for tracking walking function recovery across rehabilitation sessions. It addresses the problem that objective 3D gait measurement is confined to specialized labs, while more accessible small camera sets and inertial sensors have variable reliability that can be mistaken for patient change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable accessible gait assessment outside specialized motion-capture laboratories by improving the reliability of sensor fusion from small camera sets and body-worn inertial sensors, while distinguishing actual patient change from sensing failures.

Method: GaitVista uses a lightweight gate that assigns joint- and frame-specific visual contributions based on: camera coverage, local visual quality, cross-modal disagreement, and root-motion continuity. It exposes reliability metrics for inspection. Validated on TotalCapture (7 conditions) and MoVi (with image-derived keypoints) datasets.

Result: - Reduces average full-body and lower-body error by 27.7% and 27.8% on TotalCapture
- Achieves lowest worst-condition error among fusion methods
- Reduces gap to joint-frame oracle from 2.76-5.33cm to 1.11cm
- On MoVi: only deployable fusion method to improve over both unimodal streams, reducing marker-supported error by 6.4% vs strongest learned fusion baseline
- Improves bilateral knee-flexion waveform accuracy by 18.9%
- Raw IMU measurements reveal location- and time-varying magnetic disturbance

Conclusion: GaitVista demonstrates significant improvements in reliability-aware sensor fusion for gait measurement, though it represents progress toward accessible gait assessment rather than validated clinical deployment (benchmarks contain neurologically healthy participants in controlled settings).

Abstract: Tracking recovery of walking function requires detecting meaningful gait change across rehabilitation sessions, yet objective 3D measurement remains confined to specialized motion-capture laboratories. Small camera sets and body-worn inertial sensors broaden access, but reliability varies across joints and time, allowing sensing failures to masquerade as patient change. We present \textsc{GaitVista}, a reliability-aware measurement layer whose lightweight gate assigns joint- and frame-specific visual contributions using camera coverage, local visual quality, cross-modal disagreement, and root-motion continuity, and exposes them for inspection. Across seven clean and degraded sensing conditions on TotalCapture, \textsc{GaitVista} reduces average full-body and lower-body error by \textbf{27.7\%} and \textbf{27.8\%}, attains the lowest worst-condition error among fusion methods, and reduces the gap to a joint-frame oracle from $2.76$--$5.33$~cm for condition-blind baselines to $1.11$~cm. On MoVi with image-derived keypoints, it is the only deployable fusion method to improve over both unimodal streams, reducing marker-supported error by \textbf{6.4\%} relative to the strongest learned fusion baseline. On TotalCapture, it improves bilateral knee-flexion waveform accuracy by \textbf{18.9\%}. Raw inertial measurements from five TotalCapture participants show location- and time-varying magnetic disturbance, supporting the design's reliability premise. Both benchmarks contain neurologically healthy participants in controlled settings and retain participant-specific IMU calibration; we therefore report progress toward accessible gait assessment, not validated clinical deployment.

</details>


### [135] [Splitting Documents at Lower Cost: Multi-Split Boundary Decisions for LLM-Based Page Stream Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22620)
*Nikhil Reddy Pottanigari,Sepideh Kharaghani,Saverio Vadacchino,Alejandro Posada,Ying Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Multi-Split Boundary Decision (MSBD), a method that predicts multiple document boundaries within a page window in a single inference call, improving efficiency over standard single-boundary approaches for zero-shot page stream segmentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot LLMs can detect document boundaries without task-specific training, but existing Page Classification (PC) and Boundary Decision (BD) methods only resolve one boundary per model call, requiring multiple inference requests for document splitting.

Method: MSBD is proposed to predict multiple boundaries within a page window in a single call. The method was evaluated across multiple language models, document collections, input modalities (scanned mail, PDFs, consolidated attachments), and various window sizes to assess accuracy and efficiency trade-offs.

Result: MSBD preserves strong segmentation accuracy within a model- and corpus-dependent operating range while substantially improving inference efficiency, but shows a sharp decline at larger windows. Large windows expose distinct over- and under-segmentation behaviors across models. MSBD provided the strongest overall accuracy-efficiency trade-off.

Conclusion: Multi-boundary prediction can make zero-shot page stream segmentation more efficient when the window size is appropriately selected for the target corpus.

Abstract: Scanned mail, uploaded PDFs, and consolidated attachments often arrive as page streams that must be split into individual documents before downstream classification, extraction, or routing. Zero-shot large language models can detect document boundaries without task-specific training, but standard Page Classification (PC) and Boundary Decision (BD) formulations resolve only one boundary per model call. We introduce Multi-Split Boundary Decision (MSBD), which predicts multiple boundaries within a page window in a single call, reducing the number of inference requests. We evaluate MSBD across multiple language models, document collections, input modalities, and window sizes. The results reveal a model- and corpus-dependent operating range in which MSBD preserves strong segmentation accuracy while substantially improving inference efficiency, followed by a sharp decline at larger windows. MSBD provided the strongest overall accuracy--efficiency trade-off, while large windows expose distinct over- and under-segmentation behavior across models. These findings show that multi-boundary prediction can make zero-shot page stream segmentation more efficient when the window size is selected for the target corpus.

</details>


### [136] [Text, Pixels, or Both? Evaluating Input Representations for Multimodal Document QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22628)
*Nikhil Reddy Pottanigari,Sepideh Kharaghani,Saverio Vadacchino,Alejandro Posada,Ying Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates the choice between page images, extracted text, or both for document QA systems, testing across multiple models and corpora. Images outperform text on accuracy but have higher latency; a lightweight TF-IDF router can exploit complementary strengths.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The choice between page images, extracted text, or both is rarely studied on its own in document QA systems. This paper isolates this choice to understand the trade-offs.

Method: The authors held the prompt, judge, and scoring pipeline fixed across four commercial model endpoints, two corpora, and two context regimes (gold evidence pages and full document). They compared accuracy, latency, and cost between text and image inputs.

Result: Page images lead on accuracy at every document length on both corpora, but carry a growing latency and cost premium. Text and images fail on different questions, with exactly one representation correct on 19-25% of items. A TF-IDF router gained 2.6 points over always-text while cutting median latency 30% relative to always-vision.

Conclusion: Text and images are complementary representations for document QA. A lightweight routing mechanism can exploit this complementarity to achieve better accuracy than text-only with significantly reduced latency compared to vision-only.

Abstract: Every document QA system begins with a choice that is rarely studied on its own: whether to feed the model page images, extracted text, or both. We isolate this choice, holding the prompt, judge, and scoring pipeline fixed, across four commercial model endpoints, two corpora, and two context regimes (gold evidence pages and the full document). On documents that fit the image budget, page images lead on accuracy at every document length on both corpora, but this advantage carries a growing latency and cost premium: text latency stays roughly flat as documents lengthen while image latency rises steadily. Text and images also fail on different questions, with exactly one representation correct on 19--25% of items across the reported cells, so neither subsumes the other. Exploiting this complementarity, a lightweight TF-IDF router that reads only the question text gains 2.6 points over always-text while cutting median latency 30% relative to always-vision, on a document-disjoint held-out split.

</details>


### [137] [Self-Organizing Agent Teams Learn to Reason Together](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22682)
*Aneesh Pappu,Mirac Suzgun,Yongchan Kwon,Federico Bianchi,Batu El,Mykel J. Kochenderfer,Hancheng Cao,James Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Self-Organizing Agent Teams (SAT) learn reusable collaboration strategies from prior experience, enabling AI agents to dynamically organize roles, phases, and information flow. These teams achieve 66.7% accuracy on benchmarks vs 48.8% for the strongest member, demonstrating that organizational capabilities can be learned and transferred to new problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI agent teams rely on fixed protocols or explicit task decomposition, unlike human teams that adapt their organization from experience. The authors seek to enable "collaborative computation" where agents exchange, challenge, repair, and synthesize partial reasoning into solutions no member could produce independently.

Method: Teams of AI agents learn reusable strategies from prior collaborations, enabling them to organize roles, conversational phases, participation, and information flow. Strategies are learned on 15 mathematics and 25 graduate-level knowledge problems, then transferred unchanged to unseen benchmarks.

Result: Self-organizing teams achieve 66.7% accuracy across five mathematics and physics benchmarks, compared to 48.8% (strongest member), 58.7% (compute-matched inference), and 59.0% (perfect router). On AIME 2026, they exceed the router by 13.4 points. Demonstrability (whether a team can distinguish correct from incorrect reasoning) strongly correlates with improvement over the strongest member (Spearman ρ=0.90, p=0.005).

Conclusion: Organization itself can become an agent capability: agent teams can learn how to reason together and produce solutions their members could not reach independently. The key factor is "demonstrability" - teams benefit most when correct reasoning can be recognized once it appears.

Abstract: Collective intelligence depends not only on what team members know, but also on how they organize their work. When the structure of a solution is unknown, useful roles and divisions of labor cannot be specified in advance; teams must learn from experience how to organize reasoning as it unfolds. Human teams routinely adapt this way, while existing AI agent teams rely on fixed protocols, explicit task decomposition, or routing. We introduce Self-Organizing Agent Teams (SAT), fixed teams of AI agents that learn reusable strategies from prior collaborations to organize roles, conversational phases, participation, and information flow. These strategies enable what we call collaborative computation: agents exchange, challenge, repair, and synthesize partial reasoning into solutions no member produced independently. In two independent settings, we learn teamwork strategies that transfer unchanged to unseen benchmarks, using only 15 mathematics and 25 graduate-level knowledge problems. Across five mathematics and physics benchmarks, self-organizing teams average 66.7% accuracy, versus 48.8% for their strongest member, 58.7% for compute-matched inference by that agent, and 59.0% for a perfect router over members' independent answers; on AIME 2026, they exceed this router by 13.4 points. Because gains vary across benchmarks, we ask when self-organizing collaboration helps. Across eight benchmarks, demonstrability (the organizational-psychology construct of whether a team can distinguish correct from incorrect reasoning) strongly tracks improvement over the strongest member (Spearman $ρ=0.90$, $p=0.005$): teams benefit most when correct reasoning can be recognized once it appears. More broadly, these results suggest that organization itself can become an agent capability: agent teams can learn how to reason together and produce solutions their members could not reach independently.

</details>


### [138] [Generative Embodied Multiple Behavior Control Systems for Human-like Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22691)
*Chongyu Bao,Haokai Yang,Yuhan Wang,Zhaochong An,Kunpeng Liu,Xiaolan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a gap in human-like agent frameworks by incorporating habitual behavior control alongside goal-directed behavior. The proposed framework consists of three components: a Habitual Controller (retrieving cue-triggered behaviors from memory), a Goal-directed Controller (using a context-aware world model), and an Arbiter (dynamically balancing both systems). A keyframe-guided 3D motion generation module is also developed for reconstructing human-level behaviors in 3D environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing human-like agent frameworks primarily focus on goal-directed behavior while largely overlooking habitual behavior, which plays a crucial role in human daily life. This gap limits the believability and human-likeness of embodied agents.

Method: The framework integrates three components: (1) Habitual Controller that retrieves cue-triggered behaviors from personalized habit memory, (2) Goal-directed Controller employing a context-aware world model to predict action consequences and estimate values, and (3) Arbiter that dynamically balances the influence of both systems based on individual differences and momentary internal states. A keyframe-guided 3D motion generation module is developed for reconstructing diverse human-level behavior instructions in 3D environments.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate significant improvement in human-likeness performance through extensive evaluation methods, human studies, and ablation studies.

Conclusion: The efficacy of the approach indicates the benefits of leveraging habitual behavior and multiple behavior control system coordination for creating believable embodied human-like agents.

Abstract: An enduring and richly elaborated dichotomy in cognitive neuroscience is that of human behavior control mechanisms, divided into habitual versus goal-directed. While existing human-like agent frameworks primarily focus on modeling goal- directed behavior, habitual behavior has been largely overlooked, though it plays a crucial role in human daily life. In this paper, we address this gap by studying multiple behavior control systems that jointly model goal-directed and habitual behaviors. We propose a human behavior control mechanism-inspired framework which the Habitual Controller retrieves cue-triggered behaviors from personal- ized habit memory, while the Goal-directed Controller employs a context-aware world model to predict action consequences and estimate their values. The Arbiter dynamically balances the influence of both systems according to individual differ- ences and momentary internal states. To reconstruct diverse human-level behavior instructions in 3D environments, we further develop a keyframe-guided 3D mo- tion generation module. Through extensive evaluation methods, human studies, and ablations studies, experimental results demonstrate that human-likeness per- formance is significantly improved by our approach. The efficacy of our approach indicates the benefits of leveraging habitual behavior and multiple behavior con- trol system coordination for believable embodied human-like agents.

</details>


### [139] [Toward Auditable and Calibrated AI for Dementia-Related Crash Severity Prediction: A Selective Deferral Framework to Support Human Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22694)
*Gaurab Chhetri,Anika Baitullah,Subasish Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study reframes dementia-related crash severity prediction as a decision-aware triage problem rather than a standard classification task. Using 4,781 Texas crash records, the authors evaluate multiple model architectures (structured, narrative, fusion, BERT-family, LLMs) under strict leakage control. The best model (Gemma) achieves macro-F1 of 0.545, while selective deferral at 70% coverage improves macro-F1 to 0.573. The paper emphasizes auditability, uncertainty calibration, and human-in-the-loop review rather than accuracy alone.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional crash severity models are evaluated as ordinary classifiers, making them difficult to translate into public-sector decision workflows. The study addresses this by reframing the problem as a decision-aware triage system that controls outcome leakage, reports severe under-triage, calibrates confidence, and preserves predictions for audit—making it more practical for real-world deployment.

Method: The study uses 4,781 Texas crash records with structured fields and police narratives. Models evaluated include structured models, narrative models, fusion models, calibrated fusion, BERT-family models, and local large-language-models. Evaluation uses a stratified 70/15/15 split with leakage control. Key techniques include selective deferral and confidence calibration.

Result: Leakage-controlled Gemma achieves the highest macro-F1 of 0.545 (95% CI: 0.507-0.583). The best calibrated fusion model achieves macro-F1 of 0.522 with expected calibration error of 0.033. At 70% coverage through selective deferral, macro-F1 improves to 0.573 and severity cost reduces to 0.577. Deferred cases are flagged for human review.

Conclusion: The study provides a reproducible, leakage-controlled, and uncertainty-aware evaluation framework for crash AI systems. It emphasizes auditability and selective deferral over accuracy alone, proposing a human-review process for deferred cases. This approach makes crash severity prediction more suitable for public-sector deployment by incorporating decision-awareness and uncertainty quantification.
