Abstract: Public crash databases increasingly support automated safety analysis, but crash severity prediction remains difficult to translate into public-sector decision workflows when models are evaluated primarily as ordinary classifiers. This study reframes dementia-related crash severity modeling as a decision-aware triage problem in which a system must classify crashes into no-injury/property-damage-only (O), minor or moderate injury (BC), and fatal or severe injury (KA), while also controlling outcome leakage, reporting severe under-triage, calibrating confidence, and preserving every raw prediction for audit. Using 4,781 Texas crash records with structured fields and police narratives, we evaluate structured, narrative, fusion, calibrated fusion, BERT-family, and local large-language-model baselines under a stratified 70/15/15 split. In the reported split, leakage-controlled Gemma obtains the highest observed macro-F1 (0.545; 95% bootstrap CI [0.507, 0.583]). The best calibrated fusion model obtains macro-F1 of 0.522 and expected calibration error of 0.033. Selective deferral improves performance among cases retained for automatic classification. At 70% coverage, macro-F1 rises to 0.573 and severity cost falls to 0.577, while deferred cases are treated as candidates for a proposed human-review process and are not further evaluated in the present experiment. The study contributes a reproducible, leakage-controlled, and uncertainty-aware evaluation framework for crash AI systems, emphasizing auditability and selective deferral rather than accuracy alone.

</details>


### [140] [A Survey on the Linear Representation Hypothesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22695)
*Sewoong Lee,Marc E. Canby,Ikhyun Cho,Julia Hockenmaier*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper critically examines the "linear representation hypothesis" (LRH) across AI, neuroscience, and cognitive science, arguing that prior works have inconsistently treated it as a falsifiable scientific hypothesis. The authors propose a more rigorous formalization that makes explicit dependencies on model architecture, representation location, feature definition, and evaluation dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The LRH has been used across diverse fields but lacks consistent treatment as a falsifiable scientific hypothesis. This inconsistency undermines the interpretability and reliability of prior theoretical and methodological results, necessitating a more rigorous formalization.

Method: The authors conduct a conceptual analysis of prior works on LRH, examining inconsistencies in how the hypothesis has been formulated and evaluated. They identify four critical dependencies that must be made explicit: model, representation location, feature definition, and evaluation dataset.

Result: The analysis reveals that claims about linear representations are ill-defined without careful specification of the model, where representations are extracted, how features are defined, and what evaluation datasets are used. The paper proposes a formalization framework that makes these dependencies explicit.

Conclusion: The authors conclude that the LRH becomes a meaningful falsifiable scientific claim only when these four dependencies are carefully specified. Several non-trivial open problems are identified for future research, including developing appropriate evaluation metrics and benchmark datasets.

Abstract: The term "linear representation hypothesis" (LRH) has appeared across diverse subfields of artificial intelligence, neuroscience, and cognitive science. But previous works have not consistently treated the LRH as a falsifiable scientific hypothesis; we analyze these inconsistencies and examine their implications for how prior theoretical and methodological results should be interpreted. Based on this analysis, we argue that claims regarding linear representations become well-defined only through careful examination of the model, representation location, feature definition, and evaluation dataset. We therefore propose a more rigorous formalization of the LRH that makes these dependencies explicit and allows the hypothesis to be evaluated as a falsifiable scientific claim. Finally, we identify some non-trivial open problems that warrant further attention from the research community.

</details>


### [141] [Building Trustworthy Mental Health Benchmarks on Bluesky: A Validation-Aware Weak-Supervision Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22696)
*Gaurab Chhetri,Anandi Dutta,Subasish Das*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents a validation-aware weak-supervision system for building suicidal ideation (SI) and mental health (MH) disclosure benchmarks on Bluesky, a decentralized social media platform. Using public firehose collection, lexicon filtering, Llama-3-8B-assisted annotation, and human validation, they constructed corpora of 8,346 SI-labeled and 9,988 MH-labeled posts. Evaluation shows model performance varies by task and validation protocol, with different failure modes across tasks (SI: false negatives; MH: false positives).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized social media platforms create new opportunities and challenges for computational mental health research due to distributed responsibilities across technical and governance layers. There is a need to validate weak-supervision approaches for constructing mental health benchmarks on such platforms.

Method: The system integrates: (1) public firehose collection, (2) task-specific lexicon filtering, (3) Llama-3-8B-assisted binary annotation, (4) human-adjudicated validation subsets, and (5) transformer-based model benchmarking. They evaluated multiple models (BERT+LSTM, RoBERTa, DistilRoBERTa) using stratified cross-validation and holdout testing.

Result: BERT+LSTM achieved highest SI stratified cross-validation F1-score; RoBERTa achieved strongest SI holdout F1-score; DistilRoBERTa achieved best MH cross-validation F1-score. Human validation revealed different weak-label failure modes: SI labels dominated by false negatives, MH labels dominated by false positives.

Conclusion: Decentralized social media can support reproducible mental health benchmarking, but only when system design, label provenance, validation strategy, and deployment constraints are evaluated together. The findings emphasize the importance of task-specific validation protocols in weak-supervision systems.

Abstract: Decentralized social media platforms create new opportunities and challenges for computational mental health research because data access, moderation, labeling, and deployment responsibilities are distributed across multiple technical and governance layers. This paper presents a validation-aware weak-supervision system for constructing and evaluating suicidal ideation (SI) and broader mental health (MH) disclosure benchmarks on Bluesky, a decentralized social media platform built on the AT Protocol. The system integrates public firehose collection, task-specific lexicon filtering, Llama-3-8B-assisted binary annotation, human-adjudicated validation subsets, and transformer-based model benchmarking. Using this pipeline, we construct two task-specific corpora containing 8,346 SI-labeled posts and 9,988 MH-labeled posts. The evaluation shows that model performance depends strongly on both task definition and validation protocol. BERT+LSTM achieves the highest SI stratified cross-validation F1-score, RoBERTa achieves the strongest SI holdout F1-score, and DistilRoBERTa achieves the best MH cross-validation F1-score. Human validation reveals different weak-label failure modes across tasks, with SI labels dominated by false negatives and MH labels dominated by false positives. These findings show that decentralized social media can support reproducible mental health benchmarking, but only when system design, label provenance, validation strategy, and deployment constraints are evaluated together.

</details>


### [142] [Hapi: A Multivariable Land-Surface Transformer for Medium-Range Hydrological Forecasting at Continental Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22702)
*Hong Zhang,John K. Hutchison,Rao Kotamarthi,Jeremy Feinstein,Haiwen Guan,Romit Maulik,Vijay P. Ramalingam,Jason Stock,Tom Wall*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Hapi is a U-Net Swin Transformer model that produces 24-72 hour flood forecasts across the contiguous US at 0.05° resolution, forecasting discharge, surface runoff, snow water equivalent, and soil wetness. It uses fine 3D patches and hierarchical shifted-window attention, with learned Laplacian task weights. The model outperforms both operational physics-based and state-of-the-art AI models in flood detection, with 0.11s inference time on A100 GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate flood forecasts several days in advance are essential for flood control, water-resource management, and emergency response. Producing high-resolution forecasts over continental domains requires both local hydrological detail and spatial context from river basins to synoptic weather systems.

Method: Hapi uses a U-Net Swin Transformer architecture with fine three-dimensional patches and hierarchical shifted-window attention. It produces 24-72 hour forecasts at 0.05° resolution across the contiguous US. Learned Laplacian task weights adjust each variable's contribution to training. Validation used 2024 test data with reconstructed weather and land-surface inputs from ERA5-Land, 3,881 USGS gauges, and a Hurricane Helene case study.

Result: Hapi outperformed an operational physics-based model and a state-of-the-art AI model in flood detection on 2024 test data. Independent validation against 3,881 USGS gauges and Hurricane Helene case study confirmed its advantage over the physics-based model in reproducing daily discharge. Controlled experiments showed learned task weighting strengthens rare-flood detection, sensitive to precipitation input changes.

Conclusion: Hapi successfully produces four-variable, 72-hour forecasts across the contiguous US with an average inference time of 0.11 seconds on a single A100 GPU, demonstrating superior flood detection performance compared to both physics-based and existing AI approaches.

Abstract: Accurate flood forecasts several days in advance are essential for flood control, water-resource management, and emergency response. Producing them at high resolution over a continental domain calls for local hydrological detail together with spatial context extending from river basins to synoptic weather systems. We developed Hapi, a U-Net Swin Transformer that uses fine three-dimensional patches and hierarchical shifted-window attention to forecast discharge, surface runoff, snow water equivalent, and soil wetness across the contiguous United States. The model produces 24--72-hour forecasts at $0.05^{\circ}$ resolution, with learned Laplacian task weights adjusting each variable's contribution to training. On 2024 test data using reconstructed weather and land-surface inputs from ERA5-Land, Hapi outperformed an operational physics-based model and a state-of-the-art AI model in flood detection. Independent validation against 3,881 U.S. Geological Survey gauges and a Hurricane Helene case study supported its advantage over the physics-based model in reproducing daily discharge. Controlled experiments showed that learned task weighting strengthens rare-flood detection, which is particularly sensitive to changes in precipitation inputs. Hapi produced a four-variable, 72-hour forecast across the contiguous United States with an average inference time of 0.11 seconds on a single A100 GPU.

</details>


### [143] [Trustworthy Agentic AI: Failure Modes, Mitigation Strategies, and a Lifecycle Framework for Autonomous LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22712)
*Fayeq Jeelani Syed,Rehan Ahmad,Ali Al Bataineh,Aakriti Adhikari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This article provides a comprehensive review of security and operational risks in agentic AI systems built on large language models, introducing a unified taxonomy of failure modes and mitigation approaches, and proposing a six-phase Trustworthy Agent Development Lifecycle (TADL) framework for structured development and evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic AI systems offer enhanced utility over static language models through planning, tool use, memory, and multi-agent coordination, but they introduce new security and operational risks that require systematic analysis and mitigation strategies.

Method: The paper conducts a structured review across five interconnected trustworthiness dimensions (safety/robustness, alignment/human oversight, transparency/auditability, privacy/data governance, regulatory compliance), organizing failure modes into a unified taxonomy and examining mitigation approaches with distinction between empirically validated and conceptual techniques.

Result: The paper identifies key failure modes (indirect prompt injection, backdoor triggers, goal misgeneralization, memory contamination, cross-session data leakage) and corresponding mitigation techniques (instruction hierarchies, context isolation, spotlighting, process-based supervision, constrained tool use, privacy-preserving memory), while proposing the TADL framework covering specification, design, training, evaluation, deployment, and monitoring phases.

Conclusion: The TADL framework provides a structured foundation for developing more secure and accountable agentic systems, though it remains to be empirically validated. Significant gaps exist in current benchmarks, and future research priorities include developing better evaluation metrics and mitigation techniques.

Abstract: Agentic AI systems built on large language models can plan over multiple steps, use external tools, retain information in memory, and coordinate with other agents. These capabilities make them more useful than static language models, but they also introduce new security and operational risks. Untrusted content from websites, emails, documents, and databases can enter the same context as system instructions; persistent memory can carry compromised information across sessions; and access to external tools can turn an incorrect model response into a consequential real-world action. This article reviews the trustworthiness of agentic AI across five interconnected dimensions: safety and robustness, alignment and human oversight, transparency and auditability, privacy and data governance, and regulatory compliance. It organizes key failure modes, including indirect prompt injection, backdoor triggers, goal misgeneralization, memory contamination, and cross-session data leakage, into a unified taxonomy. It also examines major mitigation approaches, such as instruction hierarchies, context isolation, spotlighting, process-based supervision, constrained tool use, and privacy-preserving memory, while distinguishing techniques supported by empirical evidence from those that remain largely conceptual. Building on this analysis, we introduce the Trustworthy Agent Development Lifecycle (TADL), a six-phase framework covering specification, design, training, evaluation, deployment, and monitoring. For each phase, TADL identifies relevant trust activities, expected evidence, and risk-based decision gates. Although TADL has not yet been empirically validated, it provides a structured foundation for developing and evaluating more secure and accountable agentic systems. The article concludes by identifying gaps in current benchmarks and outlining priorities for future research.

</details>


### [144] [ProcessLight: Process Supervision for Large Language Model Based Traffic Signal Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22746)
*Huaitao Zhao,Tianlong Zhou,Weijie Wang,Jiasheng Shi,Weixiong Rao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a critical limitation in LLM-based traffic signal control (TSC) methods: they only optimize based on final outcomes without evaluating the quality of individual reasoning steps. The authors propose ProcessLight, a framework that decomposes signal decisions into verifiable semantic steps, and STeP-PO, a reinforcement learning framework that performs step-level credit assignment to enable fine-grained policy optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM TSC methods optimize only from final outcomes and fail to distinguish valid from flawed reasoning steps, causing useful or misleading steps to be jointly updated and thus impairing the model's learning of effective reasoning.

Method: 1) ProcessLight: Decomposes signal decisions into verifiable semantic steps.
2) STeP-PO: Uses step quality scores to evaluate local reasoning quality and step importance to measure each step's influence on the final action, then assigns step-level advantages over a semantic step tree structure. The resulting step-level advantages are propagated to reasoning tokens, enabling fine-grained policy optimization beyond outcome-only rewards.

Result: Extensive experiments over multiple real-world datasets demonstrate the superiority of the proposed methods.

Conclusion: The proposed ProcessLight framework and STeP-PO method successfully enable step-level credit assignment in LLM-based traffic signal control, allowing for fine-grained policy optimization that distinguishes between valid and flawed reasoning steps.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently been introduced into traffic signal control (TSC) as decision agents due to their strengths in human-readable reasoning generation. Yet, existing LLM TSC methods optimize only from final outcomes and fail to distinguish valid from flawed reasoning steps, causing useful or misleading steps to be jointly updated and thus impairing the model's learning of effective reasoning. To bridge this gap, we propose an LLM-based framework ProcessLight to decompose signal decisions into verifiable semantic steps. Building on ProcessLight, we further develop Step-wise Traffic Process Policy Optimization (STeP-PO), a novel reinforcement learning framework that optimizes structured reasoning processes through step-level credit assignment. Specifically, STeP-PO uses step quality scores to evaluate local reasoning quality and step importance to measure each step's influence on the final action, and then assigns step-level advantages over a semantic step tree structure. The resulting step-level advantages are propagated to reasoning tokens, enabling fine-grained policy optimization beyond outcome-only rewards. Extensive experiments over multiple real-world datasets demonstrate the superiority of our methods. Our code is available at https://github.com/wenzhaoabc/processlight.

</details>


### [145] [CTSpinoPelvic1K: spine, pelvis, ribs and femora in one coordinate frame, annotated for lumbosacral transitional anatomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22760)
*Gregory Schwing,Ashley Schehr,Annika Tekumulla,Margret Khoushi,Ryan Christian,Dane Hubers,Faris Mahjoub,Hassan Saad,Mia Sooch,Sathyagopal Siddapureddy,Michael McLellan,Jerick Kim,Miraziz Ismoilov,Nizar Alnabahneh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents CTSpinoPelvic1K, a combined CT dataset of 802 records with vertebrae and pelvis annotations, specifically designed to address the ambiguity in counting lumbosacral transitional vertebrae (LSTV) when C2 is not visible in the imaging field.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The challenge arises when imaging only covers the lumbar region (T12 to S1) without C2 for counting. LSTV anomalies (like lumbarized S1 or sacralized L5) create ambiguity in vertebral identification. Previous datasets (CTSpine1K and CTPelvic1K) were never joined, and neither included all necessary bones. The motivation was to create a comprehensive dataset that resolves vertebral identity using local morphology rather than counting from C2.

Method: The researchers combined CTSpine1K and CTPelvic1K labels on each patient's bone-richest series under a VerSe-native scheme. They added bones neither dataset previously had. The dataset was anchored on the lowest rib-bearing vertebra and S1, with specific classes for L6, T13, separate S1, and lumbar ribs. Validation included: (1) geometric invariants (802/802 passed), (2) rib-vertebra incidence across 5,749 ribs (0.035% offset), and (3) spinopelvic measures matching published values.

Result: The dataset provides 802 CT records with per-level ribs and femora, LSTV-stratified five-fold splits, and is archived at Zenodo (DOI: 10.5281/zenodo.22139642). It includes 377 records in prone position for surgical planning research.

Conclusion: The CTSpinoPelvic1K dataset enables classification of vertebrae from local morphological features without requiring C2 for counting. It supports applications in spinal morphometry, spinopelvic assessment, opportunistic screening, and surgical planning. Limitations include field-of-view constraints for thoracic ground truth, supine posture imaging, absence of a held-out test set, and the use of pseudolabels for ribs with limited Castellvi grade validation.

Abstract: Purpose: A vertebra at the lumbosacral junction is named by counting caudally from C2 on whole-spine imaging, but a lumbar case is planned on lumbar-only imaging (T12 to S1), without C2. Abdominopelvic CT holds that span plus the lowest ribs and pelvis. Where a lumbosacral transitional vertebra (LSTV) alters the count, the local anatomy is ambiguous: four rib-free vertebrae may be an L1 with a lumbar rib or an L5 assimilated to the sacrum, and six may be a sixth lumbar vertebra, a T12 with aplastic ribs, or a lumbarized S1. CTSpinoPelvic1K asks whether local morphology resolves it without the count. CTSpine1K's vertebrae and CTPelvic1K's pelvis covered these patients but were never joined; this release joins them on one series and adds the bones neither had. It provides 802 CT records with per-level ribs and femora, levels anchored on the lowest rib-bearing vertebra and S1, and classes for L6, T13, a separate S1 and lumbar ribs, so anomalies are recorded as such.
  Acquisition and Validation Methods: Records pair CTSpine1K and CTPelvic1K labels on each patient's bone-richest series under a VerSe-native scheme. Validation covered geometric invariants (802/802 pass), rib-vertebra incidence across 5,749 ribs (0.035% offset), and spinopelvic measures matching published values.
  Data Format and Usage Notes: NIfTI image/label pairs with patient-grouped LSTV-stratified five-fold splits and a loader; archived at https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.22139642.
  Potential Applications: Classifying a vertebra from local features; updating cadaveric morphometry; spinopelvic assessment; opportunistic screening; and, absent a public preoperative lumbar cohort, surgical planning research (377 records prone). Limitations: thoracic ground truth is field-of-view limited; postural angles supine; no held-out test set; ribs are triaged-review pseudolabels; Castellvi grades two-reader consensus on 33 records.

</details>


### [146] [DVA-Neurons: Design and Verification of Adaptive LIF Neurons: From Single-Neuron Dynamics to Multi-Neuron Spiking Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22775)
*Thanh Pham,Riadul Islam*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses two key limitations of Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs): (1) the lack of adaptation mechanisms in fixed-parameter Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neurons, and (2) challenges in scaling from single neurons to multi-neuron networks. The work implements adaptive LIF neurons at three architectural scales—a 2nd-order neuron with two-stage synaptic filtering, a 6-neuron fully-connected network, and a verification methodology—all targeting 14nm CMOS technology at 1GHz.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to enable practical deployment of SNNs for ultra-low-power AI inference by addressing biological realism (neuronal adaptation) and network scalability (weight distribution and spike routing). Current fixed-parameter LIF neurons cannot modulate excitability based on firing history, limiting their computational capability.

Method: The methodology involves: (1) designing a 2nd-order adaptive LIF neuron with two-stage synaptic filtering for richer temporal dynamics; (2) creating a 6-neuron fully-connected network with configurable weights (100 to 5); (3) developing a Cocotb-based Python testbench with pulsed current stimuli (amplitude 80, ISI=3) for verification; (4) synthesizing all designs for SAED 14nm CMOS technology at 1GHz.

Result: The 2nd-order neuron achieved 31% spike suppression (25 vs 36 spikes) demonstrating effective adaptation. The 6-neuron network showed 31% reduction in postsynaptic firing (18 vs 26 spikes). Area and power costs: 2nd-order neuron costs 1.77x more area and 1.52x more power than 1st-order baseline; 6-neuron network shows near-linear scaling (5.7x area, 5.3x power). Seven verification bugs were documented spanning testbench connectivity, fixed-point overflow, and Verilog expression-width semantics.

Conclusion: Adaptive LIF neurons effectively modulate firing behavior in both single-neuron and network contexts. The 2nd-order neuron provides richer temporal dynamics at acceptable overhead (1.77x area, 1.52x power). The 6-neuron network demonstrates near-linear scaling, indicating viable path to larger SNN implementations. The documented verification methodology and discovered bugs provide valuable guidance for future SNN hardware development.

Abstract: Spiking Neural Networks (SNNs) offer a promising path toward ultra-low-power artificial intelligence inference by emulating the event-driven computation of biological neurons. However, two challenges limit their practical deployment. First, fixed-parameter Leaky Integrate-and-Fire (LIF) neurons lack the adaptation mechanisms observed in biology, where neurons modulate their excitability based on firing history. Second, scaling from single neurons to multi-neuron networks introduces challenges in synaptic weight distribution and inter-neuron spike routing that are absent in isolated designs. This paper addresses both issues through the extension, verification, and physical implementation of adaptive LIF neurons at three architectural scales. This work contributes: a 2nd-order neuron with two-stage synaptic filtering for richer temporal dynamics; a fully-connected 6-neuron spiking network with configurable weights (100 to 5) demonstrating weight-based inter-neuron communication; and a direct verification methodology enabling per-cycle observation of all internal states. All designs were synthesized targeting Selected Area Electron Diffraction (SAED) 14 nm Complementary Metal-Oxide-Semiconductor (CMOS) technology at 1 GHz and verified with Cocotb-based Python testbenches under pulsed current stimuli (amplitude 80, ISI=3). The results show that adaptation effectively modulates firing: 31% suppression in the 2nd-order neuron (25 vs.\ 36 spikes) and 31% reduction in postsynaptic firing in the network (18 vs.\ 26 spikes). Physically, the 2nd-order neuron costs 1.77x more area and 1.52x more power than the 1st-order baseline, while the 6-neuron network demonstrates near-linear scaling (5.7x area, 5.3x power). Seven verification bugs spanning testbench connectivity, fixed-point overflow, and Verilog expression-width semantics are documented.

</details>


### [147] [From Research Frontier to Laboratory Bench: Design of a Four-Tier Experimental Teaching System for Multimodal Medical Image Intelligent Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22790)
*Dongjing Shan,Yamei Luo,Jin Li,Yong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This design paper presents an advanced experimental teaching system for undergraduate intelligent medical engineering programs, translating ongoing multimodal deep learning research on endometrial carcinoma into a structured lab sequence. The system addresses three educational gaps (modality, authenticity, deployment) through four pedagogical principles and a tiered curriculum with 32 laboratory units delivered via a custom virtual clinical workstation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Laboratory curricula in intelligent medical engineering lag behind the multimodal, long-tailed, and distributionally shifting realities of clinical AI. There is a need to bridge this gap by creating authentic, research-based educational experiences that prepare undergraduates for real-world clinical AI deployment.

Method: The authors identified three educational gaps (modality, authenticity, and deployment) and derived four pedagogical principles from constructive alignment, experiential learning, the research teaching nexus, and the CDIO framework. The curriculum comprises four progressive tiers plus an engineering layer, with 32 laboratory units over 64 contact hours, delivered via a custom virtual clinical workstation using de-identified multi-institutional data. Each tier maps to a specific technical bottleneck, prerequisite coursework, and criterion-referenced deliverables. Data governance, safety, and assessment protocols are specified.

Result: The paper presents a complete curriculum design with detailed specifications including learning outcomes, implementation plans, and assessment protocols. Learning outcome data will be collected across two implementation cycles.

Conclusion: This work provides a comprehensive framework for bridging the gap between clinical AI research and undergraduate engineering education, offering a scalable model for integrating authentic multimodal AI training into engineering curricula.

Abstract: Undergraduate programmes in intelligent medical engineering are expanding, yet laboratory curricula lag behind the multimodal, long-tailed, and distributionally shifting realities of clinical AI. This design paper presents an advanced experimental teaching system that translates an ongoing multimodal deep learning research project on endometrial carcinoma into a structured undergraduate lab sequence. We identify three educational gaps (modality, authenticity, and deployment) and derive four pedagogical principles from constructive alignment, experiential learning, the research teaching nexus, and the CDIO framework. The curriculum comprises four progressive tiers plus an engineering layer, with 32 laboratory units over 64 contact hours, delivered via a custom virtual clinical workstation using de-identified multi-institutional data. Each tier maps to a specific technical bottleneck, prerequisite coursework, and criterion-referenced deliverables. Data governance, safety, and assessment protocols are specified. Learning outcome data will be collected across two implementation cycles.

</details>


### [148] [ISA-Bench: A Benchmark for Computational Reasoning Across Instruction Set Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22878)
*Aditya Pola,Arkaprava Majumdar,Vineeth N. Balasubramanian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ISA-Bench is a benchmark for evaluating language models on programming games with constrained instruction sets, designed to test reasoning in unfamiliar computational models. It provides a full execution stack (parser, VM, verifier) for automated evaluation. The study finds reasoning models outperform code-specialized and general-purpose models, but unfamiliar syntax remains a major failure source. Iterative feedback improves task completion rates, though gains vary across architectures. A new "reasoning-execution gap" (REG) analysis reveals a disconnect between identifying computational strategies and implementing them correctly in target ISAs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current large language model code generation benchmarks primarily evaluate well-resourced languages like Python and Java, where models benefit from abundant training data. These benchmarks provide limited evidence about reasoning in unfamiliar computational models, such as deriving arithmetic from a single subtract instruction, coordinating parallel programs across communicating nodes, or wiring logic gates into circuits. The authors seek to address this gap by creating a benchmark that tests reasoning in constrained, unfamiliar programming environments.

Method: The authors present ISA-Bench, a benchmark of programming games with constrained instruction sets. For each game, they provide a full execution stack including parser, VM, and verifier, enabling automated evaluation with structured feedback for iterative refinement. They compare reasoning models, code-specialized models, and general-purpose models across these tasks. They also introduce a reasoning-execution gap (REG) analysis to examine the disconnect between identifying a plausible computational strategy and expressing it as a correct program in the target ISA.

Result: Reasoning models achieve higher average solve rates than code-specialized and general-purpose models. However, unfamiliar syntax remains a major source of failure. Models solve more tasks with iterative feedback, though the gains vary substantially across architectures. The REG analysis reveals a recurring disconnect between identifying a plausible computational strategy and expressing it as a correct program in the target instruction set architecture.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that while reasoning models perform better on unfamiliar computational tasks, there exists a significant reasoning-execution gap that needs to be addressed. The code is open-sourced, and the benchmark provides a valuable tool for evaluating language models' reasoning capabilities in constrained, unfamiliar programming environments.

Abstract: Large language model code generation benchmarks primarily evaluate well-resourced languages like Python and Java, where models benefit from abundant training data. They provide limited evidence about reasoning in unfamiliar computational models: deriving arithmetic from a single subtract instruction, coordinating parallel programs across communicating nodes, or wiring logic gates into circuits. We present ISA-Bench, a benchmark of programming games with constrained instruction sets. For each game we provide a full execution stack (parser, VM, and verifier), enabling automated evaluation with structured feedback for iterative refinement. Reasoning models achieve higher average solve rates than code-specialized and general-purpose models, but unfamiliar syntax remains a major source of failure. Models solve more tasks with iterative feedback, though the gains vary substantially across architectures. We introduce a reasoning--execution gap (REG) analysis that reveals a recurring disconnect between identifying a plausible computational strategy and expressing it as a correct program in the target ISA. Code is open-sourced.

</details>


### [149] [When Should a VLM Look? Paying Only for Visual Calls That Were Needed and Used](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22910)
*Kunyu Peng,Junming Liu,Ruiqi He,Qingzhuo Wang,Jianzhong Qi,Xianhui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CounterCredit is a training method for vision-language agents that addresses the problem of spurious visual tool calls (cropping/zooming). It credits only calls that are both needed and actually used by the model, using dual-channel GRPO with decision and evidence values. This method reduces spurious calls from 36-87% to 31-36% while improving performance by 6.3-9.4 points on V*, HR-Bench-4K, and HR-Bench-8K benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language agents are trained with outcome rewards that credit successful tool calls, but a successful call doesn't demonstrate the model actually needed or used the visual information. At cold-start, only 10-12% of visual calls were both needed and used, and released agents make spurious calls 36-87% of the time. About two-thirds of outcome reward goes to calls that were neither needed nor used.

Method: CounterCredit asks both questions of every image-returning call at its realized pre-call state using the policy's own gold-answer score. It computes: (1) Decision value - compares the realized visual branch with answering immediately; (2) Evidence value - compares the returned crop with random same-size patches substituted into the same call. Verified calls earn cashback while every other executed call pays rent. The price is bounded so every correct trajectory outranks every wrong one, with a dual-channel GRPO advantage keeping the price in its own units.

Result: From the same cold start, prompt pool, and budget, CounterCredit achieves 89.5% on V*, 80.2% on HR-Bench-4K, and 76.4% on HR-Bench-8K, beating outcome-only GRPO by 6.3 to 9.4 points with 1.78 vs 1.84 calls per question. Spurious-call rate is reduced to 31-36%, the lowest among evaluated agents. The same method lifts Qwen3-VL-8B base from 75.4 to 80.8 on average.

Conclusion: CounterCredit successfully addresses the spurious visual call problem by crediting only necessary and used calls. This leads to significant performance improvements (6.3-9.4 points) while reducing unnecessary tool usage by nearly half. The method demonstrates that training with more precise credit assignment for visual tool calls can substantially improve both accuracy and efficiency of vision-language agents.

Abstract: Vision-language agents that crop and zoom are trained with rewards that credit a successful tool call, yet a successful call does not show that the model needed to look or used the pixels it received. On our cold-start checkpoint only 10% to 12% of visual calls were both needed and used, and released agents make spurious calls 36% to 87% of the time on individual benchmarks. Outcome rewards, judge rewards, and branch probes each observe one side of this failure, and about two thirds of what an outcome reward pays goes to calls that were neither needed nor used. CounterCredit asks both questions of every image-returning call at its realized pre-call state, using the policy's own gold-answer score. A decision value compares the realized visual branch with answering immediately; an evidence value compares the returned crop with random same-size patches substituted into the same call. A call verified on both earns cashback and every other executed call pays rent; the price is bounded so that every correct trajectory outranks every wrong one, and a dual-channel GRPO advantage keeps the price in its own units. From the same cold start, prompt pool, and budget, CounterCredit reaches 89.5% on V*, 80.2% on HR-Bench-4K, and 76.4% on HR-Bench-8K, 6.3 to 9.4 points above outcome-only GRPO at 1.78 against 1.84 calls per question, and lowers the spurious-call rate to 31% to 36%, the lowest among the agents evaluated. The same recipe lifts a Qwen3-VL-8B base from 75.4 to 80.8 on average.

</details>


### [150] [Beyond Linear Context: Graph-Guided Evidence Navigation for Long-Novel Reasoning with a Local 9B Language Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22939)
*Wenji Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper tests whether knowledge graphs can help small language models answer questions about detective novels by organizing narrative information temporally and relationally, rather than processing text sequentially. Using 30 novels and 234 questions, the best graph approach achieved 53.85% accuracy vs 46.15% for recent-window baseline, though no comparison survived statistical correction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current long-context models process text sequentially like a printout, but human detectives organize information temporally and maintain relationship maps. This work investigates whether frozen knowledge graphs can give small models similar capabilities to connect clues from chapter one with questions at the book's end.

Method: Tested qwen3.5:9b on 30 detective novels with 234 multiple-choice questions under 9 conditions: 5 graph routes, recent-window baseline, whole-book compression, vector retrieval, and question-only control. Evaluated knowledge graph structures with varying annotation coverage (16% vs 73%).

Result: Strongest graph route achieved 53.85% (126/234) vs 46.15% (recent window), 51.28% (compression), 51.71% (vector retrieval), and 40.17% (question-only). On subset requiring book knowledge: 42.86%. None of 15 graph-baseline contrasts survived Holm correction. Two structural findings: annotated evidence concentrates in topological core (2.35x enrichment), and annotation coverage varies dramatically between pipelines (16% vs 73%).

Conclusion: The study provides exploratory evidence for knowledge graph-enhanced reading, though statistical significance is lacking. The key insight is that evidence concentrates in graph cores and annotation coverage is the critical bottleneck—pooled accuracy alone obscures which pipeline limitation is being measured. Future work should focus on improving graph construction and annotation quality.

Abstract: Long-context models read a novel the way a person reads a printout: one token after another, in narrative order, with the whole history competing for a fixed budget of attention. A detective does not work that way. They sort what happened when, and they keep a map of who relates to whom, so a clue from chapter one can meet a question asked at the end of the book. We test whether a frozen knowledge graph can give a small local model that same freedom. Thirty detective novels and 234 multiple-choice questions are answered by one fixed qwen3.5:9b reader under nine conditions: five graph routes, a recent-window baseline, whole-book compression, ordinary vector retrieval, and a question-only control. The strongest graph route reaches 53.85% (126/234) against 46.15% for the recent window, 51.28% for compression, 51.71% for vector retrieval and 40.17% for question-only. On the subset that no model can answer without the book, the graph route reaches 42.86%. None of the fifteen graph-baseline contrasts survives Holm correction, so we present the result as exploratory evidence about a design. Two structural findings survive scrutiny better than the headline number: annotated evidence concentrates in the topological core of these graphs (2.35x enrichment, pooled), and the two graph-building pipelines differ so much in annotation coverage (16% versus 73% of clue paragraphs) that pooled accuracy alone would hide which bottleneck is being measured.

</details>


<div id='cs.MM'></div>

# cs.MM [[Back]](#toc)

### [151] [Causal Localization of the Refusal Direction in Audio Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22260)
*Leonardo Haw-Yang Foo,Hung-yi Lee*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether refusal behavior in Large Audio Language Models (LALMs) originates from the speech front end or is inherited from the safety-aligned text language model. Using causal interventions (direction ablation at audio-to-LM interface and LM residual layers), researchers found that the largest effects on refusal margins occur in mid-to-late LM layers, while interface ablations have minimal impact. The study concludes that refusal is inherited from the text LM rather than computed in the audio pathway, though the audio pathway remains active.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the mechanism of refusal behavior in Large Audio Language Models - specifically whether safety alignment is carried by the speech front end or inherited from the pre-trained text language model when the model processes harmful spoken requests.

Method: Causal intervention experiments: (1) Fitting directions that separate harmful from benign prompts at each model's audio-to-LM interface and at tested LM residual layers, (2) Ablating these direction components, (3) Measuring resulting changes in first-token refusal margin. Experiments conducted across five LALMs spanning three backbone families, with four models evaluated under held-out category shift and three models passing a baseline safety gate.

Result: The largest effects occur in mid-to-late LM band, while ablations of tested interface directions have little effect. On Qwen2.5-Omni, ablating L16 direction changes margin by -7.10 versus -0.013 at the projector. Zeroing encoder output changes margin by -4.7 (audio pathway still in use). The contrast is linearly decodable at early layers where single-layer ablation has little effect, and a direction fitted on LM backbone alone transfers to the full audio model.

Conclusion: Refusal behavior is inherited from the text LM rather than computed in the audio pathway. The direction is interpreted as refusal-linked rather than harmfulness-specific due to differences in form between harmful and benign prompts. Safety audits should use interventions rather than relying on probes alone, and should examine the inherited text LM alongside the audio interface.

Abstract: A large audio language model (LALM) attaches a speech front end to a text language model (LM) that is already safety-aligned. When such a model refuses a harmful spoken request, is the refusal carried by the front end, or inherited from the text LM? We test this with causal interventions. At each model's audio-to-LM interface and at tested LM residual layers, we fit a direction separating harmful from benign prompts, ablate its component, and measure the resulting change in the model's first-token refusal margin. Four of the five models are evaluated under held-out category shift. Across five LALMs spanning three backbone families, with three models passing a baseline safety gate, the largest tested effects occur in a mid-to-late LM band, while ablations of the tested interface directions have little effect. On Qwen2.5-Omni, ablating the L16 direction changes the margin by -7.10, versus -0.013 at the projector. The audio pathway is still in use: zeroing the encoder output changes the margin by -4.7. In the same model, the contrast is linearly decodable at an early layer where single-layer ablation has little effect, and a direction fitted on the LM backbone alone transfers to the full audio model. Because harmful and benign prompts also differ in form, we interpret the direction as refusal-linked rather than harmfulness-specific. These interventions localize dependence of the refusal margin, not where refusal is computed. Moving the margin also does not always change what the model writes. Safety audits of these models should use interventions rather than rely on probes alone, and should examine the inherited text LM alongside the audio interface.

</details>


### [152] [Hi-Singers: A Comprehensive High-Quality Dataset for Expressive Audio-Driven Singing Head Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22264)
*Yichi Zhang,Hui Zhang,Guanjun Liu,Yuefeng Zou,Fengzhao Sun,Jun Yu*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: Hi-Singers is the first large-scale, high-quality dataset specifically designed for singing head synthesis, addressing the domain gap between speech-driven and singing-driven digital human generation. The dataset contains 29,608 video segments (~170 hours) with rigorous filtering, and enables models to achieve better visual realism, lip-sync consistency, and rhythmic dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current audio-driven digital human models are trained on speech data and struggle with singing synthesis due to domain gap - singing requires more exaggerated expressions, vivid jaw openings, and precise rhythmic synchronization. Existing models suffer from "rhythmic drift" and constrained dynamics when applied to singing.

Method: 1) Built Hi-Singers dataset through rigorous automated and manual filtering pipeline ensuring thematic adherence, high-resolution, and stable motion; 2) Established evaluation benchmark balanced across linguistic and musical styles; 3) Conducted extensive experiments across diverse architectures including 3D-coefficient and diffusion-based models.

Result: Hi-Singers consistently and significantly improves performance across all dimensions compared to speech-trained models. Models achieve superior visual realism, enhanced lip-sync consistency, and more precise rhythmic dynamics, effectively bridging the domain gap.

Conclusion: Hi-Singers sets a new performance standard for singing synthesis task and effectively bridges the domain gap between speech and singing domains. The dataset is publicly available.

Abstract: State-of-the-art models for audio-driven digital human generation have achieved photo-realistic results in talking-head synthesis. However, extending these models to singing-head synthesis remains challenging due to a significant Domain Gap: singing requires more exaggerated expressions, vivid jaw openings, and precise rhythmic synchronization. Current models, primarily trained on speech datasets, often struggle with "rhythmic drift" and constrained dynamics. To address this, we introduce Hi-Singers, the first large-scale, high-quality, in-the-wild video dataset specifically tailored for singing head synthesis. Hi-Singers undergoes a rigorous automated and manual filtering pipeline, ensuring strict thematic adherence, high-resolution rendering, and stable motion, resulting in 29,608 video segments totaling approximately 170 hours. We further establish a dedicated evaluation benchmark balanced across linguistic and musical styles. Extensive experiments across diverse architectures, including 3D-coefficient and diffusion-based models, demonstrate that Hi-Singers consistently and significantly improves performance across all dimensions. Specifically, it enables models to achieve superior visual realism, enhanced lip-sync consistency, and more precise rhythmic dynamics, effectively bridging the domain gap and setting a new performance standard for the singing synthesis task. The dataset is available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/CharlesZhang-USTC/Hi-Singers

</details>


### [153] [Visual Graph Reasoning via Knowledge Compilation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.22327)
*Rongzheng Wang,Zhe Wang,Ke Qin,Rongwei Wang,Muquan Li,Yizhuo Ma,Yihong Huang,Jielei Wang,Shuang Liang*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: VGCompiler is a compilation-centric paradigm for visual graph reasoning that uses two compilers - a representation compiler to recover graph structure from images, and an operation compiler to compile queries into executable operations. Built on Qwen3-VL-8B with RL training, it achieves 28.9% improvement over best closed-source VLM and 23.7% over best code-based baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current VLMs fail at visual graph reasoning because they prioritize final-answer supervision over intermediate graph representation recovery. Existing approaches cannot preserve graph topology and state from visual input, revealing a fundamental limitation in handling graph-theoretic questions from images.

Method: VGCompiler uses a two-compiler architecture: (1) Representation compiler recovers structure-preserving intermediate graph from visual input; (2) Operation compiler compiles query intent into executable graph operations. Training uses RL with layered rewards over executability, compiled graph validity, representation quality, and operation quality. A frozen observer summarizes graph/question conditions into signatures for archive retrieval and code reuse.

Result: Qwen-VGCompiler (8B backbone) surpasses strongest closed-source VLM baseline by 28.9% and strongest code-based baseline by 23.7% on GVLQA, VisionGraph, and VGCURE benchmarks. It also generalizes well to three real-world domains: metro routing, logistics delivery, and network fault assessment.

Conclusion: The compilation-centric approach effectively addresses visual graph reasoning by explicitly recovering graph representation before reasoning. This preserves graph topology and state from visual input, enabling significant performance gains across diverse benchmarks and real-world applications with heterogeneous visual graphs.

Abstract: Visual graph reasoning requires answering graph-theoretic questions directly from graph images, where graph topology and state are conveyed visually rather than given in symbolic form. Despite recent progress of vision-language models (VLMs), current approaches to visual graph reasoning still fail on simple visual graph problems. This reveals a fundamental limitation of existing approaches: they prioritize final-answer supervision over the intermediate recovery of an explicit graph representation that preserves graph topology and state from visual input. To address this limitation, we propose VGCompiler, a compilation-centric paradigm for visual graph reasoning via knowledge compilation. VGCompiler organizes reasoning around two compilers: a representation compiler that recovers a structure-preserving intermediate graph representation from visual input, and an operation compiler that compiles query intent under the recovered graph state into an executable graph operation. Specifically, we build VGCompiler on Qwen3-VL-8B and train it with reinforcement learning guided by a layered reward over executability, compiled graph validity, representation quality, and operation quality. VGCompiler uses a frozen observer to summarize graph and question conditions into lightweight signatures, enabling archive retrieval and code reuse across similar regimes. Experiments on three benchmarks GVLQA, VisionGraph, and VGCURE, show that Qwen-VGCompiler, built on an 8B backbone, surpasses the strongest closed-source VLM baseline by 28.9% and the strongest code-based baseline by 23.7%, while maintaining high efficiency. We further evaluate VGCompiler on three real-world domains, including metro routing, logistics delivery, and network fault assessment, where it generalizes across heterogeneous visual graphs and domain-grounded tasks.

</details>


### [154] [If You Hear It, Help Find It: Orthogonal Knowledge Distillation for Open-Vocabulary Audio-Visual Event Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23376)
*Yi Xu,Cheng Chen,Wenzhuo Lei*

Main category: cs.MM

TL;DR: This paper addresses open-vocabulary audio-visual event localization (OV-AVEL) by proposing OV-OrthKD, a reliability-aware asymmetric distillation framework that treats visual and audio teacher supervision differently based on their temporal-boundary reliability. The key insight is that not all teacher signals should shape the localization decision equally - supervision placement is a task-specific design choice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors observe that in OV-AVEBench, the visual teacher provides more reliable boundary cues than the audio teacher, though the audio teacher remains semantically informative. This asymmetry in reliability is setting-specific rather than a universal ranking of vision vs audio. The challenge becomes: which teacher signals should shape the localization decision, and which should remain auxiliary?

Method: The proposed OV-OrthKD framework consists of four components: (1) Visual feature transfer shapes a decision-aligned representation; (2) Audio feature transfer enriches a complementary auxiliary subspace; (3) A text prototype anchors seen/unseen category semantics; (4) An orthogonality loss limits directional overlap between the two teacher-specific projections. At inference, the student uses both modalities through query-aware fusion, while the training recipe keeps audio-teacher supervision off the segment-logit path.

Result: On OV-AVEBench, OV-OrthKD achieves 0.816 segment AP and improves F1@0.5 over the official fine-tuning baseline by 2.7 points overall and 3.4 points on unseen categories. Additional analyses (path-assignment, role-swap, corruption, and transfer) consistently support supervision placement as a task-specific design axis.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates that supervision placement - deciding which modality's signals should directly shape the localization decision versus serving as auxiliary information - is a crucial and previously overlooked design axis for OV-AVEL tasks. The proposed OV-OrthKD framework effectively leverages this concept, achieving state-of-the-art performance, particularly on unseen categories.

Abstract: Open-vocabulary audio-visual event localization (OV-AVEL) grounds a text-queried event in time from video, audio, and language. The supervision sources available to this task can differ in temporal-boundary reliability: on OV-AVEBench, our configured visual teacher gives more reliable boundary cues than the configured audio teacher, although the latter is a strong pretrained audio model and remains semantically informative. This is a setting-specific diagnostic rather than a universal ranking of vision and audio. We formulate the resulting challenge as supervision placement: which teacher signals may shape the localization decision, and which should remain auxiliary. Based on this view, we propose OV-OrthKD, a reliability-aware asymmetric distillation framework. Visual feature transfer shapes a decision-aligned representation, audio feature transfer enriches a complementary auxiliary subspace, a text prototype anchors seen/unseen category semantics, and an orthogonality loss limits directional overlap between the two teacher-specific projections. The student continues to use both modalities through query-aware fusion at inference, while the default training recipe keeps audio-teacher supervision off the segment-logit path. On OV-AVEBench, OV-OrthKD achieves 0.816 segment AP and improves F1@0.5 over the official fine-tuning baseline by 2.7 points overall and 3.4 points on unseen categories. Path-assignment, role-swap, corruption, and transfer analyses consistently support supervision placement as a task-specific design axis for OV-AVEL.

</details>
